Baron and Kenny (1986) gave the classic four-step procedure for establishing mediation, using paths conventionally labelled c, a, b and c-prime:

  • Step 1: X significantly predicts Y (path c, the total effect, is significant: there is an effect to be explained).
  • Step 2: X significantly predicts the mediator M (path a: X actually moves the proposed mechanism).
  • Step 3: M significantly predicts Y while controlling for X (path b: the mechanism affects the outcome over and above X).
  • Step 4: when M is entered into the model, the X-to-Y effect shrinks. If it drops all the way to non-significance, that is full mediation (M accounts for the entire effect); if it weakens but remains significant (the reduced path c-prime), that is partial mediation (M accounts for some of the effect, with a direct route remaining).

The indirect effect, the amount of X's influence on Y that travels through M, is the product of the two constituent paths, a × b. The total effect decomposes as c = c-prime + (a × b): direct path plus indirect path. The Sobel test evaluates whether that indirect effect a × b is statistically significant by dividing it by its pooled standard error (a formula combining the standard errors of a and b) to yield a z statistic; a z beyond plus or minus 1.96 means significant mediation at the 5% level. Worked sketch: if a = 0.50 and b = 0.40, the indirect effect is 0.50 × 0.40 = 0.20, and the Sobel test asks whether 0.20 is large relative to its standard error. Because the sampling distribution of the product a × b is skewed (non-normal) in small samples, modern practice prefers bootstrapped confidence intervals for the indirect effect (the Preacher and Hayes approach: if the bootstrap CI excludes zero, mediation is supported) over the assumption-laden Sobel test, but you must still know the Sobel logic, because it is what the exam names.

Moderation: interaction terms

A moderator is tested in an entirely different way: by entering the product interaction term X × Z into a regression alongside the main effects of X and of Z. The regression is Y = b0 + b1X + b2Z + b3(X × Z). If the coefficient on that product term, b3, is statistically significant, then Z moderates the X-to-Y relationship: the slope of Y on X is no longer constant but changes across levels of Z (the X effect is steeper at high Z than at low Z, say). To reduce the multicollinearity that arises because X and Z each correlate strongly with their own product, the predictors are usually mean-centred (each value minus its mean) before the product is computed, which also makes the lower-order coefficients interpretable at the average level of the other variable. When the moderator is categorical (sex, treatment group), the equivalent strategy is multi-group analysis: fit the model separately in each group, or use multi-group structural equation modelling, and test whether the X-to-Y path differs across groups.

High-yield

The two are tested by two different statistics, and the paper rewards knowing which goes with which: mediation is established through the indirect effect a × b (Baron and Kenny's steps, the Sobel test, or a bootstrap CI); moderation is established through the product interaction term X × Z in a regression. A mediator reduces the X-to-Y effect when added (full = to non-significance, partial = it merely weakens); a moderator produces a significant interaction.

Memory aid

Mediator = Mechanism, the Middle man (the how/why), sitting ON the chain X to M to Y. Moderator = Modifies the strength of the arrow (the when/for whom), sitting OFF to the side. Mediation uses a × b and the Sobel test; moderation uses the X × Z interaction term. Two M-words, two questions, two tests.

Exam trap

Do not say a moderator "explains why" X affects Y: that is a mediator's job (mechanism). A moderator never explains the effect; it only specifies the conditions under which the effect is stronger, weaker, or reversed. Conversely, do not test a mediator with an interaction term or a moderator with the Sobel test: matching the wrong analysis to the concept is the favourite trap here. And remember that a single variable can serve as both in a "moderated mediation" model, so the labels describe a variable's role in a given path, not a fixed property of the variable.

H. Design typologies: factorial, quasi-experimental, single-subject

Once you can control extraneous variables (Section F) and model causal pathways (Section G), the next question is how to arrange conditions into a design. The examiner's standard move here is a one-line scenario ("a researcher compares two intact classrooms before and after a new curriculum") and four design names as options; you win the mark by matching the structural signature of the scenario to the correct design. This subsection teaches the three families that dominate the paper: fully randomised factorial designs, quasi-experiments (treatment without randomisation), and single-subject designs (one participant as their own control).

True factorial designs and higher-order interactions

A factorial design manipulates two or more independent variables (called factors) simultaneously, crossing every level of each factor with every level of the others, so it yields both main effects (the separate effect of each factor averaged over the others) and interaction effects (the effect of one factor depends on the level of another). The naming convention reads off the design directly: a 2 × 3 design crosses one two-level factor with one three-level factor, producing 2 × 3 = six cells (treatment combinations); a 2 × 2 × 4 design has three factors and 16 cells. The number of numbers tells you how many factors there are; the numbers themselves tell you the levels.

Definition

A main effect is the effect of one factor on the dependent variable, averaged across all levels of the other factor(s). An interaction effect exists when the effect of one factor is not constant but changes across the levels of another factor. A 2 × 3 design tests two main effects (one per factor) and one two-way interaction.

The great efficiency of the factorial design is precisely its ability to detect interactions, which a series of separate one-variable studies could never reveal. Worked example: suppose factor A is a drug (placebo vs active) and factor B is therapy (none vs CBT). If the drug lowers depression by 5 points with no therapy but by 12 points when combined with CBT, the effect of the drug depends on the level of therapy: that is an A × B interaction, and no two single-factor experiments run in isolation could have found it. A higher-order interaction (a three-way A × B × C interaction) means a two-way interaction itself changes across the levels of a third factor; these are real but hard to interpret and demand large samples for adequate power, because the variance is sliced into ever smaller cells.

High-yield

The unique payoff of a factorial design is the interaction effect. When an interaction is significant, you must interpret it before the main effects, because a main effect can be misleading in the presence of an interaction (a "qualified" main effect). On an interaction graph, non-parallel lines = interaction present; parallel lines = no interaction; crossing (disordinal) lines signal the strongest interactions, where the direction of an effect reverses across levels of the other factor.

Quasi-experimental designs

A quasi-experiment tests a treatment but lacks random assignment to conditions, often because randomising is unethical or impossible (you cannot randomly assign children to two existing schools, or randomly allocate a flood). This missing ingredient is decisive: without randomisation the groups may differ systematically before the treatment, so the design is exposed to selection-based confounds and its internal validity is weaker than a true experiment's. The three named designs each manage that weakness differently:

  • Nonequivalent control group design: a treatment group and an intact (non-randomised) comparison group are both pre-tested and post-tested. The pre-test is the saving feature: it lets you check how comparable the groups were to start with. The signature threat is selection and the selection-by-maturation interaction, because the groups may have differed at baseline and may also change at different rates.
  • Interrupted time-series design: a single group is measured repeatedly at many points before and after the intervention (the "interruption"). A genuine treatment effect shows as a change in the level (a jump) or the slope (a change in trend) of the series at exactly the intervention point, against the backdrop of the pre-existing trend. The chief threat is history: some other event coinciding with the intervention could produce the same discontinuity.
  • Regression-discontinuity design: participants are assigned to treatment by a strict cut-off on a continuous assignment variable (for example, everyone scoring below 40 on a screening test receives remedial tutoring; everyone at or above 40 does not). A treatment effect appears as a discontinuity (a vertical jump) in the regression line of outcome on the assignment variable at the cut-off. Because assignment is governed by a fully known rule, the design has stronger causal warrant than other quasi-designs and, in the narrow band just either side of the cut-off, approaches the inferential strength of a true randomised experiment.

How it is tested: the paper typically describes the assignment mechanism and asks you to name the design. The diagnostic cue is the assignment rule, not the outcome. A cut-off score that decides who gets treated is always regression-discontinuity; many measurements across time on one group is interrupted time-series; two intact pre-tested groups is the nonequivalent control group design.

Single-subject (single-case) designs

Single-subject designs study one participant (or a few) intensively across time, using each participant as their own control by comparing behaviour across phases rather than across groups. They are central to applied behaviour analysis and to clinical case research, where group designs are often impractical. The logic is that a behaviour that changes only when (and each time) the treatment is applied is hard to explain by anything other than the treatment.

  • AB design: a baseline phase (A) followed by a treatment phase (B). It is the simplest but weakest design, because any change at B could be coincidental (a history or maturation event happening to fall at the same time), so it cannot establish a causal effect on its own.
  • ABA (reversal/withdrawal): baseline, treatment, then withdrawal of treatment back to baseline. If behaviour improves under B and reverts when the treatment is removed, the treatment is implicated, because the behaviour tracks the treatment's presence and absence. Its ethical problem is that it ends on baseline (the participant is left without the helpful treatment).
  • ABAB: adds a second treatment phase, so it both demonstrates the effect twice (much stronger causal evidence, two replications within one case) and ends on treatment (ethically preferable). This is the workhorse reversal design.
  • Multiple-baseline design: used when reversal is impossible or unethical. Some effects cannot be reversed (you cannot un-teach a learned reading skill), and withdrawing a helpful treatment is often undesirable. Here treatment is introduced in a staggered sequence across several baselines: across subjects, across behaviours, or across settings. Each baseline should change only when its own treatment begins, and the staggering means a single external (history) event cannot explain why each baseline shifted at its own different time.
Exam trap

The reason to choose a multiple-baseline over an ABAB is precisely to demonstrate experimental control without therapeutic withdrawal: it controls the history threat by staggering the introduction of treatment, not by reversing it, so you never take away a working treatment and you never rely on the behaviour being reversible. ABAB requires the behaviour to be reversible; multiple-baseline does not. A scenario that says "the treatment teaches an irreversible skill" is steering you to multiple-baseline.

I. Taxonomy of validity threats

Campbell and Stanley's classic taxonomy of threats divides into internal validity (did the treatment truly cause the observed effect, free of confounds?) and external validity (does the effect generalise beyond this particular study, sample, and setting?). This is the single most scenario-tested topic in the methodology syllabus: the examiner gives a vignette and asks which specific threat it illustrates, so you must learn each threat by its signature, the structural feature that distinguishes it from its near neighbours. Internal-validity threats are about confounds inside the study; external-validity threats are about whether the finding travels outside it.

Internal validity threats

ThreatSignature
HistoryAn external event (not the treatment) occurs between pre- and post-test and affects the outcome. The cue is "something happened in the world" during the study.
MaturationInternal changes within participants over time (growing older, more tired, more practised, spontaneously recovering) cause the change, not the treatment. (Localised maturation = a specific developmental trajectory present in one group only.)
TestingTaking the pre-test itself changes performance on the post-test (practice on the items, sensitisation to the content). The cue is that the act of measuring caused the change.
InstrumentationThe measuring instrument changes over time: a tool drifts out of calibration, raters fatigue or grow lenient ("instrumentation decay"), or the scoring standard shifts. The participants are stable; the ruler moved.
Statistical regression (regression to the mean)Groups selected for extreme scores drift back toward the mean on retesting purely as a statistical artefact, mimicking a treatment effect. The cue is selection on an extreme score.
SelectionPre-existing differences between groups (arising from non-random assignment) rather than the treatment cause the difference. The cue is intact, self-selected, or non-randomised groups.
Selection-maturation interactionThe non-equivalent groups also mature at different rates, a combined confound common in quasi-experiments.
Attrition (experimental mortality)Participants drop out non-randomly, so the groups that finish differ from the groups that started. Differential attrition (different dropout rates or reasons across groups) is the dangerous form.
Compensatory equalisationAdministrators or staff, feeling the control group is being deprived, give it extra resources, shrinking the real treatment difference.

Worked discrimination: imagine a remedial programme is offered to the lowest-scoring 10% of a class, and on retest their scores rise. The naive reading is "the programme worked", but two threats compete. Statistical regression predicts that an extreme-low group will rise toward the mean on any retest with no treatment at all, and selection notes the group was chosen precisely for being extreme. A defensible design needs an equally extreme untreated comparison group to separate the real treatment effect from regression to the mean. Recognising that this specific scenario (selection on extremes plus retest gains) screams regression to the mean is a recurrent paper item.

External validity threats

  • Selection × treatment interaction: the treatment works only for the particular (often unrepresentative) sample studied, so it does not generalise to other populations (a therapy validated only on university volunteers may not transfer to an elderly clinical sample).
  • Setting × treatment interaction: the effect holds only in the specific setting (a tightly controlled lab, one specialist clinic) and fails to transfer to ordinary settings (ecological generalisation fails).
  • Reactive arrangements / Hawthorne effect: participants change their behaviour simply because they know they are being studied, not because of the treatment. (Named for the Hawthorne Works productivity studies, where output rose under almost any change in conditions, including reverting to the original conditions.)
  • Demand characteristics: participants pick up cues about the hypothesis and consciously or unconsciously behave to confirm (or deliberately sabotage) it.
  • Experimenter expectancy / Rosenthal effect: the experimenter's expectations unintentionally bias how they treat participants or record data, producing a self-fulfilling result. The double-blind procedure (neither participant nor experimenter knows the condition) is the standard control.
Memory aid

Internal-validity threats are about confounds inside the study (was it really the treatment?); external-validity threats are about generalising outside it (does it travel?). For the reactive trio: Hawthorne = being watched changes you; demand characteristics = the participant guesses the hypothesis; Rosenthal/expectancy = the experimenter's bias leaks out. All three are fixed by blinding.

Exam trap

Do not confuse testing (the pre-test changes the participant, an internal-validity confound) with the selection × treatment external-validity threat (the pre-test sensitises participants so the result only generalises to pre-tested populations, sometimes called "reactive testing"). Likewise, maturation is change inside the person, whereas history is change in the world; both occur over the same passage of time, and the only discriminator is whether the cause was internal or external.

J. Inferential statistics: the hypothesis-gating parameters

Before any specific test, inferential statistics rests on a small set of "gating" parameters that decide what counts as evidence: the theorem that licenses the use of normal-based tests, the two kinds of decision error, the threshold (alpha) at which we reject the null, and the probability (power) of detecting a real effect. These ideas are abstract but among the most heavily and repeatedly tested in the whole paper, often as direct definitional MCQs.

Central Limit Theorem

Definition

The Central Limit Theorem (CLT): regardless of the shape of the parent population, the sampling distribution of the sample mean approaches a normal distribution as sample size increases, with the approximation generally adequate at N ≥ 30. Its standard deviation (the standard error of the mean) equals the population standard deviation divided by the square root of N.

The CLT is the large-sample licence to use z and t: parametric tests assume the sampling distribution of the mean is normal, not that the raw data are normal, and the CLT guarantees that normality once N is reasonably large even when the population is skewed. Worked example: a population of reaction times is heavily right-skewed with standard deviation 200 ms. If you draw samples of N = 64, the standard error of the mean is 200 / square root of 64 = 200 / 8 = 25 ms, and the distribution of those sample means is approximately normal despite the skewed parent. This is why a t-test on means of 64 skewed scores is defensible while the same test on the 64 individual scores might not be. Note the standard error shrinks with the square root of N: quadrupling the sample halves the standard error.

Type I and Type II errors

Null is actually TRUENull is actually FALSE
Reject nullType I error (alpha): false positiveCorrect (power, 1 minus beta)
Retain nullCorrectType II error (beta): false negative

A Type I error (alpha) is rejecting a true null hypothesis: claiming an effect that is not real (a false positive). Alpha is set by the researcher in advance, conventionally at 0.05, meaning a 5% willingness to cry wolf. A Type II error (beta) is failing to reject a false null: missing a real effect (a false negative). The two errors trade off against each other along the decision threshold: pushing the threshold to make false positives rarer inevitably makes false negatives commoner, holding everything else constant.

Memory aid

Picture the boy who cried wolf. Crying "wolf" when there is none is the Type I (alpha) false alarm. Staying silent when the wolf is really there is the Type II (beta) miss. Alpha is the error you set; beta is the error you estimate. "Type 1 = 1 false alarm, the positive you should not have made."

Statistical power

Power = 1 minus beta: the probability of correctly detecting an effect that truly exists (correctly rejecting a false null). The convention is power ≥ 0.80, so beta ≤ 0.20, accepting a 20% miss rate. Four things raise power:

  • Larger sample size (N): the single most controllable lever; a bigger N shrinks the standard error, narrowing the sampling distributions so true and null distributions overlap less.
  • Larger effect size (gamma): bigger true effects are easier to detect (this is a property of nature, not under your control, but it is estimated in advance for sample-size planning).
  • A larger (more lenient) alpha: raising alpha from 0.01 to 0.05 increases power by moving the rejection threshold, but at the cost of more Type I errors. This is the fundamental alpha-power trade-off.
  • Lower error variance / one-tailed tests: reducing measurement noise (better instruments, tighter procedures) or using a directional test concentrates the test's sensitivity where the effect is expected.

Worked intuition: two studies test the same true effect of d = 0.5. The first uses N = 20 per group and achieves power around 0.34 (it will miss the real effect about two-thirds of the time, an underpowered study). The second uses N = 64 per group and achieves power around 0.80. Same true effect, same alpha, but the larger sample buys the detection probability. This is exactly why a non-significant result from a tiny study is uninformative: it may reflect low power, not absence of effect.

High-yield

Power = 1 minus beta. The cleanest way to raise power without inflating Type I error is to increase N. Lowering alpha (say to 0.01) reduces Type I error but reduces power too (raises beta). Alpha and beta trade off; only sample size and effect size buy you out of the trade-off.

Exam trap

"Increasing power requires increasing alpha" is a half-truth distractor: raising alpha does raise power, but at the cost of more false positives, so it is not the recommended route. The recommended route is increasing N, which raises power while leaving alpha untouched. Also: power is the probability of correctly rejecting a false null, not the probability the null is false.

K. Effect size formulations

A p-value tells you only whether an effect exists (whether it is distinguishable from chance); an effect size tells you how big the effect is, on a scale independent of sample size. This matters because a large enough sample renders even a trivially small effect "statistically significant", so effect sizes (and confidence intervals around them) are now mandatory reporting in every major journal and a favourite paper topic. The two families are standardised mean differences (the d family) and proportion-of-variance-explained indices (the eta and omega family).

  • Cohen's d: the standardised mean difference, d = (mean1 minus mean2) / SDpooled, using the pooled standard deviation of the two groups in the denominator. It expresses the gap between two means in standard-deviation units. Benchmarks: 0.2 small, 0.5 medium, 0.8 large. (Hedges' g is the small-sample-corrected version, applying a downward correction factor because d is slightly biased upward in small samples.)
  • Eta-squared (eta2): for ANOVA, the proportion of total variance explained by a factor = SSeffect / SStotal. Benchmarks: 0.01 small, 0.06 medium, 0.14 large.
  • Partial eta-squared (partial eta2): SSeffect / (SSeffect + SSerror): it removes other factors' variance from the denominator, so it asks what proportion of the variance not already explained by the other factors this factor explains. In multi-factor designs the partial values are larger than ordinary eta-squared and (a common trap) can sum to more than 1.
  • Omega-squared (omega2): a less biased, more conservative estimate of variance explained, preferred for small samples because eta-squared overestimates the population effect. Omega-squared adjusts for the number of groups and the error variance, pulling the estimate down toward the true population value.

Worked example (Cohen's d): a treatment group has mean = 80 and a control group has mean = 74, and the pooled standard deviation is 12. Then d = (80 minus 74) / 12 = 6 / 12 = 0.5, a medium effect by Cohen's benchmarks: the groups differ by half a standard deviation. Notice this number does not change if you double the sample size, whereas the p-value would shrink: that independence from N is the whole point of an effect size.

Worked example (eta-squared): an ANOVA reports SSbetween (the effect) = 40 and SStotal = 200. Then eta-squared = 40 / 200 = 0.20, meaning the factor explains 20% of the total variance, a large effect. If the within (error) sum of squares were 60, the partial eta-squared would be 40 / (40 + 60) = 40 / 100 = 0.40, visibly larger than the ordinary eta-squared precisely because the denominator has been trimmed.

Exam trap

Eta-squared overestimates the population effect (it is positively biased, especially with small samples); omega-squared corrects this and is smaller. Do not report partial eta-squared as if it were ordinary eta-squared: partial values do not sum to the total variance explained and are inflated in factorial designs. And remember d is a mean-difference metric while eta-squared is a variance-explained metric: a question that pairs "Cohen's d" with "proportion of variance" is testing this category error.

L. Parametric assumption testing: the diagnostic toolkit

Every parametric test rests on assumptions about the data (normality, equal variances, independence, and for some designs sphericity or homogeneous regression slopes). This section is the examiner's goldmine because the questions are mechanical and specific: "which diagnostic test, which numeric threshold, and what do you switch to when the assumption fails?" Learn each diagnostic with its sample-size rule and its remedy.

Normality

  • Shapiro-Wilk test: the preferred normality test for small samples (N < 50); it is the most powerful normality test for small N. A significant result means the data depart from normality.
  • Kolmogorov-Smirnov test: used for larger samples (N ≥ 50); it compares the cumulative sample distribution to a reference normal (with the Lilliefors correction when the mean and variance are estimated from the sample rather than known).
  • Skewness and kurtosis: as a rough rule, values within roughly plus or minus 1 (some texts say plus or minus 2) indicate acceptable normality. Skewness is asymmetry (a long tail to one side); kurtosis is peakedness and tail-heaviness (positive kurtosis = a sharp peak with heavy tails).
Exam trap

The N split is highly testable: Shapiro-Wilk for N less than 50; Kolmogorov-Smirnov for N of 50 or more. Also note that with very large N, normality tests become hypersensitive and flag trivial, harmless departures as significant, so inspect skew/kurtosis and Q-Q plots too; do not reject normality on the p-value alone. Remember the assumption is about the residuals / sampling distribution, which the Central Limit Theorem protects at large N anyway.

Homogeneity of variance

  • Levene's test: the robust default; it tests equality of variances by running an ANOVA on the absolute deviations from each group's mean (or, in the more robust variant, the median, the "center alignment", which makes it resistant to non-normality). A significant Levene means the variances differ.
  • Bartlett's test: more powerful than Levene if the data are genuinely normal, but very sensitive to departures from normality (it is a chi-square based check). Use Bartlett only when normality is well established; otherwise prefer Levene.

Sphericity (repeated measures)

Sphericity is an assumption specific to repeated-measures ANOVA: that the variances of the differences between all possible pairs of conditions are equal. It is tested with Mauchly's test. If Mauchly's test is significant, sphericity is violated, the F-test becomes too liberal (inflated Type I error), and you correct the degrees of freedom by multiplying them by an epsilon estimate (a number between 0 and 1 that shrinks the degrees of freedom, raising the critical F):

  • Greenhouse-Geisser correction: the more conservative correction, used when the estimated epsilon is below 0.75 (a more severe violation).
  • Huynh-Feldt correction: the less conservative correction, used when epsilon is above 0.75 (a milder violation), because Greenhouse-Geisser would over-correct and cost power.
High-yield

Sphericity applies ONLY to repeated-measures (within-subjects) designs. Mauchly's test detects violation; if violated, correct df with Greenhouse-Geisser (epsilon below 0.75) or Huynh-Feldt (epsilon above 0.75). Both shrink the degrees of freedom, which raises the critical F and restores a valid Type I error rate. There is no sphericity assumption in a purely between-subjects ANOVA, so a question applying Mauchly's test to independent groups is wrong by construction.

Homogeneity of regression slopes (for ANCOVA)

ANCOVA adjusts the dependent variable for a covariate (statistically equating groups on a nuisance variable), and it assumes the covariate-to-outcome relationship (the regression slope) is the same in every group: this is homogeneity of regression slopes. It is tested by checking the covariate × group interaction; if that interaction is significant, the slopes differ, the single adjusted comparison ANCOVA computes is misleading, and you should not use ANCOVA. Worked logic: if a covariate (baseline anxiety) predicts the outcome steeply in the treated group but flatly in the control group, no single "adjusted mean difference" is honest, because the group gap itself depends on the covariate value.

Multicollinearity

In multiple regression, multicollinearity (predictors highly correlated with each other) destabilises coefficient estimates, inflating their standard errors so that individually important predictors can appear non-significant. It is diagnosed with the Variance Inflation Factor (VIF): VIF > 5 signals serious redundancy (some texts use > 10 as the severe cut-off). VIF is the reciprocal of tolerance: VIF = 1 / Tolerance, where tolerance = 1 minus the R2 of a predictor regressed on all the other predictors. Worked example: if one predictor is regressed on the others and yields R2 = 0.90, then tolerance = 1 minus 0.90 = 0.10 and VIF = 1 / 0.10 = 10, a severe multicollinearity flag; that predictor is 90% explained by the others and adds little unique information. The remedy is to drop or combine the redundant predictors.

M. Hypothesis-testing executions

Now the engine room: the actual inferential tests, organised by how many groups, how many dependent variables, and whether the groups are related. The exam tests both the correct choice of test for a described situation and the internal logic (especially the F-ratio partition and the MANOVA statistics), so we teach the family tree with worked numbers.

The t-test family

  • One-sample t-test: compares one sample mean to a known or hypothesised population value (is this class's mean IQ different from the population norm of 100?).
  • Independent-samples t-test: compares the means of two separate, unrelated groups (treatment vs control).
  • Paired-samples (dependent) t-test: compares two related measurements (pre vs post on the same people, or matched pairs); it works on the difference scores, which removes stable between-person variation and so is more powerful when measurements are correlated.
  • Welch's t-test: the correction used when the two groups have unequal variances (heterogeneity, flagged by Levene). It does not pool the variances and it adjusts the degrees of freedom downward (the Welch-Satterthwaite df), so it stays valid when the standard t-test's equal-variance assumption fails. Many statisticians now recommend Welch's as the routine default for two-group comparisons.
Exam trap

When Levene's test is significant (variances unequal), you do not abandon the t-test; you switch to Welch's t-test (the "equal variances not assumed" row in SPSS output). Switching to a non-parametric test (Mann-Whitney) is required when normality fails, not merely when variances are unequal. Conflating "unequal variances" with "use a non-parametric test" is the classic error.

ANOVA and its variants

Analysis of Variance compares means across three or more groups using a single F-ratio, thereby avoiding the inflated family-wise Type I error you would incur by running many separate t-tests. Its logic is variance partitioning:

Definition

SSTotal = SSBetween + SSWithin. Total variability splits into between-groups variability (differences among the group means, the "signal", reflecting treatment plus error) and within-groups variability (variability of scores inside each group, the "noise", pure error). F = (SSBetween/dfbetween) / (SSWithin/dfwithin) = mean square between over mean square within. A large F means the signal exceeds the noise.

Worked F-ratio: three groups of five participants each (total N = 15, so k = 3 groups). Suppose SSBetween = 60 and SSWithin = 36. The degrees of freedom are dfbetween = k minus 1 = 2 and dfwithin = N minus k = 15 minus 3 = 12. Then the mean squares are MSbetween = 60 / 2 = 30 and MSwithin = 36 / 12 = 3. The F-ratio is F = 30 / 3 = 10. You compare this 10 to the critical F at df = (2, 12); the larger the F, the stronger the evidence that the group means differ. (If F were near 1, signal and noise would be comparable, consistent with the null.)

  • Factorial ANOVA: two or more factors, yielding main effects and interactions (see Section H).
  • Repeated-measures ANOVA: the same participants measured under all conditions; more powerful (it removes stable between-subject variance from the error term) but assumes sphericity (Section L).
  • ANCOVA: ANOVA with a covariate statistically removed first, increasing power by shrinking error variance; assumes homogeneity of regression slopes (Section L).
  • MANOVA: ANOVA with two or more dependent variables analysed jointly via centroid (mean vector) comparisons, controlling Type I error across the outcomes and able to detect effects on the combination of DVs that no single univariate test would catch.

MANOVA significance is read from four multivariate test statistics: Wilks' Lambda (the most commonly reported; a smaller Lambda means a bigger effect, because it is the proportion of unexplained variance), Pillai's Trace (the most robust to assumption violations and unequal group sizes, so it is preferred when the data are imperfect), Hotelling's Trace, and Roy's Largest Root. For a two-group comparison all four give identical results.

Memory aid

Wilks' Lambda is "leftover" variance: smaller is better (less unexplained = bigger effect). Pillai's Trace is the robust one: use it when assumptions are shaky or group sizes unequal. Hotelling's Trace is essentially the two-group case extended.

Post-hoc correction protocols

A significant omnibus ANOVA tells you some means differ but not which; post-hoc tests make the pairwise comparisons while controlling the family-wise error rate that would otherwise balloon (with k groups there are k(k minus 1)/2 pairwise comparisons, and each carries its own chance of a Type I error):

  • Tukey's HSD (Honestly Significant Difference): the standard for all pairwise comparisons; well balanced between power and Type I control when group sizes are equal.
  • Bonferroni correction: divides alpha by the number of comparisons, alphanew = alpha / k. Simple and very conservative (it over-corrects with many comparisons, costing power). Worked example: with alpha = 0.05 and 10 comparisons, each is tested at 0.05 / 10 = 0.005.
  • Scheffé's test: the most conservative of all; needed for complex contrasts (combinations of several means, not just pairs). Use it when comparisons are decided after seeing the data or involve groupings of means.
  • Dunnett's test: the specialist test for comparing several treatment groups each against a single control; more powerful than Tukey for that specific job because it makes fewer comparisons.
High-yield

Match the post-hoc to the question: Tukey HSD = all pairwise; Bonferroni = a few planned comparisons (alpha/k); Scheffé = complex contrasts (most conservative); Dunnett = many treatments vs one control. Bonferroni's formula alpha/k is a near-certain MCQ.

N. Non-parametric and categorical equivalents

When parametric assumptions fail and cannot be repaired (especially when normality is violated, or when the data are inherently ordinal or nominal), you switch to non-parametric tests, which work on ranks or frequencies rather than raw interval scores and so make no distributional assumption about the population shape. Knowing the parametric-to-non-parametric mapping cold is one of the most reliably tested facts in the section:

Parametric testNon-parametric equivalentUse
Independent t-testMann-Whitney UTwo independent groups, ordinal/non-normal data.
Paired t-testWilcoxon signed-rankTwo related/matched measurements.
One-way ANOVAKruskal-Wallis HThree or more independent groups.
Repeated-measures ANOVAFriedman's test (two-way ANOVA by ranks)Three or more related measurements.

The shared logic: each test replaces the raw scores with their ranks, then asks whether the ranks are distributed differently across conditions than chance would allow. Because ranks discard the exact spacing between scores, non-parametric tests are slightly less powerful than their parametric cousins when the parametric assumptions actually hold, which is why you reserve them for when the assumptions fail rather than using them by default.

Chi-square tests for categorical data

  • Chi-square test of independence: tests whether two categorical variables are associated (laid out in a contingency table) by comparing observed and expected cell frequencies, where the expected count of a cell = (row total × column total) / grand total.
  • Chi-square goodness-of-fit: tests whether one categorical variable's observed frequencies match a hypothesised distribution (does a die land on each face equally often?).

Worked chi-square decision: a 2 × 2 table of treatment (drug vs placebo) by outcome (recovered vs not). Suppose a cell has observed count O = 30 and, from the row and column totals, an expected count E = 24. That cell contributes (O minus E)2 / E = (30 minus 24)2 / 24 = 36 / 24 = 1.5 to the chi-square statistic. You sum this contribution across all four cells to get the total chi-square, then compare it to the critical value at df = (rows minus 1) × (columns minus 1) = 1 × 1 = 1, which is 3.84 at alpha = 0.05. If the summed chi-square exceeds 3.84, the variables are associated (you reject independence).

The key assumption: expected cell frequencies should be at least 5 (in most cells). When this fails, you must correct or switch:

  • Yates' correction for continuity: applied to 2 × 2 tables to reduce chi-square's tendency to overestimate significance when counts are small (it subtracts 0.5 from each absolute observed-minus-expected difference before squaring).
  • Fisher's exact test: the proper test for small samples / small expected frequencies in a 2 × 2 table; it computes the exact probability of the observed table directly rather than approximating with the chi-square distribution.
  • McNemar's test: for paired nominal data (the same subjects measured twice, pre vs post), testing whether the proportions changed; it focuses only on the discordant cells (the people who switched category), ignoring those who stayed the same.
Exam trap

When expected frequencies fall below 5 in a 2 × 2 table, use Fisher's exact test (not ordinary chi-square). For paired nominal data (the same people before and after), use McNemar's, not the independence chi-square: McNemar is the nominal cousin of the paired t-test, whereas the chi-square of independence assumes independent observations and would be invalid on repeated measures of the same people.

O. Correlation and regression

Correlation quantifies the strength and direction of a relationship between variables; regression uses one or more variables to predict another. The exam mines this area heavily for two things: choosing the right coefficient for the data types in front of you (continuous, ordinal, true or artificial dichotomy), and the precise interpretation distinctions (partial vs semi-partial, stepwise vs hierarchical, log-odds vs odds ratio).

Bivariate correlation coefficients

  • Pearson's r: for two continuous, linearly related, normally distributed variables; the parametric coefficient. It ranges from minus 1 (perfect negative) through 0 (no linear relationship) to plus 1 (perfect positive), and its square (r2) is the proportion of shared variance.
  • Spearman's rho: the rank-order correlation; non-parametric, for ordinal data or non-linear but monotonic relationships (it is simply Pearson's r computed on the ranks of the scores).
  • Kendall's tau: another rank correlation, based on the number of concordant versus discordant pairs; it is preferred over Spearman for small samples or data with many tied ranks, and it gives a more conservative (smaller in absolute value) coefficient.

Special-distribution coefficients

The exam loves the "true vs artificial dichotomy" distinction, where a true dichotomy is genuinely two categories (alive/dead) and an artificial dichotomy is a continuous variable forced into two (pass/fail carved from a continuous exam score):

CoefficientVariable 1Variable 2
Point-biserialTrue dichotomy (genuinely two categories, e.g. alive/dead)Continuous interval
BiserialArtificial dichotomy (a continuous variable forced into two, e.g. pass/fail from a score)Continuous interval
Phi (phi)True dichotomyTrue dichotomy
TetrachoricArtificial dichotomyArtificial dichotomy
Memory aid

"Biserial has artificial dichotomies hidden in it (both words bend a continuum); point-biserial is for a true two-category split. Phi = two TRUE dichotomies; Tetrachoric = two ARTIFICIAL dichotomies. True-true = phi; fake-fake = tetrachoric.

Partial vs semi-partial correlation

Partial correlation removes the variance of a third (control) variable from both primary variables, then correlates the residuals: it asks "what is the X-Y relationship with Z held constant for both?". Semi-partial (part) correlation removes the control variable's variance from only one of the two variables. In regression, the semi-partial squared is the unique variance a predictor adds to R2. Worked illustration: studying the link between exercise (X) and mood (Y) while controlling for sleep (Z), partial correlation cleans sleep out of both exercise and mood; semi-partial cleans sleep out of exercise only, leaving mood untouched, and so answers "how much does exercise uniquely add once we know sleep?".

High-yield

Partial = remove the covariate from BOTH variables; semi-partial = remove it from only ONE. Because partial cleans both variables, it is usually the larger of the two in absolute value. This one-word difference ("both" vs "one") is the entire exam point.

Regression models

  • Ordinary Least Squares (OLS) regression: fits a line minimising the sum of squared residuals (the vertical distances of points from the line); the standard linear model.
  • Stepwise regression: predictors enter or leave automatically according to a statistical criterion (the data decide the order of entry). It is atheoretical and capitalises on chance (it can select predictors that fit this sample's noise), so it is widely criticised and should not be used to test theory.
  • Hierarchical regression: the researcher enters predictors in theory-driven blocks in a predetermined order, examining the change in R2 at each step (the increment a block adds over the earlier blocks). This is the preferred, theory-led approach.
  • Adjusted R2: R2 penalised for the number of predictors and the sample size, because raw R2 always rises (never falls) when predictors are added even if they are useless. Always report adjusted R2 in multiple regression.

Logistic regression

When the outcome is dichotomous (diagnosed vs not, relapsed vs not), OLS is inappropriate because it can predict impossible probabilities below 0 or above 1; instead you use logistic regression, which models the log-odds (the logit) of the outcome as a linear function of the predictors, mapping the unbounded linear predictor onto the 0-to-1 probability range through the logistic curve. Key quantities:

  • Odds Ratio (OR) = ebeta: exponentiating a coefficient converts log-odds back to an odds ratio. OR > 1 means the predictor raises the odds of the outcome; OR < 1 lowers them; OR = 1 means no effect. Worked example: a coefficient beta = 0.69 gives OR = e0.69 = about 2.0, so a one-unit rise in that predictor doubles the odds of the outcome.
  • Wald statistic: tests whether an individual predictor's coefficient differs significantly from zero (the logistic analogue of the t-test on an OLS regression coefficient).
  • Hosmer-Lemeshow test: the goodness-of-fit test for logistic regression; it groups cases by predicted probability and compares observed and predicted frequencies across those groups. Here a non-significant result is the good outcome (it means the model's predictions match the data).
Exam trap

For the Hosmer-Lemeshow test you WANT a non-significant p-value (good fit). A significant Hosmer-Lemeshow means the model does NOT fit the data; this inverted interpretation is a favourite trick. Also remember OR = ebeta, not beta itself: the raw coefficient is in log-odds units, so a coefficient of 0 corresponds to an odds ratio of 1 (no effect), not 0.

P. Factor analytic modelling

Factor analysis reduces many observed (measured) variables to a few underlying dimensions (factors), on the assumption that the observed correlations among items arise because the items share these latent causes. It is the statistical backbone of test construction (deriving the subscales of a personality inventory) and of construct validation, so the paper tests it through three decisions: the extraction method, the number of factors to keep, and the rotation type.

PCA vs Principal Axis Factoring

Definition

Principal Component Analysis (PCA) is a data reduction technique: it analyses total variance (common, unique, and error variance together) to produce components that summarise the variables as efficiently as possible. Principal Axis Factoring (PAF) is true factor analysis: it analyses only the shared (common) variance to uncover the latent constructs presumed to cause the correlations.

Use PCA when the goal is pure dimension reduction or computing composite scores; use PAF (or maximum-likelihood factoring) when the goal is to model latent constructs, which is the usual clinical aim. The mechanical difference sits on the diagonal of the correlation matrix being analysed: PCA places 1.0 on the diagonal (all of each variable's variance), whereas PAF places the communalities there (the shared variance only). That single change is why PCA can look like it explains more variance: it is counting the unique and error variance that PAF deliberately excludes.

Factor extraction criteria (how many factors to keep)

  • Kaiser criterion: retain factors with eigenvalues greater than 1.0 (an eigenvalue is the amount of variance a factor explains in units of single variables; an eigenvalue below 1 means the factor explains less than a single original variable, so it is not worth retaining). Simple, but it tends to over-extract factors.
  • Scree plot: plot the eigenvalues in descending order and find the "elbow" where the curve flattens; keep the factors above the bend (Cattell's scree test). Subjective but widely used.
  • Horn's parallel analysis: the most accurate modern method: generate eigenvalues from many sets of random data of the same size as yours, and retain only those real factors whose eigenvalues exceed the corresponding eigenvalues from the random data. It corrects the over-extraction of the Kaiser rule.
Exam trap

The Kaiser criterion (eigenvalue > 1) tends to OVER-extract factors, and the scree test is subjective; Horn's parallel analysis is the recommended, most accurate criterion. A question asking "the most accurate method to determine the number of factors" wants parallel analysis, not Kaiser. Do not confuse the eigenvalue (variance per factor) with the communality (variance per variable).

Rotation: orthogonal vs oblique

Rotation does not change how much variance is explained; it redistributes the loadings to make the solution interpretable, maximising high loadings and minimising low ones (Thurstone's "simple structure"). The fundamental choice is whether the factors are permitted to correlate:

  • Orthogonal rotation forces the factors to remain uncorrelated (a 90-degree, independent reference frame). Methods: Varimax (the most common; it simplifies the columns, that is, the factors) and Quartimax (it simplifies the rows, that is, the variables). Use when factors are assumed independent.
  • Oblique rotation allows the factors to correlate, reflecting reality, because psychological constructs usually overlap (anxiety and depression intercorrelate). Methods: Promax and Direct Oblimin. Oblique rotations additionally yield a factor-correlation matrix and separate pattern and structure matrices (the pattern matrix giving unique loadings, the structure matrix giving raw correlations).
High-yield

Orthogonal (Varimax, Quartimax) = uncorrelated factors, 90 degrees; Oblique (Promax, Direct Oblimin) = correlated factors. For real clinical constructs, which almost always intercorrelate, oblique rotation is usually more appropriate and is the defensible default. Varimax is the single most-named rotation in MCQs.

Loadings, cross-loadings, and communality

A factor loading is the correlation between a variable and a factor; a loading above .32 is the conventional minimum for an item to be considered meaningfully related to a factor (.32 squared is about 0.10, so roughly 10% shared variance). A cross-loading occurs when a variable loads above the cut-off on two or more factors, making it ambiguous about which construct it measures; such items are usually dropped or revised. The communality (h2) of a variable is the proportion of its variance explained by all the retained factors combined, computed as the sum of its squared loadings across the factors; a low communality (say below 0.30) means the retained factors capture little of that variable, marking it a candidate for removal. Worked example: an item loads 0.6 on Factor 1 and 0.3 on Factor 2; its communality is 0.62 + 0.32 = 0.36 + 0.09 = 0.45, so the factors explain 45% of that item's variance.

Memory aid

Loading > .32 to keep an item; communality h2 = shared variance an item gives to the factors; cross-loading = an item that cheats on two factors (drop it). Eigenvalue is per-factor variance (keep the factor if > 1); communality is per-variable variance.

High-yield

The "when assumptions fail, what do you switch to" logic is the backbone of this whole section, and the exam tests it constantly. Lock in the chain: variances unequal → Welch's t; normality fails → non-parametric (Mann-Whitney, Wilcoxon, Kruskal-Wallis, Friedman); sphericity fails → Greenhouse-Geisser / Huynh-Feldt; small expected cell counts in 2 × 2 → Fisher's exact (or Yates' correction); paired nominal → McNemar; slopes not homogeneous → ANCOVA invalid; high VIF → multicollinearity, drop or combine predictors.

3 · Psychopathology & Clinical Syndromes

This is the highest-yield section in the entire entrance, because clinical psychology papers test it the way an anatomy paper tests bones: by the exact number. A disorder is not a vague mood but an operationalised object with a symptom count, a duration gate, and a near-neighbour it must be told apart from. The single most-tested fact type here is the duration threshold, the precise window of time a presentation must occupy to earn its name, because two disorders can share every symptom and differ only in how long they last. Brief psychotic disorder and schizophrenia look identical in a snapshot; only the clock separates them. The second most-tested fact type is the symptom-count gate (five of nine for depression, three of twelve for catatonia, two of five for schizophrenia Criterion A), and the third is the obligatory symptom, the one item that must be present whatever else is (depressed mood or anhedonia for depression; a delusion, hallucination, or disorganised speech for schizophrenia). Master these three machines (the clock, the count, the obligatory item) and you can reconstruct most criteria sets under exam pressure. This section teaches each disorder from its mechanism up, gives you the exact criteria and durations verbatim in tables you can memorise, and for every entry states the differential, the trick of telling it from the condition it is most often confused with, because that confusion is exactly what the distractor options are built from. We begin with how the two classification systems are architected and what causal models underpin them, then march through the full spectrum disorder by disorder.

A. Diagnostic Frameworks & Comparative Systems

Before any single disorder can be learned, you must grasp the scaffolding that holds all of them, because a recurring class of exam item asks not "what are the criteria for X" but "how do the two great manuals differ in the way they file X." A nosology is a system of classification of disease; psychiatry runs on two, and they embody two different philosophies of what a mental disorder even is.

The two nosologies that dominate are the American Psychiatric Association's Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, Fifth Edition, Text Revision (DSM-5-TR, 2022) and the World Health Organization's International Classification of Diseases, Eleventh Revision (ICD-11), whose mental-and-behavioural-disorders chapter came into force in January 2022. Both are descriptive: they group disorders by observable clinical features rather than by presumed cause, a deliberately atheoretical stance inherited from DSM-III (1980), which broke from the earlier psychoanalytic, aetiology-driven framework in which disorders were defined by their supposed unconscious origin. That 1980 rupture is itself a tested fact: DSM-III is the edition credited with introducing operational criteria, the multiaxial system (later abolished in DSM-5, 2013, which collapsed the five axes into a single non-axial listing), and the atheoretical descriptive stance. DSM is built for a primarily American clinical and research audience and is famously operationalised: it specifies explicit symptom counts and time frames so that two clinicians applying it to the same patient should reach the same label (the goal of high inter-rater reliability). ICD is a global public-health instrument used for mortality and morbidity statistics across all member states; its Clinical Descriptions and Diagnostic Requirements (CDDR) read as flexible prototypes rather than rigid checklists, because they must function across radically different health systems, from tertiary hospitals to community health workers with no laboratory access.

Definition

A categorical classification treats a disorder as a discrete kind: a person either meets criteria or does not (DSM's dominant logic). A dimensional classification rates a problem along a continuum of severity or trait level, accepting that pathology shades gradually into normality. The standing critique of pure categorical systems is that they draw artificial boundaries, generate excessive comorbidity (a patient piling up four labels for one underlying problem), produce diagnostic heterogeneity (two people with the same label sharing almost no symptoms), and dump residual cases into vague "not otherwise specified" or "other specified" bins.

Definition

Reliability is the consistency of a diagnosis (do different clinicians, or the same clinician at two times, assign the same label?). Validity is whether the diagnostic category corresponds to a real, distinct entity with a characteristic cause, course, and treatment response. The historic complaint against DSM is that its operationalised criteria bought reliability at the possible expense of validity: categories that are reproducible but may not carve nature at its joints. This reliability-versus-validity tension is itself examinable.

The structural differences you must know

The exam loves the points where the two systems diverge in architecture, not just in wording. Several divergences are tested repeatedly, and you should be able to state each as a one-line contrast.

1. Categorical versus dimensional spine. DSM-5-TR remains predominantly categorical, bolting dimensional elements on (severity specifiers, the cross-cutting symptom measures, the Section III alternative model for personality). ICD-11 keeps categories for most disorders but turns strongly dimensional precisely where DSM does not: personality.

2. Acute stress disorder moved out of anxiety. In older frameworks acute and post-traumatic stress reactions sat near the anxiety disorders. ICD-11 carved out a dedicated grouping, Disorders specifically associated with stress, and relocated the trauma reactions there, alongside its new prolonged grief disorder and a reworked adjustment disorder. DSM-5 had already created a separate Trauma- and Stressor-Related Disorders chapter, so both systems now treat trauma reactions as their own block rather than as a subtype of anxiety. The high-yield phrasing is that ICD-11 deleted acute stress disorder from the anxiety grouping and placed it in a trauma-specific block; ICD-11 also classifies acute stress reaction itself as a normal reaction (a factor influencing health status rather than a mental disorder), which is a favourite trick.

3. Abolition of categorical personality disorders. This is the headline difference. The traditional model in both systems was a fixed list of named personality types. DSM-5 kept its ten categorical personality disorders in the main text but parked an Alternative Model for Personality Disorders (AMPD) in Section III, rating level of personality functioning plus five pathological trait domains. ICD-11 went all the way and abolished the categorical types entirely. It first assigns a global severity (personality difficulty below threshold, then mild, moderate, or severe) and then optionally adds up to five trait domain qualifiers: negative affectivity, detachment, dissociality, disinhibition, and anankastia, plus an optional borderline pattern qualifier retained for treatment continuity.

4. Gender and other relocations. ICD-11 moved gender incongruence out of the mental disorders chapter altogether (into a new chapter on conditions related to sexual health), explicitly de-pathologising it; DSM-5-TR retains gender dysphoria within its mental-disorders text, framing the distress (not the identity) as the clinical focus. ICD-11 also introduced complex PTSD, prolonged grief disorder (a diagnosis DSM-5-TR added too, in its 2022 revision), and gaming disorder as a recognised behavioural addiction, none of which mirror DSM exactly.

FeatureDSM-5-TRICD-11
PublisherAmerican Psychiatric AssociationWorld Health Organization
Primary purposeClinical and research diagnosisGlobal health statistics plus clinical use
Criteria styleOperationalised: explicit symptom counts and durationsPrototype-based flexible descriptions (CDDR)
Overall spinePredominantly categorical, dimensional add-onsCategorical for most disorders, dimensional for personality
Personality disordersTen categorical types retained; dimensional AMPD in Section IIICategorical types abolished: global severity plus trait qualifiers
Acute stress reactionAcute stress disorder is a diagnosable disorder in the trauma chapterAcute stress reaction reframed as a normal reaction (not a mental disorder); trauma block separate from anxiety
Gender conceptGender dysphoria (within mental disorders)Gender incongruence (moved out of mental disorders entirely)
High-yield

ICD-11 is the system that scrapped the ten categorical personality disorders for a global severity plus trait-qualifier dimensional model. DSM-5 still lists all ten categorically and offers its dimensional model only in Section III as an emerging measure. If an item asks "which system uses a dimensional model of personality," the answer is ICD-11. If it asks "which edition introduced operational criteria and the atheoretical stance," the answer is DSM-III (1980).

Exam trap

Do not say DSM-5 "removed" the categorical personality disorders. It kept all ten in the main text and merely added the alternative model in Section III. Only ICD-11 abolished the categories outright. The trap option flips the two systems. A second trap reverses the gender entries: ICD-11 de-pathologised gender (incongruence, out of mental disorders); DSM-5-TR keeps gender dysphoria inside.

Worked example: reading a comparison stem

Consider a typical item: "A clinician using a manual rates a patient's personality not by assigning one of ten named types but by setting a global severity and then noting prominent traits such as detachment and disinhibition. Which manual is in use?" Decode it by the architecture: naming traits plus a severity grade, with no categorical type, is the ICD-11 personality model. The five trait qualifiers (negative affectivity, detachment, dissociality, disinhibition, anankastia) are the give-away vocabulary. The answer is ICD-11. This is exactly the reasoning the comparison items demand: spot the architectural fingerprint, not the disorder name.

Etiological paradigms

Beyond classification, the paper tests the major causal frameworks, because every therapy chapter and every formulation question rests on them. Learn the five paradigms below as a set, because comparison items ("which model explains symptoms by conditional assumptions") are common.

Molecular genetics and behaviour genetics. Heritability is estimated chiefly from twin studies: comparing concordance in monozygotic (identical, sharing ~100% of genes) versus dizygotic (fraternal, ~50%) twins. The heritability coefficient, written as h squared (h2), is the proportion of phenotypic variance in a population attributable to genetic variance; for schizophrenia twin-based heritability is roughly 0.8, for bipolar disorder roughly 0.7 to 0.8, both among the most heritable disorders. Linkage analysis tracks whether a disorder co-segregates within families with known genetic markers of identified chromosomal location, while genome-wide association studies (GWAS) scan the whole genome for common variants of small effect. Adoption studies separate genes from rearing environment by following children adopted away from affected biological parents. The takeaway tested most is that severe disorders (schizophrenia, bipolar, autism) are highly heritable and polygenic: many genes of small effect, not one gene; and that heritability is a population statistic, not a statement about how "genetic" any one person's illness is.

Neurofunctional circuitry. This paradigm locates pathology in altered brain circuits and neurotransmitter systems: the dopamine hypothesis of psychosis (mesolimbic hyperdopaminergia driving positive symptoms, mesocortical hypodopaminergia driving negative symptoms), serotonergic and noradrenergic dysregulation in mood and anxiety, the monoamine hypothesis of depression, and structural or functional changes such as enlarged lateral ventricles in schizophrenia or amygdala hyper-reactivity in anxiety and PTSD.

Psychodynamic structural and object-relational models. The structural model (Freud) explains symptoms as the surface compromise of intrapsychic conflict among id, ego, and superego, managed by defence mechanisms; signal anxiety warns the ego of a threatened breakthrough of forbidden impulses. Object-relations theory (Klein, Fairbairn, Winnicott, Kernberg) shifts the unit of analysis from drives to internalised relationships: pathology reflects deficient or distorted internal representations of self and others laid down in early caregiving, so a borderline patient is understood through unintegrated "all-good" and "all-bad" object images (the root of splitting).

Cognitive-behavioural vulnerabilities. Behaviourism explains pathology through learning: classical conditioning (a neutral cue paired with fear), operant conditioning (avoidance negatively reinforced because it relieves anxiety, the engine that maintains phobias and OCD), and modelling/observational learning. The cognitive layer (Beck, Ellis) adds enduring schemas, deep core beliefs about self, world, and future (Beck's negative cognitive triad in depression), and the conditional (operational) assumptions built on them ("if I am not perfect, I am worthless"), which, when activated by a stressor, generate the automatic negative thoughts that drive symptoms.

Diathesis-stress integration. The unifying model. A diathesis is a pre-existing vulnerability (genetic, neurobiological, or cognitive) that lies dormant until a sufficient environmental stress triggers it. Neither alone suffices: a high diathesis needs only mild stress to decompensate, a low diathesis needs severe stress. The closely related biopsychosocial model (Engel) generalises this to the routine interplay of biological, psychological, and social contributors to any presentation.

Memory aid

Map each paradigm to its signature noun: genetics = heritability, neuro = circuit, psychodynamic = conflict / object, cognitive = schema / assumption, integrative = diathesis + stress. Match the noun in the stem to the paradigm. If a stem mentions "conditional assumptions" or "core beliefs," it is cognitive; if "object representations" or "id-ego-superego," it is psychodynamic.

B. Schizophrenia Spectrum & Psychotic Disorders

Psychosis is a loss of contact with reality, expressed through delusions, hallucinations, disorganised thinking and speech, grossly disorganised or catatonic behaviour, and negative symptoms. Schizophrenia is the prototype, and the whole spectrum is organised as a ladder of duration: the same psychotic picture earns a different name depending only on how long it has lasted and how completely the person recovers. DSM-5 requires at least two of five Criterion A symptoms present for a significant portion of one month (the active phase), at least one of those two being delusions, hallucinations, or disorganised speech (these three are the obligatory "core positive" trio); continuous signs of disturbance must persist for at least six months total, a window that may include prodromal and residual phases of attenuated symptoms. The five Criterion A symptoms are delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech, grossly disorganised or catatonic behaviour, and negative symptoms. Criterion B demands marked socio-occupational dysfunction, and criteria for substance use, medical illness, and the autism/communication-disorder overlap must be excluded. The whole spectrum is gated by duration, and that ladder is the most-tested fact in the section.

DisorderDuration gate (verbatim)Key feature / differential
Brief psychotic disorderAt least 1 day, less than 1 month, with eventual full return to premorbid functioningShortest rung; often stress-precipitated; recovers completely
Schizophreniform disorderBetween 1 and 6 months (total duration)Same symptoms as schizophrenia but caught before 6 months; impaired function not required
SchizophreniaAt least 6 months continuous disturbance, including at least 1 month of active-phase symptomsThe full disorder; marked socio-occupational decline required
Schizoaffective disorderDelusions or hallucinations for at least 2 weeks in the absence of a prominent mood episode, with a major mood episode for the majority of total illnessBridges psychosis and mood; see differential below
Delusional disorderAt least 1 delusion for at least 1 month; no prominent hallucinations; function otherwise largely intactNon-bizarre delusion, otherwise normal behaviour
Memory aid

Chant the psychotic ladder by length: brief is shortest (under one month), schizophreniform sits in the middle (one to six months), schizophrenia is longest (six months and beyond). The cut points 1 month and 6 months are simply the boundaries between the rungs. "One day to one month to six months" is the whole gate sequence.

Epidemiology and course (where tested)

A small cluster of epidemiological facts recurs. Schizophrenia has a lifetime prevalence of roughly 1% (about 0.3 to 0.7%) worldwide, broadly similar across cultures, with men tending to onset earlier (late teens to mid-twenties) than women (late twenties, with a smaller second peak after midlife). Twin and family data give the high heritability quoted above (around 0.8). The dopamine hypothesis (mesolimbic overactivity for positive symptoms) and the structural finding of enlarged ventricles anchor the biological story. Course modifiers worth knowing: acute onset, prominent mood symptoms, a clear precipitant, good premorbid functioning, and being married predict better outcome; insidious onset, prominent negative symptoms, and early age of onset predict worse outcome. These prognostic markers are reliably tested as "which feature predicts a good outcome" items.

Brief psychotic disorder and schizophreniform disorder

Brief psychotic disorder is the abrupt onset of one or more positive psychotic symptoms (delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech, or grossly disorganised/catatonic behaviour) lasting at least one day but less than one month, after which the person returns fully to premorbid functioning. It is frequently precipitated by a marked stressor (the "with marked stressor" specifier, historically brief reactive psychosis) and may occur with peripartum onset. Schizophreniform disorder is, symptomatically, schizophrenia caught early: the identical Criterion A picture but with a total duration of one to six months. Crucially, it does not require the decline in social or occupational functioning that schizophrenia mandates. If symptoms persist past six months the diagnosis converts to schizophrenia; if they remit before one month the label was brief psychotic disorder. A provisional specifier is used while waiting to see whether the person will recover before six months.

Worked vignette: a previously well 22-year-old develops persecutory delusions and auditory hallucinations three weeks after a bereavement. At week three he is floridly psychotic. If he recovers fully by week four (under one month), the retrospective label is brief psychotic disorder, with marked stressor. If he is still symptomatic at week ten (between one and six months) but his job and relationships are intact, it is schizophreniform disorder. If at month seven he remains psychotic with clear occupational collapse, it is schizophrenia. One patient, three labels, decided entirely by the clock and the functional decline.

Exam trap

The schizophreniform window is reckoned on total duration including prodrome and residual, not just the one-month active phase. And it is the one psychotic-spectrum diagnosis that does not require functional decline. Distractors swap these in. A further trap: brief psychotic disorder demands eventual full return to baseline; incomplete recovery pushes the case up the ladder.

Schneiderian first-rank symptoms

Kurt Schneider (1959) listed symptoms he thought, when present without organic cause, strongly suggested schizophrenia. They are heavily tested by name. The classic eleven, grouped: (1) audible thoughts (thought echo, hearing one's thoughts aloud); (2) voices arguing or discussing the patient in the third person; (3) voices commenting on one's actions (running commentary); (4) thought withdrawal (thoughts removed by an outside force); (5) thought insertion (alien thoughts placed in the mind); (6) thought broadcasting (thoughts diffuse out to others); (7) made (passivity) feelings; (8) made impulses; (9) made volitional acts; (10) somatic passivity (bodily sensations imposed by others); and (11) delusional perception (a normal percept given a private delusional meaning). Note that DSM-5 dropped the old special weighting that a single bizarre delusion or first-rank hallucination alone sufficed for diagnosis; two Criterion A symptoms are now always required, because research showed first-rank symptoms are neither specific to schizophrenia (they occur in mania and organic states) nor prognostically decisive.

High-yield

The "made" phenomena (made feelings, made impulses, made acts) plus somatic passivity are delusions of control / passivity: the sense that an external agent is driving one's body or mind. Thought insertion, withdrawal, and broadcasting are the thought-alienation triad. These are the most-asked Schneiderian items. The newer high-yield fact: DSM-5 removed their special diagnostic weighting, so a lone bizarre delusion no longer suffices.

Positive versus negative symptoms: the five A's

Positive symptoms are pathological additions to normal experience: delusions (fixed false beliefs held despite contrary evidence, e.g. persecutory, grandiose, referential, nihilistic), hallucinations (percepts without an external stimulus, auditory being most common in schizophrenia), disorganised speech (formal thought disorder: derailment/loosening of associations, tangentiality, word salad, neologisms), and grossly disorganised or bizarre behaviour. Negative symptoms are deficits, the loss of normal function, and they predict poorer prognosis and respond least to antipsychotics (especially the older typical agents). Memorise them as the five A's: avolition (loss of goal-directed drive), alogia (poverty of speech), anhedonia (inability to feel pleasure), asociality (withdrawal from social contact), and affective flattening (blunted/diminished emotional expression). The mesolimbic-mesocortical split from the biopsychology section maps onto this divide: limbic overactivity drives the positive symptoms, cortical underactivity drives the negative and cognitive ones.

Memory aid

The five negative symptoms are the 5 A's: Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality, Affective flattening. Positive symptoms ADD to experience; negative symptoms SUBTRACT from it. Positive symptoms respond to D2 blockade; negative symptoms barely respond, which is why they dominate the long-term disability.

Catatonia: 3 of 12 signs

Catatonia is a syndrome of marked psychomotor disturbance that can accompany schizophrenia, mood disorders (it is in fact commonest in mood disorders), or medical conditions; in DSM-5 it is a specifier and a stand-alone "catatonia associated with another mental disorder," not only a schizophrenia subtype. DSM-5 requires at least 3 of 12 signs. Learn all twelve, because the paper asks both the threshold number and the full list: (1) stupor (no psychomotor activity, not actively relating to environment); (2) catalepsy (passive induction of a posture held against gravity); (3) waxy flexibility (slight, even resistance to repositioning, like bending a candle); (4) mutism (no, or almost no, verbal response); (5) negativism (opposition to or no response to instructions); (6) posturing (spontaneous maintenance of a posture against gravity); (7) mannerisms (odd caricatures of normal actions); (8) stereotypies (repetitive, non-goal-directed movements); (9) psychomotor agitation (excessive, purposeless motor activity); (10) grimacing; (11) echolalia (mimicking another's speech); and (12) echopraxia (mimicking another's movements).

High-yield

Catatonia = 3 of 12 signs. Distinguish catalepsy (posture passively induced and held) from waxy flexibility (the even, candle-like resistance felt while repositioning) and from cataplexy (sudden loss of muscle tone triggered by emotion, which belongs to narcolepsy, not catatonia). The catalepsy / cataplexy swap is a classic distractor. Treatment fact sometimes tested: catatonia responds to benzodiazepines (lorazepam) and to ECT, not primarily to antipsychotics, which can worsen it.

Schizoaffective disorder and delusional disorder

Schizoaffective disorder requires an uninterrupted period during which a major mood episode (depressive or manic) is concurrent with Criterion A schizophrenia symptoms, PLUS delusions or hallucinations for at least two weeks in the absence of a prominent mood episode at some point in the lifetime, AND mood-episode symptoms present for the majority of the total active and residual illness duration. The two-week psychosis-without-mood window is the linchpin that separates it from a mood disorder with psychotic features. It is the bridge diagnosis: more mood than schizophrenia, more psychosis than a pure mood disorder.

Delusional disorder requires one or more delusions for at least one month, with Criterion A schizophrenia otherwise never met, no prominent hallucinations, and functioning not markedly impaired apart from the delusion's direct impact; behaviour is not obviously bizarre. The delusions may be non-bizarre (plausible scenarios such as being followed or deceived) or bizarre. Subtypes name the delusion's theme: erotomanic (a famous or higher-status person is in love with the patient), grandiose, jealous (the Othello syndrome of infidelity belief), persecutory (the commonest), somatic, mixed.

Exam trap

Schizoaffective vs. mood disorder with psychotic features: if psychosis occurs only during mood episodes, it is a mood disorder with psychotic features; if there is a period of at least two weeks of psychosis with the mood episode absent, it is schizoaffective. That two-week window is the entire differential. Delusional disorder vs. schizophrenia: delusional disorder spares function and lacks the other Criterion A symptoms; if hallucinations are prominent or function collapses, it is no longer delusional disorder.

The ICD-11 contrast for psychosis

ICD-11 keeps schizophrenia but, like the other clusters, softens the rigid time-and-count gates into prototypes. Two contrasts are worth carrying. First, ICD-11 requires a duration of symptoms of at least one month for schizophrenia (it does not impose DSM's full six-month total), so the same patient can meet ICD schizophrenia earlier than DSM schizophrenia, which is a tested discrepancy. Second, ICD-11 abolished the classical clinical subtypes (paranoid, hebephrenic/disorganised, catatonic, simple) that ICD-10 still listed, replacing them with cross-cutting symptom-domain specifiers; DSM-5 had already dropped the same subtypes. So the headline is: both modern systems deleted the named subtypes, but they differ on the duration gate (DSM six months total, ICD-11 one month).

C. Bipolar & Related Disorders

The bipolar disorders are defined by the presence of elevated-mood episodes (manic or hypomanic) on a background that usually also includes depression. The decisive distinctions are the duration and severity of the high. The conceptual key is that bipolarity is diagnosed off the highest mood state a person has ever reached: a single lifetime manic episode permanently defines Bipolar I, however much depression dominates the rest of the course. This is why the elevated pole, not the depressive one, is the diagnostic hinge.

Definition
