A manic episode is a distinct period of abnormally elevated, expansive, or irritable mood with increased energy or goal-directed activity, lasting at least 7 consecutive days (or any duration if hospitalisation is required), present most of the day nearly every day, with at least 3 of the DIG FAST symptoms (4 if mood is only irritable) and causing marked impairment, psychosis, or the need for hospitalisation. A hypomanic episode has the same symptom set and the same count (3, or 4 if only irritable) but lasts at least 4 consecutive days, is an unequivocal change in functioning observable by others, and does not cause marked impairment, psychosis, or hospitalisation. If psychosis or hospitalisation is present, the episode is manic by definition regardless of length.

DisorderDefining requirement (verbatim)
Bipolar I disorderAt least 1 manic episode (at least 7 days, or any duration if hospitalised); severe socio-occupational impairment. Depressive episodes usual but not required for diagnosis.
Bipolar II disorderAt least 1 hypomanic episode (at least 4 days, clear functional change, no gross impairment or hospitalisation) AND at least 1 major depressive episode. Never a full manic episode.
Cyclothymic disorderAt least 2 years in adults (1 year in children/adolescents) of numerous hypomanic and depressive symptoms that never meet full episode criteria; symptom-free intervals cannot exceed 2 months.
Rapid cycling (specifier)At least 4 distinct major mood episodes within a 12-month period.

Bipolar I is the only one requiring a full manic episode; a single lifetime manic episode suffices for the diagnosis even with no depression ever. Bipolar II requires both a hypomanic episode and a major depressive episode, and the presence of any full manic episode immediately reclassifies it as Bipolar I. It is a common error to treat Bipolar II as "milder"; its depressive burden is often heavier and more chronic, and its suicide risk is high. Cyclothymia is the persistent, sub-threshold chatter of highs and lows, never reaching full episode intensity, over the required two-year span (with no symptom-free gap longer than two months). Rapid cycling is a specifier, not a separate disorder, applied when four or more episodes occur in twelve months; it is associated with poorer treatment response and, in some patients, with antidepressant exposure.

Worked vignette: a 30-year-old has had two clear-cut major depressive episodes and, separately, a five-day period of decreased need for sleep, pressured speech, and overspending that her family noticed but that did not stop her working or require admission. The high is four days or more, observable, without gross impairment or hospitalisation: a hypomanic episode. Hypomania plus major depression equals Bipolar II. Now suppose that high had instead lasted eight days and ended in a psychiatric admission for grandiose, reckless behaviour: that is a manic episode, and the diagnosis jumps to Bipolar I, retrospectively reclassifying the whole illness.

Memory aid

Mania symptoms = DIG FAST: Distractibility, Insomnia (decreased need for sleep), Grandiosity, Flight of ideas, Activity increase, Speech (pressured), Thoughtlessness (risk-taking, e.g. spending, sex, reckless driving). Mania needs 3 of these (4 if mood is only irritable). "Decreased need for sleep" (feels rested on little sleep) is the bipolar marker, distinct from the insomnia of depression (wants sleep, cannot get it).

Exam trap

The mania duration is 7 days; hypomania is 4 days. The decisive line between them is not just length but impairment: hospitalisation, psychosis, or marked dysfunction automatically makes it mania regardless of duration. A 3-day high that lands the patient in hospital is a manic episode. A second trap: cyclothymia and persistent depressive disorder share the two-year-in-adults / one-year-in-youth gate, but cyclothymia has both poles (sub-threshold highs and lows) whereas persistent depressive disorder is depressive only.

Mixed features, differentials and the antidepressant caution

Two further points are reliably examined. First, the "with mixed features" specifier replaced the old DSM-IV "mixed episode": a manic or hypomanic episode can carry several depressive symptoms simultaneously (or a depressive episode can carry several manic symptoms), capturing the agitated, dysphoric high that does not fit a clean pole. Second, the central management caution: giving an antidepressant alone to a bipolar patient can precipitate a switch into mania or hypomania (a manic switch) or accelerate cycling, which is why a confirmed manic history reroutes treatment to mood stabilisers (lithium, valproate, lamotrigine) or atypical antipsychotics rather than antidepressant monotherapy. Lithium is the classic mood stabiliser, with the additional tested fact that it reduces suicide risk. The most consequential clinical differential is therefore bipolar depression versus unipolar depression: a person presenting in a depressive episode may actually be bipolar, and the give-aways of hidden bipolarity are early age of onset, recurrent brief depressions, a family history of bipolar disorder, psychotic or atypical features, and any past hypomania the patient under-reports because the high felt good. Missing this is the commonest real-world and exam error in the mood chapter.

High-yield

A single lifetime manic episode equals Bipolar I forever, regardless of how much depression dominates. Bipolar II = hypomania + major depression, never a full mania. The "mood plus increased energy/activity" gateway is a DSM-5 addition to Criterion A. Antidepressant monotherapy in bipolar disorder risks a manic switch; mood stabilisers are first-line, and lithium lowers suicide risk.

The ICD-11 contrast for bipolar

ICD-11 retains Bipolar type I and type II and cyclothymic disorder, and broadly mirrors the DSM durations (seven days for mania, four for hypomania). The notable DSM-5 change that ICD-11 also reflects is the addition of increased energy or activity to elevated/irritable mood as a required Criterion A feature for mania and hypomania (mood alone is no longer enough; there must be the energy/activity change too). This "mood plus energy" gateway is a modern, examinable update introduced to reduce over-diagnosis from mood elevation alone.

D. Depressive Disorders

The depressive disorders share a common feature of sad, empty, or irritable mood with somatic and cognitive changes that impair functioning; they differ in duration, timing, and developmental presentation. The depressive disorders chapter in DSM-5 was split off from the bipolar chapter (in DSM-IV they sat together as "mood disorders"), a structural change worth noting because it underlines that unipolar depression and bipolar disorder are now treated as distinct families.

Definition

Major depressive disorder (MDD) requires at least 5 of 9 symptoms during the same 2-week period, representing a change from previous functioning, and at least one of the five must be either depressed mood or loss of interest/pleasure (anhedonia). The nine: depressed mood; anhedonia; significant weight/appetite change; insomnia or hypersomnia; psychomotor agitation or retardation; fatigue/loss of energy; worthlessness or excessive guilt; impaired concentration/indecisiveness; recurrent thoughts of death or suicidality. Symptoms must cause distress or impairment and not be attributable to a substance or medical condition.

DisorderCriteria and duration (verbatim)
Major depressive disorderAt least 5 of 9 symptoms in the same 2-week block, including depressed mood or anhedonia.
Persistent depressive disorder (dysthymia)Depressed mood for at least 2 years in adults (1 year in children/adolescents); sub-threshold; no symptom-free gap longer than 2 months.
Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder (DMDD)Severe recurrent temper outbursts (verbal/behavioural) out of proportion, at least 3 times per week, across at least 12 months, with onset before age 10 (diagnosed ages 6 to 18).
Premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD)Mood and physical symptoms in the final week before menses, improving within days of onset and minimal post-menses, across most cycles of the past year.

Persistent depressive disorder (the DSM-5 merger of chronic MDD and the older dysthymia) is the long, low-grade depression: at least two years (one in youth) of depressed mood plus two of six features (poor appetite or overeating, insomnia or hypersomnia, low energy, low self-esteem, poor concentration, hopelessness), with no break exceeding two months. A patient can carry both diagnoses simultaneously (so-called double depression: a major depressive episode superimposed on dysthymia). DMDD was introduced to curb the over-diagnosis of paediatric bipolar disorder; its core is a persistently irritable or angry mood between the outbursts, distinguishing it from the episodic mood elevation of bipolar disorder, and it can only be first diagnosed between ages 6 and 18 with onset before age 10. PMDD ties depressive symptoms to the luteal phase of the menstrual cycle, remitting after menses, and is the depressive-chapter entry most easily forgotten.

Epidemiology (where tested)

MDD is among the commonest mental disorders, with a lifetime prevalence around 15 to 20% and a consistent finding that it is roughly twice as common in women as in men from adolescence onward (the 2:1 female-to-male ratio is a near-certain item). Mean age of onset is the mid-to-late twenties. The monoamine hypothesis (deficiency of serotonin and noradrenaline) underpins the first-line pharmacology (SSRIs), and the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis hyperactivity (raised cortisol, dexamethasone non-suppression) is the classic biological correlate. The 2:1 sex ratio for unipolar depression contrasts with the roughly equal sex ratio for Bipolar I, a comparison the paper sometimes tests directly.

Specifier mechanics

Specifiers refine an episode and are reliably tested. Melancholic features: profound despondency with loss of pleasure in nearly everything, mood non-reactive (does not lift even briefly to good news), distinct quality of depressed mood, worse in the morning (diurnal variation), terminal/early-morning insomnia (waking hours before the alarm), marked psychomotor change, anorexia/weight loss, and excessive guilt. Atypical features: the mirror image, mood reactivity preserved (brightens to positives), plus at least two of hyperphagia (increased appetite/weight), hypersomnia, leaden paralysis (heavy, leaden limbs), and a long-standing pattern of interpersonal rejection sensitivity. Peripartum onset: onset of the episode during pregnancy or within 4 weeks postpartum. Other specifiers include with psychotic features, with anxious distress, with mixed features, with catatonia, and with seasonal pattern (the basis of the lay term "seasonal affective disorder," now a seasonal-pattern specifier rather than a separate diagnosis).

Exam trap

The single cleanest melancholic-versus-atypical discriminator is mood reactivity. Melancholic mood is non-reactive (nothing lifts it) with early-morning waking and weight loss; atypical mood is reactive with oversleeping and overeating. The "leaden paralysis" and rejection sensitivity belong to atypical. A separate trap: the bereavement exclusion was removed in DSM-5, so a major depressive episode can be diagnosed during grief if full criteria are met (a much-debated change).

Key differentials within the depressive chapter

The chapter's internal differentials are tested as cleanly as its durations. MDD versus persistent depressive disorder turns on duration and intensity: MDD is the acute, full-threshold two-week (or longer) episode, whereas persistent depressive disorder is the chronic, often sub-threshold two-year floor; a patient meeting both at once is the classic double depression. MDD versus normal grief: ordinary bereavement waxes and wanes in waves tied to reminders, self-esteem is usually preserved, and thoughts of death centre on joining the deceased rather than on worthlessness, whereas a major depressive episode brings pervasive low mood, self-loathing, and global anhedonia; since DSM-5 removed the bereavement exclusion, the two can coexist. DMDD versus paediatric bipolar disorder: DMDD shows a persistently irritable baseline punctuated by outbursts, with no discrete episodes of elevated mood, while bipolar disorder shows episodic mood elevation against a normal baseline; DMDD exists precisely to stop chronically irritable children being mislabelled bipolar. DMDD versus oppositional defiant disorder: the severe temper outbursts and the threshold (three or more per week, twelve months) push toward DMDD, and where both could apply DMDD takes precedence over ODD.

Memory aid

Anchor the duration ladder of low mood: a major depressive episode needs 2 weeks, persistent depressive disorder needs 2 years (1 year in youth), and DMDD needs 12 months of outbursts with onset before age 10. The obligatory MDD symptom is always depressed mood or anhedonia; without one of those two you cannot reach five-of-nine in a valid way.

The ICD-11 contrast for depression

ICD-11 groups depressive disorders separately from bipolar, as DSM does, and uses a similar two-week duration and symptom-cluster logic, but its CDDR phrase the symptoms as affective, cognitive-behavioural, and neurovegetative clusters rather than a strict five-of-nine count. ICD-11 introduced mixed depressive and anxiety disorder as a recognised category (sub-threshold depression and anxiety together), which DSM handles only through an "with anxious distress" specifier, not a stand-alone diagnosis. ICD-11 also classifies dysthymic disorder and recognises premenstrual dysphoric disorder. The examinable contrast: ICD-11 has a free-standing mixed anxiety-depression diagnosis; DSM-5 does not.

E. Anxiety Disorders

Anxiety disorders share excessive fear (an emotional response to real or perceived imminent threat, with sympathetic surge and fight-or-flight) and anxiety (anticipation of future threat, with vigilance and muscle tension), together with associated behavioural disturbance, chiefly avoidance. They are separated largely by the object of the fear and the duration over which it must persist. DSM-5 reorganised this chapter: obsessive-compulsive disorder and the trauma disorders were moved out into their own chapters, leaving the anxiety chapter to the disorders below, a structural fact worth carrying because older texts and ICD-10 still grouped them together.

DisorderCriteria and duration (verbatim)
Panic disorderRecurrent unexpected panic attacks followed by at least 1 month of persistent worry about future attacks or maladaptive behavioural change.
Generalised anxiety disorder (GAD)Excessive, uncontrollable worry about multiple domains for at least 6 months, with at least 3 of 6 somatic/arousal signs in adults (1 in children).
Specific phobiaMarked fear of a specific object/situation, lasting at least 6 months, driving avoidance.
Social anxiety disorderMarked fear of social/performance scrutiny, lasting at least 6 months.
AgoraphobiaMarked fear of at least 2 of 5 situations (public transport, open spaces, enclosed places, queues/crowds, being outside the home alone), lasting at least 6 months.
Separation anxiety disorderDevelopmentally inappropriate fear of separation: at least 4 weeks in children/adolescents, at least 6 months in adults.
Selective mutismConsistent failure to speak in specific social situations (e.g. school) despite speaking elsewhere, for at least 1 month (not limited to the first month of school).

A panic attack is an abrupt surge of intense fear or discomfort peaking within minutes, with at least 4 of 13 somatic/cognitive symptoms (palpitations, sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, choking, chest pain, nausea, dizziness, chills/heat, paraesthesias, derealisation/depersonalisation, fear of losing control or "going mad," fear of dying). The attack itself is not a disorder and can occur as a specifier across many conditions; panic disorder needs the recurrent unexpected attacks plus the one-month anticipatory worry or maladaptive change (the second-arrow, the fear of the fear). GAD's six somatic signs are restlessness/feeling keyed-up, easy fatigue, difficulty concentrating, irritability, muscle tension, and sleep disturbance; three of six are needed in adults (only one in children). Agoraphobia's "2 of 5 situations" and GAD's "3 somatic signs over 6 months" are favourite number facts. Most anxiety disorders show the familiar female preponderance (roughly 2:1), and onset clusters in childhood (separation anxiety, specific phobia, social anxiety) earlier than GAD and panic, which typically begin in adolescence to adulthood.

Worked vignette: a 28-year-old has had several out-of-the-blue episodes of pounding heart, breathlessness, and a conviction she is about to die, each peaking in minutes; for the past six weeks she has avoided the gym and stopped driving alone for fear of another. The attacks are unexpected and recurrent; the one-month-plus worry and avoidance follow. That is panic disorder. If she instead feared the gym and car specifically because escape would be hard if an attack struck, and feared at least two such situations, the avoidance would also earn an agoraphobia diagnosis layered on top.

Exam trap

Specific phobia vs. agoraphobia vs. social anxiety: all three run at least 6 months. Specific phobia fears a discrete object; social anxiety fears scrutiny/embarrassment; agoraphobia fears situations from which escape is difficult or help unavailable if panic strikes (needs 2 of 5 listed). Match the feared cue, not just the avoidance. A second trap: in DSM-5, agoraphobia and panic disorder are now separate, independently coded diagnoses (no longer "panic disorder with agoraphobia" as one label).

Memory aid

Separation anxiety durations: think "children are quick, adults are slow," 4 weeks for children, 6 months for adults. The longer adult requirement guards against over-diagnosing normal adult attachment worry. For GAD remember the six somatic signs with "WASTED"-style recall: Worry plus restlessness, fatigue, concentration loss, irritability, muscle tension, sleep loss; three of these six in adults.

Maintenance mechanism and the panic-specific differentials

The behavioural account of why anxiety disorders persist is itself examinable. Mowrer's two-factor theory explains a phobia in two steps: the fear is first acquired by classical conditioning (a neutral cue paired with a frightening experience), then maintained by operant conditioning, because avoidance is negatively reinforced (escaping the cue relieves anxiety, and that relief rewards the avoidance, so it strengthens and the fear never extinguishes). This is the rationale for exposure therapy: blocking avoidance lets extinction occur. In panic disorder, Clark's cognitive model adds the catastrophic misinterpretation of benign bodily sensations (reading a racing heart as an imminent heart attack), which fuels the next attack. The differentials to lock down: panic attacks in panic disorder are unexpected (out of the blue), whereas panic attacks that are reliably cued by a feared object or situation point instead to a phobia or to agoraphobia; and a panic attack is never itself a diagnosis but a transdiagnostic specifier. A crucial medical differential: the somatic storm of a panic attack can mimic, and must be distinguished from, cardiac, respiratory, endocrine (thyroid, phaeochromocytoma), and substance-related causes, so anxiety disorders are diagnoses that also require organic mimics to be excluded.

High-yield

The duration spine: panic disorder needs 1 month of post-attack worry; GAD, specific phobia, social anxiety, and agoraphobia all need 6 months; separation anxiety needs 4 weeks in children, 6 months in adults; selective mutism needs 1 month. The count facts: panic attack = 4 of 13 symptoms; GAD = 3 of 6 somatic signs (1 in children); agoraphobia = fear of 2 of 5 situations. Maintenance is by negative reinforcement of avoidance; exposure is the corrective.

The ICD-11 contrast for anxiety

ICD-11 keeps a broadly parallel anxiety-and-fear-related-disorders grouping, but with two examinable differences. First, ICD-11 places separation anxiety disorder and selective mutism inside the anxiety grouping at all ages (DSM-5 also moved these into the anxiety chapter from the childhood chapter, so the systems now agree, a change from ICD-10/DSM-IV). Second, as noted under frameworks, ICD-11 keeps the trauma disorders entirely out of the anxiety block, and it does not require DSM's precise numeric counts, instead asking for a characteristic pattern over a comparable time frame. The single cleanest contrast to memorise: both modern systems now file separation anxiety and selective mutism as anxiety disorders, abandoning the old "disorders of childhood" placement.

F. Obsessive-Compulsive & Related Disorders

DSM-5 grouped these as a chapter separate from anxiety because they share repetitive, ritualised, body-focused or thought-focused behaviour and overlapping circuitry, specifically the cortico-striato-thalamo-cortical (CSTC) loops running through the orbitofrontal cortex, anterior cingulate, and caudate. This shared circuitry, and the shared partial response to serotonergic agents (SSRIs at higher doses) and to exposure-and-response-prevention, is the rationale for the chapter. ICD-11 likewise created an obsessive-compulsive-and-related-disorders grouping, so the two systems agree on carving OCD out of anxiety.

Definition

Obsessions are recurrent, intrusive, unwanted, ego-dystonic thoughts, urges, or images that cause marked anxiety, which the person attempts to ignore, suppress, or neutralise. Compulsions are repetitive behaviours (washing, checking, ordering) or mental acts (counting, praying, repeating words) the person feels driven to perform in response to an obsession or according to rigid rules, aimed at reducing anxiety or preventing a dreaded event, but not realistically connected to what they aim to prevent, or clearly excessive. OCD requires obsessions and/or compulsions that are time-consuming (more than 1 hour per day) or cause clinically significant distress or impairment.

OCD carries insight specifiers: good or fair insight (recognises beliefs are probably or possibly untrue), poor insight (thinks they are probably true), and absent insight / delusional beliefs (completely convinced they are true, which does not reclassify it as a psychotic disorder), plus a tic-related specifier (a current or past tic disorder, commoner in early-onset male cases). The mechanism that maintains OCD is the negative-reinforcement loop from the behavioural paradigm: the compulsion transiently relieves obsessional anxiety, and that relief reinforces the compulsion, so the ritual strengthens over time even as it fails to address the feared outcome. The other disorders in the chapter:

  • Body dysmorphic disorder (BDD): preoccupation with one or more perceived defects in appearance that are unnoticeable or slight to others, with repetitive behaviours (mirror checking, excessive grooming, skin picking, reassurance seeking) or mental acts (comparing one's appearance with others). The muscle dysmorphia specifier covers the belief that one's build is too small or insufficiently muscular (seen more in men). BDD carries a high suicide risk and a high rate of futile cosmetic procedures.
  • Hoarding disorder: persistent difficulty discarding possessions regardless of value, driven by a perceived need to save and by distress at discarding, producing accumulation that congests and clutters active living spaces and compromises their intended use (a kitchen too cluttered to cook in). It was a new, free-standing DSM-5 diagnosis, previously folded into OCD or OCPD.
  • Trichotillomania (hair-pulling disorder): recurrent pulling out of one's own hair causing hair loss, with repeated attempts to decrease or stop. Classed among the body-focused repetitive behaviours.
  • Excoriation (skin-picking) disorder: recurrent skin picking causing skin lesions, with repeated attempts to stop. The other body-focused repetitive behaviour.

PANDAS (Paediatric Autoimmune Neuropsychiatric Disorders Associated with Streptococcal infections) and the broader PITAND/PANS spectrum describe an abrupt, dramatic onset of OCD symptoms and/or tics in children following streptococcal (PANDAS) or other infectious or immune triggers, hypothesised to arise from autoantibodies cross-reacting with basal-ganglia neurons (the same CSTC circuitry). They are the autoimmune etiological network of the OCD chapter, and the clue in a stem is the sudden, overnight onset of OCD or tics in a child after a sore throat or infection.

Worked vignette: a 26-year-old spends three hours a day washing his hands because intrusive, distressing images of contamination flood his mind; he knows the fear is excessive but cannot resist, and washing briefly relieves the dread before it returns. Time-consuming (over one hour), ego-dystonic intrusive thoughts (obsessions) driving a ritual (compulsion) that he recognises as excessive (good insight): this is OCD. Contrast a 26-year-old who insists colleagues follow his rigid filing system, never delegates because no one meets his standards, and feels his perfectionism is entirely correct: no intrusive thoughts, no rituals, the traits feel right to him (ego-syntonic). That is obsessive-compulsive personality disorder, not OCD.

Exam trap

Obsessions are ego-dystonic (experienced as alien, intrusive, distressing, resisted). This contrasts with the ego-syntonic traits of obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (OCPD), where the perfectionism and rigidity feel right and correct to the person. OCD is anxiety-driven ritual with true obsessions and compulsions; OCPD is a pervasive rigid personality style with neither. Do not confuse them; the ego-dystonic versus ego-syntonic distinction is the whole discriminator.

Epidemiology, treatment and the OCD-versus-related differentials

OCD has a lifetime prevalence of roughly 2 to 3%, a roughly equal sex ratio in adults (with male preponderance in early-onset childhood cases), and a characteristic bimodal onset (a childhood/adolescent peak and an early-adult peak). The most common symptom dimensions are contamination/washing, harm/checking, symmetry/ordering, and forbidden (aggressive, sexual, religious) intrusive thoughts with covert mental rituals; the last group is easily missed because the compulsions are internal. First-line treatments are exposure and response prevention (ERP), the behavioural gold standard, and serotonergic medication (SSRIs, often at higher doses and longer trials than for depression, plus clomipramine), which is the pharmacological hint that the chapter sits on serotonergic CSTC circuitry. The internal differentials are heavily tested. OCD versus generalised anxiety disorder: GAD worries are about real-life concerns (work, health, finances) and lack the ritualised neutralising compulsions of OCD. OCD versus body dysmorphic disorder: both involve intrusive preoccupation and checking, but BDD's content is fixed on perceived ugliness of appearance. OCD versus hoarding: classic OCD discarding difficulty is driven by an obsession (contamination fear), whereas hoarding disorder reflects a perceived need to save and distress at parting, without a triggering obsession. Trichotillomania and excoriation versus OCD: the body-focused repetitive behaviours are not preceded by intrusive obsessional thoughts; the pulling or picking is its own habit-driven act, which is why they sit as a distinct sub-grouping.

Memory aid

Separate the four "related" disorders by their target: BDD targets appearance, hoarding targets possessions, trichotillomania targets hair, excoriation targets skin. Hair plus skin are the two body-focused repetitive behaviours. Treatment hook: "ERP plus SSRI" is the OCD answer, and the SSRI dose runs higher than in depression.

High-yield

OCD time gate = more than 1 hour per day (or marked distress/impairment). Insight specifiers run good/fair, poor, and absent/delusional, and even the delusional level stays in the OCD chapter (it does not become schizophrenia). The shared neurocircuitry of the whole chapter is the cortico-striato-thalamo-cortical (CSTC) loop. Trichotillomania and excoriation are the two body-focused repetitive behaviours; hoarding is the free-standing DSM-5 newcomer.

G. Trauma- & Stressor-Related Disorders

This chapter is set apart from every other in the manual by a single structural feature: a defined external stressor is written into the diagnostic criteria as the necessary precipitant. In the anxiety and mood chapters the cause is left open; here the disorder cannot be diagnosed at all unless an identifiable event preceded it. DSM-5 created this chapter de novo (the reactions previously sat among the anxiety disorders), and ICD-11 made the parallel move with its Disorders specifically associated with stress grouping. The whole chapter is then organised by a single axis the examiners exploit relentlessly: the clock. Acute stress disorder, PTSD, and adjustment disorder share much of their symptom universe and are separated chiefly by onset and duration gates. Learn those gates verbatim, because the distractor options are simply the same disorder with the wrong number of days attached.

Definition

Post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) follows exposure to actual or threatened death, serious injury, or sexual violence, sustained in one of four qualifying ways: directly experiencing it, witnessing it in person, learning that it happened to a close family member or friend (the event being violent or accidental), or experiencing repeated or extreme exposure to aversive details (the occupational route, e.g. first responders, abuse investigators). The symptoms fall into 4 clusters and must persist for more than 1 month with clinically significant distress or impairment.

The Criterion A stressor and the four symptom clusters

The qualifying stressor (Criterion A) is deliberately narrow: it must involve death, serious injury, or sexual violence. Ordinary severe stressors that do not cross this threshold (divorce, job loss, a non-violent bereavement, a serious but non-life-threatening illness) do not qualify for PTSD; they route to adjustment disorder instead. Importantly, learning of an event qualifies only if it happened to a close other and was violent or accidental; reading about a disaster in the news, or routine media exposure for non-work reasons, is explicitly excluded. The four symptom clusters, each with its minimum count, are:

ClusterMinimum symptomsContent
B. Intrusion (re-experiencing)1 of 5Intrusive involuntary memories, recurrent distressing dreams, dissociative flashbacks (acting/feeling as if the event were recurring), intense psychological distress at internal or external cues, marked physiological reactivity to cues.
C. Avoidance1 of 2Persistent effortful avoidance of trauma-related thoughts/feelings, or of external reminders (people, places, conversations, objects, situations).
D. Negative alterations in cognition and mood2 of 7Dissociative amnesia for the event, persistent exaggerated negative beliefs about self/world, distorted blame of self or others, persistent negative emotional state, markedly diminished interest (anhedonia), detachment/estrangement, persistent inability to feel positive emotions.
E. Alterations in arousal and reactivity2 of 6Irritable behaviour/angry outbursts, reckless or self-destructive behaviour, hypervigilance, exaggerated startle response, concentration problems, sleep disturbance.

The full diagnosis therefore requires the qualifying stressor (A) plus at least one intrusion symptom (B), at least one avoidance symptom (C), at least two negative-cognition/mood symptoms (D), and at least two arousal symptoms (E), all lasting more than one month (Criterion F) with distress/impairment (G) not attributable to a substance or medical condition (H). DSM-5 also defines two specifiers: with dissociative symptoms (depersonalisation or derealisation), and with delayed expression, applied when the full criteria are not met until at least six months after the event. A separate algorithm exists for PTSD in children 6 years and younger, with developmentally adjusted, lower thresholds.

The duration ladder: acute stress disorder, PTSD, adjustment disorder

DisorderOnset / duration (verbatim)Stressor required
Acute stress disorderSymptoms lasting from 3 days to 1 month after trauma exposure (if they persist beyond 1 month, re-diagnose as PTSD)Criterion-A trauma (death, serious injury, sexual violence)
PTSDSymptoms (4 clusters) persisting for more than 1 monthCriterion-A trauma
Adjustment disorderSymptoms emerge within 3 months of the stressor and resolve within 6 months once the stressor or its consequences endAny identifiable stressor (need not be traumatic)

Acute stress disorder (ASD) and PTSD share the symptom universe; the distinction is purely temporal. Within the first month after exposure the diagnosis is acute stress disorder (it cannot be made before day 3, which carves out the immediate normal shock reaction); from one month onward it becomes PTSD. ASD's criteria require 9 or more symptoms of 14 drawn across five categories (intrusion, negative mood, dissociation, avoidance, arousal), pooled rather than split into the four PTSD clusters, which is itself a tested contrast. Adjustment disorder is the residual stressor-linked diagnosis: clinically significant emotional or behavioural symptoms, out of proportion to an identifiable but ordinary stressor (a stressor that need not meet the Criterion-A bar), beginning within three months of onset and remitting within six months of the stressor or its consequences ending. If the reaction meets criteria for another specific disorder (a full depressive episode, say), that disorder is diagnosed instead; adjustment disorder is what remains when the reaction is real, impairing, but sub-threshold for anything more specific.

Reactive attachment disorder versus disinhibited social engagement disorder

Two childhood disorders in this chapter share an identical aetiology, a pattern of grossly insufficient care (social neglect, repeated changes of primary caregiver that prevent stable attachment, or rearing in settings such as institutions that severely limit selective attachment), yet present as behavioural opposites. Reactive attachment disorder (RAD) is the internalising pathway: a consistently inhibited, emotionally withdrawn child who rarely seeks comfort when distressed and rarely responds to it, with minimal social and emotional responsiveness, limited positive affect, and unexplained irritability or fearfulness. Disinhibited social engagement disorder (DSED) is the externalising pathway: a child who is overly familiar with relative strangers, reduced or absent reticence in approaching unfamiliar adults, willingness to wander off with a near-stranger, showing no normal stranger wariness. Both require a developmental age of at least 9 months (so an attachment can be expected to have formed).

ICD-11 contrast: complex PTSD and prolonged grief

ICD-11 reshaped this domain in three high-yield ways. First, it formally added Complex PTSD (CPTSD) as a separate sibling of PTSD. CPTSD requires the three core PTSD elements (re-experiencing in the here-and-now, avoidance, persistent sense of current threat) plus three additional disturbances in self-organisation (DSO): affect dysregulation, persistent negative self-concept (worthlessness, shame, guilt), and disturbances in relationships (difficulty feeling close to others). It typically follows prolonged, repeated, inescapable trauma (childhood abuse, captivity, torture, domestic violence). DSM-5 has no separate complex-PTSD category; it folds such cases into PTSD, optionally with the dissociative specifier. Second, ICD-11 added prolonged grief disorder (persistent, pervasive longing or preoccupation with the deceased beyond the expected social or cultural norm, at least six months after the loss); DSM-5-TR later added its own parallel prolonged grief disorder. Third, ICD-11 reframes the immediate stress reaction itself, acute stress reaction, as a normal reaction coded among factors influencing health (a Z-code), not as a mental disorder at all, which is a favourite trap.

High-yield

Memorise the clock: acute stress disorder = 3 days to 1 month; PTSD = more than 1 month; adjustment disorder = onset within 3 months, resolves within 6 months. PTSD has 4 symptom clusters (intrusion, avoidance, negative cognition/mood, arousal); the Criterion-A stressor must involve death, serious injury, or sexual violence. ICD-11 alone adds Complex PTSD (PTSD triad plus the three disturbances in self-organisation).

Exam trap

RAD vs. DSED: same pathogenic care, opposite behaviour. RAD = withdrawn, inhibited, does not seek or respond to comfort (internalising); DSED = disinhibited, over-friendly, approaches and wanders off with strangers (externalising). The "child who wanders off with a stranger without checking back" is DSED. Second trap: a severe but non-Criterion-A stressor (divorce, redundancy) producing impairing symptoms is adjustment disorder, never PTSD, because the trauma gate is not crossed.

Memory aid

The PTSD clusters spell a rough "I Am Nervous And Reactive": Intrusion, Avoidance, Negative cognition/mood, Arousal/Reactivity. For the clock, ASD is the short fuse (under a month, starting day 3) and PTSD is the long burn (over a month).

H. Dissociative & Somatic Symptom Disorders

Two families are taught together here because the examiners pair them and because both turn on a deceptively simple discriminator. Dissociative disorders involve a disruption of the normal integration of consciousness, memory, identity, emotion, perception, body representation, or behaviour. Somatic symptom and related disorders involve prominent physical symptoms or health concerns producing distress and impairment. The pivotal DSM-5 reform across the somatic group was the abandonment of "medically unexplained symptoms" as the organising idea: a clinician can no longer diagnose simply because a cause was not found. The new criteria are positive psychological ones (disproportionate thoughts, feelings, and behaviours about the symptom), so a patient with a genuine medical illness can still carry the diagnosis if the psychological response is excessive.

DisorderCore criterion (verbatim where dated)Key discriminator
Dissociative identity disorder (DID)Disruption of identity with at least 2 distinct personality states plus recurrent gaps in recall (amnesia) for everyday events, personal information, or traumaTwo-or-more identity states plus amnesia; rooted in severe early trauma
Dissociative amnesiaInability to recall important autobiographical (usually traumatic/stressful) information; localised, selective, or generalised; specifier with dissociative fugueMemory loss too extensive for ordinary forgetting; no alternate identities
Depersonalisation / derealisation disorderPersistent or recurrent unreality/detachment from one's own mind/body (depersonalisation) or surroundings (derealisation), with intact reality testingPreserved insight keeps it out of the psychotic spectrum
Somatic symptom disorderAt least 1 distressing somatic symptom PLUS disproportionate, persistent thoughts/feelings/behaviours about it, lasting at least 6 monthsSymptom is present; response is excessive
Illness anxiety disorderPreoccupation with having/acquiring a serious illness; somatic symptoms absent or mild; high health anxiety for at least 6 monthsWorry without the symptom (formerly hypochondriasis)
Conversion disorder (FND)Neurological symptom (motor/sensory) showing clinical incompatibility with recognised neurological/medical diseasePositive incompatibility signs, not mere absence of cause

The dissociative disorders

Dissociative identity disorder (DID), historically multiple personality disorder, hinges on the coexistence of two or more distinct personality states (in some cultures described as an experience of possession), bringing marked discontinuity in sense of self and agency together with alterations in affect, behaviour, consciousness, memory, perception, and cognition. The recurrent amnesia (gaps in recall for everyday events, important personal information, or traumatic events) is a required, not optional, feature; it is strongly associated with severe, repeated childhood trauma. Dissociative amnesia is an inability to recall important autobiographical information, typically of a traumatic or stressful nature, that is too extensive to be ordinary forgetfulness; it may be localised (a circumscribed period), selective (parts of a period), or generalised (whole identity and life history, a rare and dramatic presentation). Its dissociative fugue specifier adds apparently purposeful travel or bewildered wandering associated with the amnesia. Depersonalisation/derealisation disorder is defined by what it does not abolish: insight. The person feels detached from, or an outside observer of, their own thoughts and body (depersonalisation) or experiences the world as unreal, dreamlike, or visually distorted (derealisation), yet knows these are subjective experiences. That preserved reality testing is precisely what separates it from psychosis.

The somatic symptom disorders

Somatic symptom disorder requires one or more genuinely distressing somatic symptoms plus excessive thoughts, feelings, or behaviours about them (disproportionate persistent thoughts about seriousness, high health anxiety, or excessive time and energy devoted to the symptoms) for at least six months; whether the symptom is medically explained is irrelevant. Illness anxiety disorder is the worry stripped of the symptom: preoccupation with having or acquiring a serious illness, with somatic symptoms absent or only mild, and a high level of health anxiety for at least six months (this absorbed most of the old hypochondriasis diagnosis). Conversion disorder / functional neurological symptom disorder presents with altered voluntary motor or sensory function (weakness, paralysis, abnormal movements, non-epileptic seizures, sensory loss, blindness) where examination demonstrates positive incompatibility with recognised neurology, for example Hoover's sign (hip-extension weakness that resolves on contralateral hip flexion) or tremor entrainment. The diagnosis rests on that demonstrated internal inconsistency, not on the mere failure to find a lesion.

Factitious disorder versus malingering

Factitious disorder is the deliberate falsification or induction of physical or psychological signs of illness in oneself (or, in factitious disorder imposed on another, formerly Munchausen syndrome by proxy, in a victim) with deceptive intent, but in the absence of obvious external rewards; the motive is the internal psychological gain of occupying the sick role. Malingering is the conscious fabrication or exaggeration of symptoms for explicit external (secondary) gain: avoiding work or military duty, evading criminal prosecution, obtaining money, drugs, or disability benefits. Malingering is not a mental disorder at all but a V/Z-code condition that may be a focus of clinical attention.

Worked vignette

A 34-year-old man is brought to A&E unable to move his right leg after a minor car accident in which a passenger died; neurological examination shows give-way weakness, a positive Hoover's sign, and intact reflexes, with normal imaging. He is bewildered and frightened by the deficit and seeks no compensation. Reason it through: the symptom is genuinely not under voluntary control (no conscious production), the examination shows positive incompatibility with neurology (Hoover's sign), and there is no external reward sought. This is conversion disorder (FND), not malingering (no secondary gain, not consciously produced) and not factitious disorder (not deliberately induced). Had the same man been deliberately faking the paralysis to win an injury claim, it would be malingering; had he induced it for the role of patient with no external payoff, factitious disorder.

Definition

Dissociation is a partial or complete disruption of the normal integration of memory, identity, consciousness, perception, or behaviour. Somatisation is the expression of psychological distress through bodily symptoms and health concerns. The first fragments the mind's integration; the second routes distress into the body.

High-yield

The single discriminator between factitious disorder and malingering is the presence of external (secondary) gain: malingering is conscious deception for an external reward; factitious disorder is conscious deception with no external reward, only the internal need to be sick. Conversion disorder differs from both: the symptom is not consciously produced. Depersonalisation/derealisation disorder is the dissociative disorder defined by intact reality testing.

Exam trap

DID vs. dissociative amnesia: both feature amnesia, but only DID has the two-or-more distinct identity states. Amnesia plus alternate identities is DID; amnesia alone (even with a fugue) is dissociative amnesia. Second trap: somatic symptom disorder requires a real, present somatic symptom with an excessive response; illness anxiety disorder is the one with somatic symptoms absent or minimal. Do not pick illness anxiety disorder for a patient who has a distressing symptom.

I. Feeding, Eating & Sleep-Wake Disorders

Two short but number-dense chapters are taught together. The eating disorders are separated almost entirely by weight and by the frequency-and-duration of the binge-or-compensation cycle; the sleep-wake disorders the examiners favour are gated by the 3-times-per-week-for-3-months rule that recurs across the group. Memorise the numbers first, because the prose merely explains what the numbers already decide.

DisorderCriteria and duration (verbatim)Differential anchor
Anorexia nervosaRestriction of energy intake leading to significantly low body weight; intense fear of gaining weight or becoming fat; disturbance in body-image / denial of seriousness of low weight. Subtypes: restricting vs. binge-eating/purging (last 3 months)Defined by low weight
Bulimia nervosaRecurrent binge eating PLUS recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviours (self-induced vomiting, laxatives, diuretics, fasting, excessive exercise), both at least once a week for 3 months; self-evaluation unduly influenced by shape and weightNormal or above-normal weight
Binge-eating disorderRecurrent binges (eating an objectively large amount with loss of control) with marked distress, at least once a week for 3 months, in the absence of compensatory behavioursBinge without purge
Insomnia disorderDissatisfaction with sleep quantity/quality (difficulty initiating, maintaining, or early-morning waking) at least 3 nights per week for 3 months, despite adequate opportunity to sleepDifficulty getting sleep
Hypersomnolence disorderExcessive sleepiness despite a main sleep period of at least 7 hours, at least 3 times per week for 3 monthsToo much sleep, no orexin loss
NarcolepsyRecurrent irrepressible need to sleep / lapses into sleep within the same day, at least 3 times per week over the past 3 months, with cataplexy and/or hypocretin (orexin) deficiency on CSF assay or characteristic REM findingsSleep attacks plus cataplexy / orexin loss

The eating disorders

Anorexia nervosa is defined by three things together: persistent restriction of energy intake leading to a significantly low body weight (in the context of age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health), an intense fear of gaining weight or persistent behaviour that interferes with weight gain even though already underweight, and a disturbance in the way body weight or shape is experienced (or denial of the seriousness of the low weight). Its two subtypes are reckoned over the last three months: the restricting type achieves weight loss through dieting, fasting, or excessive exercise without regular bingeing or purging, while the binge-eating/purging type involves recurrent bingeing or purging episodes. Bulimia nervosa is the cycle of recurrent binge eating (a discrete period of eating an objectively large amount with a sense of loss of control) followed by recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviour to prevent weight gain, both occurring at least once a week for three months, with self-evaluation unduly tied to shape and weight; sufferers are characteristically at normal or above-normal weight. Binge-eating disorder shares the recurrent binges and the once-a-week-for-three-months frequency but has no compensatory behaviour, and the binges are marked by associated features (eating rapidly, until uncomfortably full, when not hungry, alone from embarrassment, with subsequent guilt).

The sleep-wake disorders

Insomnia disorder is dissatisfaction with sleep quantity or quality marked by difficulty initiating sleep, maintaining sleep, or early-morning awakening, present at least three nights per week for at least three months despite adequate opportunity. Hypersomnolence disorder is its mirror, excessive sleepiness despite a main sleep period of at least seven hours, with recurrent lapses, prolonged unrefreshing sleep, or difficulty being fully awake, again at least three times a week for three months. Narcolepsy couples recurrent irrepressible sleep attacks (at least three times a week over three months) with cataplexy (sudden, brief, bilateral loss of muscle tone with retained consciousness, classically triggered by strong emotion such as laughter) and/or a demonstrated deficiency of the hypothalamic neuropeptide hypocretin (orexin) in cerebrospinal fluid; sleep-onset REM periods and hypnagogic/hypnopompic hallucinations with sleep paralysis are associated features. ICD-11 classifies the sleep-wake disorders in a separate chapter of their own (not within the mental-and-behavioural-disorders block), reflecting their straddling of neurology and psychiatry, a point worth knowing for system-comparison items.

Worked vignette

An 18-year-old woman, BMI 15.5, is terrified of gaining weight and insists she looks fat despite obvious emaciation; she binges roughly twice a week and then vomits. Resist the reflex to call this bulimia just because she binges and purges. The decisive fact is her significantly low weight: she is at an anorexic weight, so the diagnosis is anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging subtype, not bulimia nervosa. Had her weight been normal or above, with the same binge-purge cycle at least weekly for three months, it would be bulimia. Weight, not the behaviour, makes the call.

Definition

A binge is eating, within a discrete period (e.g. two hours), an amount of food definitely larger than most people would eat in similar circumstances, accompanied by a sense of loss of control over the eating. The loss-of-control element, not the quantity alone, is what makes it a binge.

High-yield

Both bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder require binges at least once a week for 3 months; the difference is that bulimia has compensatory behaviour and binge-eating disorder does not. The sleep trio (insomnia, hypersomnolence, narcolepsy) all use 3 times/nights per week for 3 months. Narcolepsy is anchored by cataplexy and hypocretin/orexin deficiency.

Exam trap

Cataplexy vs. catalepsy vs. cataptlexy confusions: cataplexy (sudden emotion-triggered loss of muscle tone) belongs to narcolepsy, whereas catalepsy (passively induced, held posture) and waxy flexibility belong to catatonia. The narcolepsy item is the one that hangs on cataplexy and orexin loss. Second trap: anorexia binge-purge subtype and bulimia both binge and purge; the discriminator is body weight (low = anorexia, normal/high = bulimia), never the behaviour itself.

J. Personality Disorders (by Cluster)

A personality disorder is not a symptom that comes and goes but an enduring architecture of the self that warps experience and behaviour across situations. The chapter is examined in two layers: the general criteria that any personality disorder must satisfy, and the ten specific types sorted into three clusters, each type paired with the near-neighbour it is most often confused with. Master both layers, because the paper tests the general definition as readily as the individual labels.

Definition

The general DSM-5 criteria for a personality disorder require an enduring pattern of inner experience and behaviour that deviates markedly from cultural expectation, manifest in at least two of four areas (cognition, affectivity, interpersonal functioning, impulse control). The pattern must be pervasive and inflexible across a broad range of situations, stable and of long duration with onset traceable to adolescence or early adulthood, not better explained by another mental disorder, and not due to a substance or medical condition. It causes clinically significant distress or impairment. The traits are characteristically ego-syntonic (felt as part of the self, not as alien intrusions), which is why insight and help-seeking are often poor.

ClusterDisorderCore patternDifferentiate from
A (odd / eccentric)ParanoidPervasive distrust and suspiciousness; reads malice into others' motives; bears grudges; doubts loyaltyDelusional disorder persecutory type (fixed delusion vs. pervasive suspicion); schizoid (engaged but suspicious vs. indifferent)
SchizoidDetachment from social relationships and a restricted range of emotional expression; the genuine loner who neither desires nor enjoys closenessAvoidant (wants closeness but fears it); schizotypal (adds odd cognition/perception)
SchizotypalAcute discomfort in close relationships PLUS cognitive/perceptual distortions and eccentric behaviour (ideas of reference, magical thinking, odd speech, unusual perceptions)Schizophrenia (no frank psychosis); schizoid (schizoid lacks the cognitive/perceptual oddities)
B (dramatic / erratic)AntisocialPervasive disregard for and violation of others' rights since age 15; deceit, impulsivity, aggression, irresponsibility, lack of remorse; person must be at least 18, with evidence of conduct disorder before age 15Conduct disorder (the under-18 precursor); narcissistic (exploits for admiration, not criminal gain)
BorderlineInstability of relationships, self-image, and affect with marked impulsivity; frantic efforts to avoid abandonment; identity disturbance; recurrent self-harm/suicidality; chronic emptiness; defence of splittingBipolar disorder (BPD's mood shifts are rapid, reactive, and interpersonal, not sustained episodes); histrionic (less self-harm and emptiness)
HistrionicPervasive excessive emotionality and attention-seeking; theatrical, suggestible, sexually provocative, shallow shifting emotionBorderline (histrionic lacks self-destructiveness and identity collapse); narcissistic (seeks attention vs. admiration)
NarcissisticGrandiosity, need for admiration, lack of empathy; sense of entitlement, envy, exploitativeness, fragile self-esteemAntisocial (narcissist wants status not crime); histrionic (admiration vs. mere attention)
C (anxious / fearful)AvoidantSocial inhibition, feelings of inadequacy, hypersensitivity to negative evaluation; avoids interaction despite craving itSchizoid (avoidant desires closeness; schizoid does not); social anxiety disorder (a state vs. a pervasive trait)
DependentPervasive excessive need to be taken care of; submissive, clinging; difficulty making decisions or being alone; urgently seeks a new relationship when one endsBorderline (dependent fears abandonment with submission, not rage and self-harm)
Obsessive-compulsive (OCPD)Preoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism, and control at the expense of flexibility, openness, and efficiency; rigidity, miserliness, workaholismOCD (OCPD is ego-syntonic with no true obsessions/compulsions)

Reading the three clusters

Cluster A (odd/eccentric) shades toward the psychotic spectrum, and schizotypal personality disorder in particular is genetically linked to schizophrenia (it sits within the schizophrenia spectrum chapter as well). The internal differential is clean: paranoid is suspicion with social engagement; schizoid is genuine indifference to relationships without the cognitive distortions; schizotypal adds the magical thinking, ideas of reference, and perceptual aberrations that schizoid lacks. Cluster B (dramatic/erratic) is the emotionally dysregulated group. Antisocial personality disorder carries hard age gates: it is diagnosed only from age 18, and there must be documented conduct disorder with onset before age 15; under 18 the diagnosis is conduct disorder, which ties this chapter back to the neurodevelopmental one. Borderline's signature defence is splitting, the inability to hold the good and bad qualities of self or others together, so people are idealised then abruptly devalued; its mood instability is rapid and reactive to interpersonal triggers, which is the key separator from the sustained episodes of bipolar disorder. Cluster C (anxious/fearful) is governed by fear: the crucial contrast is avoidant (craves closeness but is paralysed by fear of rejection) against schizoid from Cluster A (does not want closeness at all).

Worked vignette

A 26-year-old woman has stormy, intense relationships that swing from idealising a new partner as perfect to condemning them as worthless within days; she dreads being left, cuts herself when a friend cancels plans, and describes a chronic sense of emptiness and an unstable sense of who she is. The rapid idealise-then-devalue pattern is splitting; combined with abandonment fear, identity disturbance, affective instability, self-harm, and emptiness, this is borderline personality disorder. Note why it is not bipolar disorder: her mood swings are reactive to interpersonal events and last hours to days, not the sustained, autonomous week-long episodes of mania or the two-week blocks of depression.

ICD-11 contrast: the dimensional model

This is the headline system difference and a near-certain exam item. ICD-11 abolished the ten categorical types entirely. In their place a clinician first assigns a global severity (a sub-threshold "personality difficulty", then mild, moderate, or severe personality disorder, graded by harm to self and others and by pervasiveness), then optionally adds up to five trait domain qualifiers: negative affectivity, detachment, dissociality, disinhibition, and anankastia. A single borderline pattern qualifier was retained, largely to preserve treatment continuity and the evidence base for borderline-specific therapies. DSM-5 went only halfway: it kept all ten categorical types in the main text and parked an Alternative Model for Personality Disorders (AMPD) in Section III, which rates level of personality functioning plus five pathological trait domains (negative affectivity, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition, psychoticism). So both systems describe a dimensional model, but only ICD-11 made it the official, sole system.

High-yield

Antisocial personality disorder: conduct disorder before age 15, pattern since age 15, diagnosed only at age 18 or older. Splitting is the hallmark defence of borderline personality disorder. ICD-11 abolished the categorical types for a severity-plus-five-trait-qualifier model (negative affectivity, detachment, dissociality, disinhibition, anankastia, with an optional borderline pattern). The general criteria require deviation in at least 2 of 4 areas, onset by adolescence/early adulthood.

Exam trap

Avoidant vs. schizoid: both are socially withdrawn, but the avoidant person desires relationships and avoids them from fear of rejection (Cluster C), while the schizoid person is genuinely indifferent to them (Cluster A). Desire-but-afraid = avoidant; doesn't-care = schizoid. Second trap: OCPD is ego-syntonic and has no true obsessions or compulsions, unlike OCD, which is ego-dystonic and ritual-driven. Third: do not say DSM-5 "removed" the ten types; it kept them and added the alternative model. Only ICD-11 abolished the categories.

Memory aid

Cluster labels: A = "weird" (odd/eccentric, the Mad), B = "wild" (dramatic/erratic, the Bad), C = "worried" (anxious/fearful, the Sad). The ICD-11 five traits spell a rough "DDDNA": Detachment, Dissociality, Disinhibition, Negative affectivity, Anankastia.

K. Neurodevelopmental & Childhood Disorders

The final chapter covers disorders that onset in the developmental period and are defined by deviation from expected developmental milestones rather than by a decline from a previously normal baseline. The examiners drill the exact thresholds: the symptom counts and age gates of ADHD, the dual-domain logic of autism and intellectual disability, and the ODD-to-conduct-disorder gradient that ultimately feeds antisocial personality disorder. Learn the numbers as a block; they are the spine of the chapter.

Intellectual disability and specific learning disorder

Intellectual disability (intellectual developmental disorder) requires both of two deficits, with onset in the developmental period: (a) deficits in intellectual functions (reasoning, problem-solving, planning, abstract thinking, judgement, academic and experiential learning) confirmed by both clinical assessment and individualised, standardised intelligence testing; and (b) deficits in adaptive functioning that result in failure to meet developmental and socio-cultural standards for personal independence and social responsibility, across the three domains: conceptual (language, reading, money, time), social (empathy, interpersonal skills, judgement), and practical (self-care, job, money management). The decisive DSM-5 reform is that severity (mild, moderate, severe, profound) is set by adaptive functioning, not by IQ band alone, because adaptive functioning determines the level of support actually needed. Specific learning disorder is the circumscribed cousin: persistent difficulty (at least six months despite intervention) in a specific academic skill, with the skill substantially below that expected for age, specified as with impairment in reading (dyslexia), in written expression, or in mathematics (dyscalculia), and not explained by intellectual disability or sensory deficit.

Autism spectrum disorder

Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) also requires both of two domains, present from early development: (a) persistent deficits in social communication and social interaction across multiple contexts, demonstrated by deficits in all three of social-emotional reciprocity, nonverbal communicative behaviours, and developing/maintaining/understanding relationships; and (b) restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour, interests, or activities, shown by at least two of four: stereotyped/repetitive movements or speech, insistence on sameness and inflexible routines, highly fixated intense interests, and hyper- or hypo-reactivity to sensory input. Symptoms must be present in the early developmental period (though they may not fully manifest until social demands exceed capacity), and severity is rated 1 to 3 by the level of support required. DSM-5 collapsed the older separate labels (autistic disorder, Asperger's disorder, childhood disintegrative disorder, PDD-NOS) into the single spectrum. ICD-11 likewise uses a unified autism spectrum disorder with qualifiers for intellectual and language impairment, a point of convergence between the systems.

ADHD: the exact symptom counts and age gates

Definition

Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) requires a persistent pattern of inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity that interferes with functioning or development. The symptom threshold is at least 6 symptoms from a domain (inattention, or hyperactivity-impulsivity), reduced to at least 5 symptoms for those aged 17 or older, persisting for at least 6 months, with several symptoms present before age 12, and present in at least 2 settings (e.g. home and school). Presentations: predominantly inattentive, predominantly hyperactive-impulsive, or combined.

DisorderCriteria and duration (verbatim)
ADHDAt least 6 symptoms (5 if age 17 or older) of inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity, for at least 6 months, with several symptoms present before age 12, across at least 2 settings
Oppositional defiant disorder (ODD)Angry/irritable mood, argumentative/defiant behaviour, or vindictiveness (at least 4 symptoms across the categories), lasting at least 6 months, with at least one person who is not a sibling
Conduct disorder (CD)Repetitive, persistent violation of others' rights or major age-appropriate norms across 4 clusters, with at least 3 symptoms in the past 12 months (and at least 1 in the past 6 months); childhood-onset (before age 10) vs. adolescent-onset specifier

The disruptive-behaviour gradient: ODD, conduct disorder, antisocial personality

The disruptive disorders form a developmental escalation that the examiners love to test as a sequence. Oppositional defiant disorder (ODD) is the milder, earlier pattern: a recurrent pattern of angry/irritable mood, argumentative/defiant behaviour, or vindictiveness lasting at least six months, directed mainly at authority figures, without the serious rights-violations of conduct disorder. Conduct disorder (CD) is graver: a repetitive and persistent pattern that violates the basic rights of others or major societal norms, across four symptom clusters, namely (1) aggression to people and animals, (2) destruction of property, (3) deceitfulness or theft, and (4) serious violations of rules (truancy, running away), with at least three symptoms in the past twelve months. Its childhood-onset subtype (at least one symptom before age 10) carries a worse prognosis. Conduct disorder before age 15 is the documented precursor required for the later diagnosis of antisocial personality disorder at age 18 or over, which is exactly how the neurodevelopmental and personality chapters interlock. The depressive chapter's disruptive mood dysregulation disorder (DMDD) also sits at this junction: chronic, persistent irritability with severe outbursts at least three times a week for at least twelve months, onset before age 10, introduced to curb the over-diagnosis of paediatric bipolar disorder.

Worked vignette

A 9-year-old boy is described by his teacher as unable to stay seated, blurting answers, losing his pencils and worksheets, and unable to follow multi-step instructions; the same pattern is reported at home and has been present for over a year. Apply the gates: at least six symptoms across inattention and hyperactivity-impulsivity, lasting more than six months, onset before age 12, present in at least two settings (home and school). This is ADHD, combined presentation. Now contrast a different 9-year-old who is not merely restless but deliberately cruel to the family cat, has set a fire, shoplifts, and stays out all night: that crosses into violating others' rights and major norms across the four clusters, so it is conduct disorder, not ADHD, although the two frequently co-occur.

High-yield

Pin the numbers: ADHD = at least 6 symptoms (5 if 17 or older), at least 6 months, several symptoms before age 12, at least 2 settings. Conduct disorder = 4 clusters, at least 3 symptoms in 12 months. ODD = at least 6 months. Intellectual disability = three adaptive domains (conceptual, social, practical), severity set by adaptive function not IQ band. ASD = social-communication deficits (all 3) and restricted/repetitive behaviours (at least 2 of 4), early-developmental onset. DMDD = outbursts at least 3 times/week for at least 12 months, onset before age 10.

Exam trap

ODD vs. conduct disorder: ODD is defiance, anger, and argumentativeness without serious aggression, property destruction, theft, or rule violations; conduct disorder crosses into violating others' rights across its four clusters. The "child who is argumentative and loses his temper at his parents" is ODD; the "child who is cruel to animals, sets fires, and steals" is conduct disorder. Second trap: ADHD's age gate is symptoms before age 12 (not before age 7, the old DSM-IV figure, which is the classic distractor), and the symptom count drops from 6 to 5 only at age 17 and over.

Memory aid

Conduct disorder's four clusters spell a rough "Animals, Arson, And Absconding plus theft": Aggression to people/animals, destruction of property (Arson is the vivid exemplar), deceitfulness/theft, serious rule violations (Absconding/truancy). For ADHD remember "6, 6, 12, 2": 6 symptoms, 6 months, before 12, in 2 settings.

4 · Psychological Assessment & Psychodiagnostics

Psychological testing is the operational backbone of clinical psychology: it converts vague referral questions ("is this child gifted?", "is this an organic dementia or a depressive pseudodementia?", "is this patient psychotic or malingering?") into reproducible numbers and structured behaviour samples. This section teaches the instruments that dominate the M.Phil paper, beginning with the family that quantifies general mental ability and then localises deficits to brain systems. For every test you must hold four things in your head at once: what it measures, how it is structured (the exact subtests, indices and norms), how it is scored and interpreted, and the named author and year behind it. The examiner's favourite distractors live precisely where two of these slip out of alignment: an index mistaken for the full-scale score, a subtest filed under the wrong index, a ratio IQ confused with a deviation IQ, a fixed battery confused with a flexible one, or the wrong theorist attached to the wrong battery. The whole craft of the assessment paper is telling a test apart from its near-neighbour, so each instrument below is taught from the floor up and then explicitly contrasted with the one it is most often confused with. Read each until you can reproduce its architecture, its numbers and its trap from memory.

A. The Wechsler Intelligence Matrix (WAIS-IV / WISC-V)

David Wechsler built the dominant family of individually administered intelligence tests, defining intelligence as "the global capacity of the individual to act purposefully, to think rationally and to deal effectively with his environment." That definition matters because it is global and aggregative: Wechsler treated intelligence as a single overall capacity expressed through many specific abilities, which is why his tests yield one summary IQ resting on top of several narrower scores. His central departure from the original Stanford-Binet was to abandon the mental-age ratio IQ and adopt the deviation IQ: a person's score is expressed as a standard score with a population mean of 100 and a standard deviation of 15, so an IQ simply states how many standard deviations the examinee sits above or below same-age peers. The adult form is the Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale, fourth edition (WAIS-IV, 2008), for ages 16 to 90; the child form is the Wechsler Intelligence Scale for Children, fifth edition (WISC-V, 2014), for ages 6 to 16; the preschool form is the WPPSI (ages 2 years 6 months to 7 years 7 months), a third member of the family that the paper sometimes drops in as a date-or-age distractor. Earlier Wechsler scales reported a Verbal IQ and a Performance IQ; the modern editions have abandoned that two-IQ split in favour of a single Full Scale IQ (FSIQ) sitting on top of four (WAIS-IV) or five (WISC-V) index scores.

Definition

The Full Scale IQ (FSIQ) is the single best estimate of general intellectual ability (Spearman's g), derived from the core subtests across all index domains. Each subtest yields a scaled score (mean 10, standard deviation 3); the index scores and the FSIQ are composites rescaled to mean 100, standard deviation 15. The two metrics are different scales: a "12" is a subtest scaled score (above average), while a "112" is a composite/IQ. Confusing the two metrics is a standard slip.

Definition

A deviation IQ expresses ability as a standard score relative to the same-age peer group (mean 100, standard deviation 15), so it stays meaningful across the lifespan. The older ratio IQ (mental age divided by chronological age, times 100) breaks down in adulthood because mental age plateaus while chronological age keeps rising, artificially deflating adult scores. Wechsler's adoption of the deviation IQ was the decisive methodological reform of the field.

Classification bands

The deviation metric maps onto standard descriptive bands, and the examiner expects you to attach the right label to the right range. Because the standard deviation is 15, each band is roughly one standard deviation wide.

FSIQ rangeClassification (WAIS-IV terminology)Approx. percentile / population
130 and aboveVery SuperiorTop 2.2% (about +2 SD)
120 to 129SuperiorAbout 6.7%
110 to 119High AverageAbout 16.1%
90 to 109AverageThe middle 50% (mean 100)
80 to 89Low AverageAbout 16.1%
70 to 79BorderlineAbout 6.7%
69 and belowExtremely Low (formerly "Intellectually Deficient")Bottom 2.2% (about −2 SD); the IQ criterion for intellectual disability

The four core structural indices and their subtests

The architecture of the WAIS-IV is a four-index structure. Memorise both the index names and exactly which subtests load on each, because the paper loves to mis-sort a subtest (for example, planting Arithmetic under the verbal index when it actually loads on working memory, or planting Block Design under processing speed when it loads on perceptual reasoning).

IndexWhat it measuresCore subtestsSupplemental subtests
Verbal Comprehension Index (VCI)Acquired verbal knowledge, verbal concept formation, crystallised intelligenceSimilarities, Vocabulary, InformationComprehension
Perceptual Reasoning Index (PRI)Fluid reasoning, non-verbal problem solving, visual-spatial processingBlock Design, Matrix Reasoning, Visual PuzzlesFigure Weights, Picture Completion
Working Memory Index (WMI)Attention, concentration, mental manipulation of information held in mindDigit Span, ArithmeticLetter-Number Sequencing
Processing Speed Index (PSI)Speed of visual scanning, graphomotor speed, mental quickness on simple tasksSymbol Search, CodingCancellation

Take each in turn. In the Verbal Comprehension Index, Similarities asks how two concepts are alike (in what way are a dog and a lion alike?) and taps abstract verbal reasoning; Vocabulary asks for word definitions and is the single best correlate of overall FSIQ and the most resistant to decline (a classic "hold" test, used to estimate premorbid ability); Information samples general fund of acquired knowledge; the supplemental Comprehension probes practical reasoning and understanding of social conventions. In the Perceptual Reasoning Index, Block Design requires the examinee to reproduce a printed geometric pattern using red-and-white blocks under time pressure (it is exquisitely sensitive to right-parietal and constructional dysfunction); Matrix Reasoning presents an incomplete visual pattern matrix to be completed from options (a near-pure fluid-reasoning, g-loaded task); Visual Puzzles asks which three pieces combine to make a target shape (mental rotation and spatial analysis); supplemental Figure Weights uses balance-scale problems for quantitative-analogical reasoning, and Picture Completion asks what important part is missing from a picture. In the Working Memory Index, Digit Span has three parts (forwards, which is simple attention span; backwards, which adds mental manipulation; and sequencing, which requires reordering), Arithmetic presents mentally solved word problems under time pressure, and supplemental Letter-Number Sequencing requires reordering an intermixed string of letters and numbers. In the Processing Speed Index, Symbol Search requires scanning to decide whether target symbols appear in a search group, Coding requires writing the symbol paired with each digit using a key (sensitive to attention, motor speed and incidental learning), and supplemental Cancellation requires marking target shapes among distractors.

Memory aid

For the four indices use "V P W P" = Verbal, Perceptual, Working memory, Processing speed. For VCI core trio: "SVI" (Similarities, Vocabulary, Information). For PRI core trio: "BMV" (Block Design, Matrix Reasoning, Visual Puzzles). For PSI: Symbol Search and Coding are the two pencil-speed tasks. Arithmetic sounds verbal but lives in Working memory; Digit Span is its partner there.

Administration and scoring logic

The Wechsler scales are individually administered (one examiner, one examinee, roughly 60 to 90 minutes), not group paper-and-pencil tests, which is part of their clinical value: the examiner observes how the person solves problems, not only the final score. Most subtests use basal and ceiling rules to keep testing efficient. The basal is a run of consecutive correct items establishing that easier items below would also be passed (so they are credited without administration); the ceiling is a run of consecutive failures establishing that harder items above would also be failed (so testing stops). Raw scores within each subtest are first converted to age-scaled scaled scores (mean 10, SD 3) using the norms table for the examinee's age band; the relevant scaled scores are then summed and converted to each index composite and to the FSIQ (mean 100, SD 15). The clinical reading then proceeds from the top down: first the FSIQ as a global estimate, then the four indices, then, crucially, the scatter between them.

Clinical interpretation: index discrepancies and profile analysis

The single most clinically useful output is not the FSIQ but the pattern of index discrepancies, because a meaningful gap between indices localises where the difficulty lies. When the four indices differ widely the FSIQ becomes an average of unlike things and is itself less interpretable; the clinician then interprets at the index level. A large VCI greater than PRI split suggests verbal-crystallised strengths over fluid-perceptual ability (and is sometimes seen with right-hemisphere or visuo-spatial difficulty), while PRI greater than VCI suggests the reverse (and can accompany language-based learning difficulty). A selectively depressed WMI or PSI against intact VCI and PRI is the classic signature of attentional disorders, anxiety, depression and ADHD, because working memory and processing speed are the functions most vulnerable to these states. Profile (ipsative) analysis then compares each subtest scaled score against the person's own mean to find genuine strengths and weaknesses; the examiner expects you to know that this is interpreted cautiously, since subtest scores are less reliable than composites and chance scatter is common.

General Ability Index versus Cognitive Proficiency Index

This is the highest-yield conceptual point in the whole Wechsler topic. Beyond the FSIQ, the WAIS-IV provides two ancillary composites that repartition the same subtests in a clinically meaningful way.

Definition

The General Ability Index (GAI) is computed from VCI + PRI only. It deliberately extracts the higher-order reasoning component (verbal and perceptual reasoning) while removing the contributions of working memory and processing speed. The Cognitive Proficiency Index (CPI) is computed from WMI + PSI only: it isolates the efficiency machinery (how quickly and reliably the brain manipulates and processes information).

The clinical logic: working memory and processing speed are the cognitive functions most vulnerable to attentional disorders, anxiety, depression, traumatic brain injury and ADHD.
