  • nAff (need for Affiliation): the drive to form and maintain friendly, warm relationships and to be close to others.
  • nAgg (need for Aggression): the drive to overcome opposition forcefully, to attack, injure or oppose.
  • nPow (need for Power, Dominance): the drive to control, influence or direct others.
  • nAut (need for Autonomy) and nNur (need for Nurturance): the drives to be independent of constraint and to support/protect others respectively.

The clinician scores which needs appear, how strongly (their intensity, duration and frequency across cards), and in which combinations, in the hero's behaviour. The need-achievement scoring system developed by David McClelland and John Atkinson turned this into a quantified research tool for measuring achievement motivation.

External Press

A press (p) is an environmental force that facilitates or obstructs the hero's pursuit of a need: what the world does to the hero (a hostile authority, a loss, an opportunity, an obstacle). Murray drew a crucial distinction between two kinds of press:

Definition

Alpha Press is the objective environment as it actually is: the real, quantifiable situational forces that an outside observer would agree are present. Beta Press is the subjective environment as the hero perceives and interprets it: the personal, often distorted construal of what the situation means. The gap between alpha and beta press (objective reality versus subjective interpretation) is itself diagnostic, because a wide divergence reveals how the person misreads or colours their world.

The Thema

The Thema is the overarching plot or behavioural unit that emerges from the interaction of the hero's needs with the environmental press: the recurring need-press-outcome pattern across the stories. A simple thema is one need meeting one press with one resolution; a complex thema interweaves several. Recurrent themas across many cards reveal the examinee's stable, characteristic conflicts and motivational style.

Worked example: across several cards a storyteller's hero is a young man straining to succeed (high nAch) who is repeatedly thwarted by a domineering father figure (a hostile beta press), and each story ends in defeat or surrender. The recurrent thema, high achievement need crushed by a perceived oppressive authority, suggests a person who strives but anticipates failure at the hands of authority, and the fact that the oppressive father is a beta press (the examinee's construal, since the cards do not specify it) tells the clinician this expectation is supplied from within.

Related apperceptive tests

The TAT spawned a family of apperceptive methods you should be able to name and contrast: the Children's Apperception Test (CAT, Leopold and Sonya Bellak) replaces the human figures with animals for ages roughly 3 to 10, on the assumption that young children project more readily onto animals; the Senior Apperception Test (SAT) adapts the method to the concerns of the elderly; and in India the Indian adaptations by Uma Chowdhury redrew the cards with Indian figures and settings to reduce cultural distance. Contrast all of these with the Rorschach: apperceptive tests sample thematic content and motivation through storytelling, whereas the Rorschach samples perceptual structure through inkblot responses.

High-yield

TAT = Murray and Morgan (1935), interpreted via Need-Press Theory (personology); 31 cards, about 20 used, one blank. Hero = the identified-with character; Need = internal drive (nAch achievement, nAff affiliation, nAgg aggression, nPow power); Press = external force (Alpha = objective reality, Beta = subjective perception); Thema = the recurrent plot born from the need-press collision. McClelland and Atkinson quantified nAch; the CAT (Bellak) uses animal figures for children.

Exam trap

Keep the TAT and Rorschach contributions straight: the TAT samples motivation and interpersonal themes via Murray's Need-Press theory, whereas the Rorschach samples perception, affect regulation and reality-testing via Exner's structural variables. On press, remember Alpha = objective / actual and Beta = subjective / perceived; the favourite distractor reverses them. The full set is 31 cards (about 20 used), not 10 (that is the Rorschach). The Hero is the character of identification, not necessarily the card's central figure if the storyteller clearly invests in someone else. The CAT uses animal figures (Bellak), not the WAIS; do not muddle the apperceptive family with intelligence tests.

Memory aid

"Alpha = Actual (objective), Beta = Belief (subjective)." Both start with the same first letters as their meaning. Hero feels Needs; world applies Press; the story they make together is the Thema. "Ror10schach has ten blots; the TAT tells 31-card stories."

5 · Psychotherapy & Clinical Interventions

Psychotherapy is the planned, systematic application of psychological principles to relieve distress and produce lasting change in how a person thinks, feels, behaves, and relates. For a clinical entrance examination this section is unusually high-value because the questions cluster around a small number of fixed facts: who originated a model and in what year, the single sentence that captures the model's theory of pathology (why the person is ill), the matching theory of change (why the person gets better), the named technique attached to it and exactly how that technique is carried out, the indication it is chosen for, and the look-alike model it is most often confused with. A candidate who can say that flooding is prolonged real-life exposure while implosion is imaginal exposure built on psychodynamic themes, or that Klein gave us the paranoid-schizoid position while Mahler gave us separation-individuation and Winnicott the good-enough mother, or that Beck restructures the content of thought while Hayes only changes one's relationship to thought, has already banked the marks that vaguer candidates lose. This section teaches each approach from its theory of pathology and theory of change upward, then the techniques that follow step by step from that theory, gives a worked clinical example, states how the topic surfaces in the paper, and always flags the exact distractor the paper will use. We move through the families in the order the syllabus sets them: the traditional behavioural and cognitive-behavioural therapies, the third-wave acceptance therapies, the psychoanalytic and humanistic-existential and systemic paradigms, and finally clinical ethics and the Indian mental-health legislation.

A. Traditional Behavioural Interventions

Behaviour therapy rests on a single claim: maladaptive behaviour is learned, and what is learned can be unlearned by the same laws of classical and operant conditioning that produced it. This is its theory of pathology (a symptom is a conditioned response or an operant maintained by its consequences) and it dictates its theory of change (re-condition the response, or alter the contingencies). There is no appeal to unconscious conflict, and crucially no claim that the symptom is a sign of a deeper disorder that must be uncovered; the target is the observable behaviour and the contingencies that maintain it, here and now. The two engines are Pavlovian (classical) conditioning, which Wolpe harnessed for anxiety, and Skinnerian (operant) conditioning, which gave us functional analysis and the reinforcement technologies. Behaviour therapy values precise operational definitions, baseline measurement, and graphed outcome data, an empirical temperament that the examiners like to contrast with the interpretive stance of psychoanalysis.

Wolpe: reciprocal inhibition and systematic desensitisation

Joseph Wolpe (1958, in Psychotherapy by Reciprocal Inhibition) proposed the principle of reciprocal inhibition: if a response antagonistic to anxiety can be made to occur in the presence of an anxiety-evoking stimulus, it will weaken the bond between that stimulus and the anxiety. The classic antagonist is deep muscular relaxation, because the parasympathetic relaxation response and the sympathetic anxiety response are physiologically incompatible; a body cannot be simultaneously deeply relaxed and acutely anxious. The theory of change is therefore counter-conditioning: pairing the feared cue with a competing, incompatible state until the cue no longer evokes fear. Wolpe derived the idea partly from Jacobson's relaxation work and from animal experiments (feeding cats in the presence of a conditioned fear stimulus, so that eating, an appetitive response, inhibited the fear).

Wolpe operationalised this as systematic desensitisation, a three-step protocol that you should be able to recite in order:

  1. Relaxation training. The patient is trained in deep relaxation, usually Jacobson's progressive muscle relaxation (tensing then releasing muscle groups in sequence), until they can enter a calm state on cue.
  2. Hierarchy construction. Therapist and patient build an idiosyncratic anxiety hierarchy: a personally tailored, ranked list of feared situations from least to most threatening, each rated on the Subjective Units of Distress Scale (SUDS), a 0 to 100 self-report scale where 0 is complete calm and 100 is the worst imaginable terror. "Idiosyncratic" is the load-bearing word: the hierarchy is built for this individual, not from a standard list.
  3. Graded pairing. While deeply relaxed, the patient is exposed (in imagination first, then in vivo) to the lowest item; only when a rung evokes no anxiety (SUDS returns near baseline) does the patient move up. Exposure is therefore graded, gradual, and always paired with relaxation, the exact opposite of flooding.

Worked example. A student with a dog phobia (peak SUDS 95 at "a large dog jumps on me") is taught relaxation, then constructed a hierarchy: looking at a photo of a dog (SUDS 15), a video of a dog (25), a leashed puppy across the room (40), a leashed puppy beside her (60), patting a calm adult dog (80). Relaxed, she visualises item one until SUDS drops to 5, then proceeds; the leashed-puppy step is later repeated in vivo. By pairing each cue with relaxation the conditioned bond between dog and fear is progressively dissolved.

Definition

SUDS (Subjective Units of Distress Scale): a 0 to 100 self-rating of present anxiety used to anchor each step of the hierarchy and to decide when the patient is calm enough to advance. Reciprocal inhibition: the weakening of an anxiety response by simultaneously eliciting an incompatible response (relaxation). Counter-conditioning: replacing one conditioned response (fear) to a stimulus with a new, incompatible one (calm).

High-yield

Systematic desensitisation is graded exposure plus relaxation, built on reciprocal inhibition and ordered by a SUDS-rated, idiosyncratic anxiety hierarchy. Originator: Joseph Wolpe (1958). The three steps are relaxation training, hierarchy construction, graded pairing. Distinguish it sharply from flooding, which uses no relaxation and no hierarchy. Items test the year, the SUDS scale name, and the relaxation method (Jacobson).

Skinner: operant conditioning and functional analysis

B. F. Skinner's operant conditioning holds that behaviour is shaped by its consequences: reinforcement increases the future probability of a behaviour, punishment decreases it. The clinician's first tool is functional behavioural analysis, the ABC sequence: identify the Antecedent (the trigger or setting event that precedes the behaviour), the Behaviour itself (defined observably and measurably, never as a trait), and the Consequence (what follows and thereby maintains it). Mapping the ABC reveals the function the behaviour serves, conventionally one of four: escape/avoidance, attention, tangible reward, or sensory self-stimulation. Function, not topography, drives the intervention: two children who both hit may do so for opposite functions (one to gain attention, one to escape a demand), and they need opposite treatments.

A token economy applies operant principles to a ward, classroom, or family: desired behaviours earn tokens (secondary, conditioned reinforcers) that are later exchanged for backup reinforcers (privileges, snacks, activities). It works only if the target behaviours are clearly specified, tokens are delivered immediately and contingently, and the exchange rate is meaningful; it was historically validated by Ayllon and Azrin on chronic psychiatric wards.

Applied Behaviour Analysis (ABA) is the structured application of operant principles, most associated with autism and developmental disability and often delivered through discrete-trial training. Its core teaching procedures are:

  • Shaping: reinforcing successive approximations to a target behaviour, so a behaviour the person cannot yet perform is built up step by step from rough attempts (reinforcing any vocal sound, then sounds resembling "wa", then "water").
  • Chaining: teaching a multi-step skill (for example handwashing) as a linked sequence of discrete steps. In forward chaining the first step is taught first and later steps are prompted; in backward chaining the therapist completes all but the last step and the learner performs the final step (gaining the natural reinforcement of completion immediately), then works backward step by step.
  • Prompting and fading: a prompt (physical guidance, gesture, verbal cue) evokes the behaviour, and the prompt is then systematically faded so the behaviour comes under natural control rather than depending on the helper.
  • The Premack principle: a high-probability (preferred) behaviour can reinforce a low-probability (less preferred) one. Stated as "first eat your vegetables, then you may play": access to the preferred activity is made contingent on completing the non-preferred one. Also called the "grandma's rule" of reinforcement.

Two contingency tools complete the operant toolkit and are frequently confused. Differential reinforcement reinforces a desirable alternative while withholding reinforcement from the problem behaviour (for example DRO, differential reinforcement of other behaviour). Time-out (time-out from positive reinforcement) and response cost (removing a token or privilege) are forms of negative punishment: they reduce behaviour by removing something pleasant, distinct from positive punishment which adds something aversive.

Exam trap

Forward versus backward chaining is a favourite reversal. Forward chaining teaches step 1 first; backward chaining has the learner perform the last step first (the therapist having done the rest), so completion-reinforcement is immediate. Shaping reinforces approximations of one behaviour; chaining links several discrete behaviours into a sequence. Time-out and response cost are negative punishment (removing a positive), not negative reinforcement (which increases behaviour). Do not conflate these.

Memory aid

Premack = "Preferred behaviour Pays for the Painful one." Grandma's rule: dessert is earned by eating dinner. ABC = the chain you read left to right; the Consequence on the right is what you change to change the Behaviour in the middle.

Exposure paradigms: flooding, implosion, ERP

Exposure therapies all work by confronting the feared stimulus while preventing the escape or avoidance that normally maintains the fear; the avoidance is the engine of the disorder, so blocking it is the engine of the cure. Where systematic desensitisation is graded and gentle, the flooding family is intensive and immediate, exposing the patient to high anxiety from the outset.

FeatureSystematic desensitisationFloodingImplosion (implosive therapy)
PaceGraded, bottom of hierarchy upMaximum from the outset, no hierarchyMaximum, imaginally, no hierarchy
Relaxation used?Yes (reciprocal inhibition)NoNo
Mode of exposureImaginal then in vivoIn vivo (real-life), prolongedImaginal (in imagination)
Content of scenesReal feared situationsThe actual feared situationExaggerated scenes loaded with psychodynamic themes
MechanismCounter-conditioningExtinction through sustained exposure without avoidanceExtinction of conditioned anxiety to imagined cues
OriginatorWolpe (1958)In-vivo prolonged exposure traditionThomas Stampfl (1967)

Both flooding and implosion drop the hierarchy and the relaxation of desensitisation and instead expose the patient to high anxiety until it spontaneously subsides; the patient learns, through direct experience, that the feared catastrophe does not occur and that anxiety falls on its own when avoidance is blocked. The mechanism is extinction: the conditioned stimulus is presented repeatedly without the feared unconditioned consequence, so the conditioned fear response weakens.

High-yield

The cleanest one-line discriminator: flooding = in-vivo (real) prolonged exposure; implosion = imaginal exposure using psychodynamic anxiety themes (Stampfl, 1967). Both abandon the graded hierarchy and the relaxation that define systematic desensitisation. Stampfl's name and the word "imaginal" are the implosion tells.

Exposure and Response Prevention (ERP) is the gold-standard behavioural treatment for obsessive-compulsive disorder, developed by Victor Meyer (1966). The patient is deliberately exposed to the obsessional trigger (touching a "contaminated" surface) and then prevented from performing the compulsive ritual (hand-washing). The compulsion normally produces negative reinforcement: the ritual lowers anxiety, the relief reinforces the ritual, and the loop tightens. ERP breaks this loop by blocking the response so the patient discovers that anxiety habituates on its own and the feared outcome does not materialise. The two mechanisms cited are habituation (within- and between-session decline of the anxiety response with repeated exposure) and, in the modern account, inhibitory learning (the original fear association is not erased but a new, competing "safety" association is learned and comes to inhibit it). Worked example: a patient with contamination OCD touches a doorknob (exposure) and agrees not to wash for a set period (response prevention); SUDS rises to 80, then falls to 30 over forty minutes purely through the passage of time, teaching that the ritual was never what made the anxiety drop.

Exam trap

In OCD the compulsion is maintained by negative reinforcement (removal of anxiety), not positive reinforcement. ERP works by preventing the response so the negative-reinforcement loop cannot complete. The modern mechanism is inhibitory learning (a new safety memory inhibits the old fear), not simple erasure of the fear. ERP is for OCD; systematic desensitisation is the prototype for simple phobia.

Behavioural activation and biofeedback

Behavioural activation (BA) targets major depression (associated with Lewinsohn's reinforcement model and later Jacobson's component analysis). Its theory of pathology is that depression is maintained by a vicious cycle: low mood leads to withdrawal and inactivity, which removes the person from sources of reinforcement (mastery and pleasure), which deepens low mood. Treatment schedules graded, valued, and pleasurable activities to re-establish contact with positive reinforcement, working from the outside in (action before motivation), without requiring any cognitive restructuring. A component analysis famously found BA alone as effective as full cognitive therapy for depression, which is exactly the sort of "surprising equivalence" finding the paper likes to test.

Biofeedback gives the patient real-time information about a physiological process normally outside awareness: muscle tension via electromyography (EMG), heart-rate variability, skin conductance (electrodermal), peripheral temperature, or brain electrical activity via EEG in neurofeedback. By seeing or hearing the signal, the patient learns voluntary control through operant shaping, lowering arousal in anxiety, tension headache, hypertension, and Raynaud's phenomenon (temperature biofeedback). The mechanism is operant: the displayed signal acts as immediate reinforcement for movement in the desired direction. How it is tested: match the modality to the measure (EMG to muscle tension and tension headache; thermal to Raynaud's; EEG to neurofeedback).

B. Cognitive Restructuring: Beck and Ellis

Cognitive therapy shifts the target inward: distress is driven not by events themselves but by the interpretation of events. This is the cognitive theory of pathology (faulty information processing generates distorted appraisals that drive emotion) and theory of change (identify, test, and revise the appraisal, and the emotion follows). Two figures dominate, and the examiners test the difference between them relentlessly, because their models are superficially similar but use entirely different vocabularies and therapeutic styles.

Beck's cognitive model and the cognitive triad

Aaron T. Beck (a trained psychoanalyst) developed cognitive therapy in the 1960s from his observation that depressed patients reported a stream of negative thoughts that did not fit the drive-conflict model. His cognitive model proposes a three-level architecture of thought. At the deepest level sit core beliefs / schemas, absolute global statements about the self formed early in life ("I am unlovable," "I am incompetent"). Schemas generate intermediate beliefs: the conditional rules, attitudes, and assumptions that operate as if-then contracts ("If I am not perfect, then I am a failure"). When an environmental event activates a schema, it throws up automatic thoughts: the rapid, involuntary, situation-specific cognitions that flit through the mind ("I'll mess this up") and directly drive the emotional and behavioural reaction.

In depression specifically, Beck described the cognitive triad: simultaneous negative views of (1) the self ("I am worthless"), (2) the world / current experience ("everything is against me"), and (3) the future ("nothing will improve"; hopelessness). The triad is the engine of depressive cognition and a near-certain examination item.

Definition

Beck's three levels, deepest to most surface: core beliefs / schemas (global self-statements) then intermediate beliefs (conditional rules and assumptions) then automatic thoughts (moment-to-moment cognitions triggered by events). The cognitive triad = negative views of self, world, and future.

The cognitive distortions are the systematic errors of reasoning by which automatic thoughts get generated. Learn the full list with its signature definition, because items pair the name to a one-line example and ask you to name the distortion:

DistortionWhat it isWorked one-liner
Arbitrary inferenceDrawing a conclusion with no supporting evidence (jumping to conclusions)"She did not wave, so she hates me" (no evidence)
Selective abstractionFocusing on a single negative detail out of context while ignoring the wholeOne critical line in a glowing appraisal ruins it
OvergeneralisationDrawing a sweeping rule from one isolated incident"I failed once, so I always fail"
Dichotomous (all-or-nothing) thinkingBlack-and-white reasoning with no middle ground"If I am not the best, I am a total failure"
PersonalisationAttributing external events to oneself without basis"The team lost because of me"
Magnification / minimisationExaggerating negatives or shrinking positives (the "catastrophising" subtype magnifies threat)A minor error felt as a disaster

Beck's techniques follow directly from the model and are all forms of testing thoughts rather than asserting their falsity. The therapeutic stance is collaborative empiricism: therapist and patient work as co-scientists, treating thoughts as hypotheses to be tested rather than facts. Socratic questioning / guided discovery uses a chain of careful, non-leading questions ("What is the evidence for that? Is there another way to see it? What would you tell a friend?") to lead the patient to examine and revise a belief themselves rather than being told. The thought record (dysfunctional thought record) is the worksheet on which the patient logs situation, automatic thought, emotion and intensity, evidence for and against, and a balanced alternative thought. Behavioural experiments (reality-testing) design real-world tests of a prediction ("if I speak up I will be humiliated") to gather disconfirming evidence. The downward arrow technique repeatedly asks "and if that were true, what would it mean about you?" to drill from a surface automatic thought down to the underlying core belief. Worked example: a patient's automatic thought "I'll be quiet at the party" yields, via downward arrow, "they'll think I'm boring", then "no one will ever like me", then the core belief "I am fundamentally unlikeable", which becomes the real target.

Memory aid

The downward arrow points downward: it digs from a surface automatic thought to the deep core belief. The cognitive triad is self, world, future: "me, my surroundings, my tomorrow." Beck's voice is the gentle question; Ellis's voice is the forceful challenge.

Ellis: Rational Emotive Behaviour Therapy (REBT)

Albert Ellis founded Rational Emotive Behaviour Therapy (REBT) in 1955, making it the earliest of the cognitive therapies (predating Beck). Its theory of change is the ABCDE model. An Activating event (A) does not cause the emotion directly; it is filtered through the person's Belief (B) about it, and it is the belief, when irrational, that produces the disturbed emotional/behavioural Consequence (C). This "B causes C, not A" insight is the heart of REBT. Therapy then adds D, active Disputation of the irrational belief, and E, an Effective new rational philosophy with the healthier emotion that follows. Disputation is itself threefold: logical ("does it follow?"), empirical ("where is the evidence?"), and pragmatic ("where does holding this belief get you?").

Ellis held that the core pathogenic beliefs are absolutist demands: rigid "musts," "shoulds," and "oughts" imposed on oneself, others, or the world. He coined musturbation for the tyranny of these demands and spoke of "the tyranny of the shoulds." From the core demand flow three derivative irrational beliefs: awfulising ("it is the end of the world"), low frustration tolerance / "I can't stand it"-itis, and global self-rating / damnation ("I am worthless"). Disputation attacks the rigid demand and replaces it with a flexible preference ("I would strongly prefer to succeed, but I do not have to"). Worked example: a student who failed an exam holds the demand "I absolutely must succeed and it is unbearable that I did not"; the therapist disputes empirically ("what proves you must?") and pragmatically ("how does that demand help you revise?"), arriving at the preference "I wanted to pass and I will work harder", which converts despair into healthy disappointment.

High-yield

REBT: Albert Ellis (1955), the first cognitive therapy. ABCDE = Activating event, Belief, Consequence, Disputation, Effective new philosophy. Pathology lies in irrational absolutist demands (musts/shoulds, "musturbation"); the three derivatives are awfulising, low frustration tolerance, and global self-rating.

Exam trap

Beck versus Ellis. Beck speaks of the cognitive triad, schemas, automatic thoughts, and cognitive distortions, and his style is gentle Socratic collaborative empiricism. Ellis speaks of the ABC(DE) model, irrational beliefs, musturbation and shoulds, and his style is direct, forceful disputation. If a stem says "musturbation," "ABCDE," or "irrational beliefs," the answer is Ellis/REBT; if it says "cognitive triad," "automatic thoughts," or "collaborative empiricism," the answer is Beck. REBT is older (1955); Beck's model is 1960s.

C. Third-Wave Therapies: DBT, ACT, MBCT

The "third wave" (the first wave being behaviour therapy, the second being Beck/Ellis cognitive therapy) does not try to change the content of thoughts so much as the person's relationship to them. The shared theory of change is that suffering is amplified by the struggle to control or eliminate inner experience (experiential avoidance), so the cure is acceptance, mindfulness, present-moment awareness, and values-guided action layered on top of behavioural and cognitive technique. This single contrast (change the content versus change the relationship) is the most reliable third-wave examination theme.

Linehan: Dialectical Behaviour Therapy (DBT)

Marsha Linehan developed Dialectical Behaviour Therapy (DBT) in the late 1980s and early 1990s specifically for borderline personality disorder and chronic parasuicidality (recurrent self-harm and suicidal behaviour). Its theory of pathology is the biosocial model: a biologically based emotional vulnerability (high sensitivity, high reactivity, slow return to baseline) interacting with an invalidating environment that dismisses or punishes the child's emotional experience, yielding pervasive emotion dysregulation. Its central insight is a dialectic: the simultaneous holding of two opposites, acceptance (validating the patient exactly as they are) and change (relentlessly working toward new behaviour). Pure change-focused therapy feels invalidating to these patients and they drop out; pure acceptance leaves the life-threatening behaviour untouched. The synthesis of the two is the engine of change. Full DBT has four modes of delivery: individual therapy, group skills training, between-session telephone coaching, and a therapist consultation team.

DBT delivers skills through four modules, two acceptance-oriented and two change-oriented:

  • Core Mindfulness (acceptance): the foundational module; observing, describing, and participating without judgement, learning "wise mind" (the synthesis of emotion mind and reasonable mind).
  • Distress Tolerance (acceptance): surviving crises without making them worse. Key skills are TIPP (Temperature, Intense exercise, Paced breathing, Paired/Progressive muscle relaxation) to rapidly drop physiological arousal, and radical acceptance (fully accepting reality as it is, to end the suffering that comes from fighting it).
  • Emotion Regulation (change): identifying, understanding, and modulating emotions, including opposite action (acting against the urge an emotion drives, for example approaching when fear says avoid).
  • Interpersonal Effectiveness (change): asking for what one needs and saying no while keeping the relationship and self-respect, taught through DEAR MAN (Describe, Express, Assert, Reinforce, stay Mindful, Appear confident, Negotiate).

How it is tested: match each acronym to its module (TIPP and radical acceptance to Distress Tolerance, DEAR MAN to Interpersonal Effectiveness, opposite action to Emotion Regulation, wise mind to Core Mindfulness) and recall the indication (BPD/parasuicidality) and the acceptance-change dialectic.

High-yield

DBT: originator Marsha Linehan, for borderline personality disorder / parasuicidality, built on the biosocial model, central acceptance-change dialectic, four modules (Mindfulness, Distress Tolerance, Emotion Regulation, Interpersonal Effectiveness). Attach TIPP and radical acceptance to Distress Tolerance and DEAR MAN to Interpersonal Effectiveness.

Hayes: Acceptance and Commitment Therapy (ACT)

Steven Hayes developed Acceptance and Commitment Therapy (ACT, said as one word "act"), grounded in Relational Frame Theory (RFT), a behavioural account of how human language and cognition arbitrarily relate events and so make us suffer over words and thoughts. ACT's theory of pathology is psychological inflexibility driven by experiential avoidance and cognitive fusion; its goal and theory of change is psychological flexibility: the ability to stay in contact with the present moment and act on one's values even in the presence of painful thoughts and feelings. The model is the Hexaflex, six interlocking processes:

  • Acceptance: making room for unwanted internal experiences rather than struggling to change or suppress them.
  • Cognitive defusion: stepping back from thoughts and seeing them as just thoughts, words and pictures, not literal truths or commands (for example saying a feared thought aloud repeatedly until it loses its grip).
  • Contact with the present moment: flexible, mindful attention to the here and now.
  • Self-as-context: the stable "observing self" that notices experience but is not defined by any particular thought or feeling.
  • Values: clarifying what truly matters, the chosen life directions.
  • Committed action: taking concrete, persistent, values-guided steps even when it is uncomfortable.

Worked example: a socially anxious client does not dispute "everyone will judge me" (that would be Beck); instead she practises defusion ("I am having the thought that everyone will judge me"), accepts the anxiety as a passenger on the bus, clarifies the value of friendship, and takes committed action by attending the gathering with the anxiety present rather than waiting for it to vanish.

Memory aid

ACT in three words: "Accept, Choose, Take action." The Hexaflex pairs into three dyads: be open (acceptance + defusion), be present (present moment + self-as-context), do what matters (values + committed action).

Exam trap

The theoretical base of ACT is Relational Frame Theory, not Beck's schema theory. ACT does not dispute or restructure the content of thoughts (that is Beck/Ellis); it changes the person's relationship to thoughts via defusion. Mixing up ACT (Hayes, RFT, Hexaflex, psychological flexibility) with DBT (Linehan, dialectic, four modules, BPD) is the commonest error in this block.

Segal, Williams, and Teasdale: MBCT

Mindfulness-Based Cognitive Therapy (MBCT) (Segal, Williams, and Teasdale, around 2002) integrates Beck's cognitive therapy with Jon Kabat-Zinn's Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction (MBSR), delivered as an eight-week group programme. Its specific indication is the prevention of depressive relapse in people with three or more previous episodes of recurrent depression. The mechanism is decentring: by teaching mindful awareness, MBCT helps patients notice the early sad-mood-triggered ruminative thinking that reactivates depression (the "differential activation" account) and step out of the rumination loop before it pulls them back into a full episode. It teaches patients to relate to thoughts as passing mental events rather than facts, the third-wave move applied to relapse prevention.

High-yield

MBCT = Beck's CBT + Kabat-Zinn's MBSR, an eight-week group programme aimed squarely at preventing relapse in recurrent depression (three or more episodes) by interrupting rumination through decentring. It is preventive, not primarily a treatment for the acute episode. MBSR (Kabat-Zinn) is the parent stress-reduction programme; MBCT adds the cognitive relapse-prevention focus.

D. Psychoanalytic and Psychodynamic Paradigms

Psychoanalysis, founded by Sigmund Freud around the turn of the twentieth century, holds that present symptoms are the surface expression of unconscious conflict, much of it rooted in early childhood and sexuality, kept out of awareness by defence. This is its theory of pathology: the symptom is a compromise formation, a disguised expression of a repressed wish in conflict with a prohibition. The theory of change is insight: making the unconscious conscious so that the buried conflict can be re-experienced, understood, and worked through, freeing the energy bound up in the symptom. Classical analysis is intensive (several sessions a week, the couch, the analyst out of sight); psychodynamic psychotherapy is its less intensive, face-to-face descendant.

The two models of mind

Freud offered two overlapping maps. The topographical model (earlier) divides the mind by accessibility to awareness: the Conscious (what is in mind now), the Preconscious (what can be readily brought to mind), and the Unconscious (the vast reservoir of repressed wishes and memories that cannot ordinarily be accessed). The later structural model (1923) divides the mind by function into three agencies:

  • Id: present from birth, entirely unconscious, the reservoir of instinctual drives; it runs on the pleasure principle (immediate gratification) and primary process thinking (illogical, timeless, wish-fulfilling).
  • Ego: the executive that mediates between id, superego, and reality; it runs on the reality principle, using reality testing and secondary process (logical, planful) thinking to delay gratification, and it deploys the defences.
  • Superego: the internalised moral standards and ideals (conscience plus ego-ideal), formed by identification with parental prohibitions at the resolution of the Oedipus complex.
Exam trap

Topographical (Conscious / Preconscious / Unconscious) and structural (Id / Ego / Superego) are different models, not the same map relabelled. Pleasure principle and primary process belong to the id; reality principle, reality testing, and secondary process belong to the ego. Items swap these attributes (attaching the reality principle to the id is the classic trick).

Defence mechanisms

Defences are unconscious ego operations that protect against anxiety arising from conflict. Examiners want them grouped by maturity, an arrangement associated with George Vaillant:

LevelMechanismsSignature definition (one example)
MatureSublimation, Humour, Suppression, Altruism, AnticipationSublimation: channelling an unacceptable impulse into a socially valued activity (aggression into surgery or sport)
Neurotic / immatureReaction formation, Projection, Displacement, Intellectualisation, Rationalisation, Regression, Isolation of affect, UndoingReaction formation: expressing the opposite of a forbidden impulse (excessive kindness masking hatred)
PsychoticDenial, Distortion, Projective identificationDenial: refusing to acknowledge an obvious external reality

Note the fine discriminations the paper exploits. Projection attributes one's own unacceptable feeling to another ("he hates me," when in fact I hate him), whereas displacement redirects the feeling onto a safer target (anger at the boss vented on the spouse). Reaction formation converts the impulse into its opposite. Rationalisation supplies a plausible but false justification, while intellectualisation retreats into abstract thinking to avoid the feeling, and isolation of affect keeps the idea but strips off its emotion. Undoing magically reverses an unacceptable act (a compulsive ritual), and regression retreats to an earlier developmental mode. Suppression (mature, conscious postponement of a thought) must be distinguished from repression (the basic, unconscious banishing of material). Projective identification (a Kleinian, psychotic-level defence) goes beyond projection: the person not only projects a feeling into another but unconsciously pressures that other into actually feeling and enacting it, so the recipient really does become angry or anxious.

Memory aid

Mature defences spell a handy "SASH": Sublimation, Altruism, Suppression, Humour. Suppression is the conscious cousin of repression: suppression you choose, repression happens to you. Projection puts the feeling onto someone; projective identification puts it into them and makes them live it out.

The classical techniques

Freudian technique aims to surface the unconscious and analyse what obstructs that surfacing. Free association asks the patient to say whatever comes to mind without censorship (the "fundamental rule"); the gaps, slips, and detours reveal where conflict lies. Dream analysis treats the dream as the "royal road to the unconscious," distinguishing the remembered manifest content from the underlying latent content, the two connected by the dream-work (condensation, displacement, symbolisation). Transference is the patient's unconscious projection onto the therapist of feelings and relational patterns originally directed at important childhood figures; it is not noise but the central therapeutic material, because the old conflict is now live in the room to be interpreted (a "transference neurosis"). Countertransference is the therapist's own emotional reaction to the patient, to be monitored and managed and, in modern relational use, mined as information about the patient's inner world. Resistance is anything in the patient that opposes the work (lateness, silence, forgetting, intellectualising); analysing the resistance is itself therapeutic. The analyst's main instrument is interpretation, often preceded by clarification and confrontation, and lasting change comes through working through, the repeated re-encounter of the same conflict in many guises until insight is consolidated.

Definition

Transference: patient's feelings about past figures redirected onto the therapist. Countertransference: the therapist's emotional reaction to the patient. Manifest content is the dream as remembered; latent content is its hidden meaning. Working through: the repeated analysis of the same conflict across situations until insight produces durable change.

Extended schools

Psychoanalysis branched into post-Freudian schools that the syllabus names explicitly. This cluster of names and signature terms is the single densest source of distractors in the whole section, so fix each theorist to one unmistakable term.

  • Ego psychology (Anna Freud, Heinz Hartmann): shifts focus from the id to the ego and its defences. Anna Freud catalogued the defence mechanisms (The Ego and the Mechanisms of Defence, 1936); Hartmann introduced the "conflict-free ego sphere" and the idea of adaptation to an "average expectable environment."
  • Object relations theory centres on internalised relationships ("objects") rather than drives. Melanie Klein proposed two developmental positions: the paranoid-schizoid position (the infant splits objects into all-good and all-bad to manage persecutory anxiety) and the later depressive position (recognising that the loved and hated object are one whole person, bringing guilt and the wish to repair). Margaret Mahler described separation-individuation, the process by which the infant emerges from fusion with the mother through subphases (normal autism, symbiosis, then the four sub-phases of differentiation, practising, rapprochement, and consolidation/object constancy). Donald Winnicott gave us the good-enough mother (who need not be perfect, only reliable enough), the transitional object (the blanket or teddy that bridges inner and outer reality), the holding environment, and the true self versus false self (a compliant false self develops when the environment fails to meet the infant's spontaneous gestures).
  • Self psychology (Heinz Kohut) holds that the self is built through selfobject needs met by caregivers: mirroring (being admired and affirmed), idealising (merging with an admired, calm figure), and twinship / alter-ego (feeling essentially alike with others). These needs are met and gradually internalised through "transmuting internalisation," and chronic empathic failures produce narcissistic pathology.
Exam trap

The object-relations names are the most confused cluster in this section. Fix them by their unique term: Klein = paranoid-schizoid and depressive positions (plus splitting, projective identification). Mahler = separation-individuation subphases (rapprochement is hers). Winnicott = good-enough mother, transitional object, true/false self, holding environment. Kohut = self psychology, selfobject, mirroring/idealising/twinship. Anna Freud = the defences; Hartmann = conflict-free ego sphere.

E. Humanistic-Existential Therapies

The "third force" (after psychoanalysis and behaviourism) rejects both the deterministic unconscious of Freud and the mechanistic stimulus-response account of the behaviourists. Its theory of pathology is that distress arises when the person's innate growth tendency is blocked, typically by conditions of worth imposed from outside or by avoidance of the unavoidable facts of existence. People are seen as fundamentally growth-oriented, free, and responsible; the therapeutic task is to provide conditions in which the person's own drive toward self-actualisation can resume. The stance is phenomenological (entering the client's subjective world) and the relationship itself, not technique, is held to be the active ingredient.

Rogers: Person-Centred Therapy

Carl Rogers built Person-Centred (client-centred) Therapy on the belief that the client, not the expert therapist, holds the direction of change. His theory of pathology is incongruence between the organism's actual experience and the self-concept, produced by internalised conditions of worth (the child learns it is valued only when it meets others' conditions, and so distorts or denies experiences that threaten that approval). In his 1957 paper Rogers specified six necessary and sufficient conditions for therapeutic change, of which the famous three are the therapist-offered conditions:

  • Empathy (accurate empathic understanding): accurately sensing and reflecting the client's internal frame of reference, "as if" one were the client, without losing the "as if".
  • Congruence (genuineness): the therapist being authentic and transparent within the relationship, not hiding behind a professional facade.
  • Unconditional positive regard: a non-possessive, non-judgemental warmth and acceptance of the client regardless of what they disclose.

The full six conditions also require that two persons are in psychological contact, that the client is in a state of incongruence, and that the client perceives the empathy and regard offered. When these conditions are reliably present, the client's incongruence between the real self and the ideal self reduces and the actualising tendency resumes. Rogers held these conditions to be both necessary (nothing works without them) and sufficient (nothing else is required), a strong claim the examiners quote directly.

Worked example. A client confesses she resents her ageing parent and feels she is "a terrible daughter." A directive therapist might advise or reassure; the person-centred therapist instead offers unconditional positive regard (accepting the resentment without judgement), empathy (reflecting "it sounds exhausting and you feel guilty for even saying it"), and congruence (responding as a real person, not from behind a role). Feeling accepted with the very feeling she condemned in herself, she can re-own the experience instead of denying it, and her self-concept widens to include it, reducing incongruence. The technical hallmark is reflection of feeling and the deliberate absence of advice, interpretation, or direction; the client leads.

High-yield

Rogers's three core therapist conditions: empathy, congruence (genuineness), unconditional positive regard, set out as necessary and sufficient conditions for change (1957). A favourite item asks for "the three Rogerian conditions," names one (for example unconditional positive regard) and asks its school (person-centred), or tests the phrase "necessary and sufficient." Pathology = incongruence from conditions of worth; the actualising tendency is the growth motive Rogers posits in everyone.

Perls: Gestalt Therapy

Fritz Perls (with Laura Perls and Paul Goodman) founded Gestalt therapy, which prizes immediate awareness in the here-and-now over insight into the past ("lose your mind and come to your senses"). Health is good contact at the contact boundary between self and environment; neurosis is unfinished business, incomplete emotional situations that press for closure, and disturbances of contact (introjection, projection, retroflection, confluence). Techniques are active and experiential: the empty chair (the client dialogues with an imagined other, or a disowned part of the self, placed in an empty chair), the two-chair work for internal conflicts (for example between "top dog" and "underdog"), exaggeration, staying with the feeling, and dream work in which every dream element is enacted as a part of the self. Its theoretical heart is the paradoxical theory of change (formulated by Arnold Beisser): change occurs not when one tries to become what one is not, but when one fully becomes what one is; trying to force change entrenches the status quo, while full acceptance of the present state frees it to shift.

Memory aid

Empty chair = Gestalt = Perls. "Unfinished business" and "contact boundary" are the Gestalt flags. The paradoxical theory of change: "you change by accepting what you already are, not by chasing what you are not."

Frankl: Logotherapy

Viktor Frankl founded logotherapy ("therapy through meaning," the "third Viennese school" after Freud and Adler), forged from his concentration-camp survival and set out in Man's Search for Meaning. The central human drive is the "will to meaning"; its frustration produces an existential vacuum, a pervasive sense of emptiness, boredom, and meaninglessness. Two named techniques are routinely tested: dereflection (redirecting attention away from anxious self-monitoring onto something outside the self, useful in sexual and sleep problems where excessive self-observation, hyper-reflection, is itself the trouble) and paradoxical intention (deliberately wishing for or exaggerating the very thing one fears, for example an insomniac trying hard to stay awake, or a patient who fears sweating trying to sweat a litre, which removes the anticipatory anxiety that maintained the symptom). Worked example: a man with a fear of blushing in meetings is told to try to blush as red as possible on purpose; the harder he tries, the more the anticipatory dread that drove the blushing collapses.

Yalom: existential psychotherapy

Irvin Yalom systematised existential therapy around four ultimate concerns (givens) of existence that confront every person: death (the inevitability of one's own ending), freedom (the burden of responsibility that comes with having no firm external ground beneath us), existential isolation (the unbridgeable gap between oneself and any other, however close), and meaninglessness (the absence of any pre-given meaning to life). Anxiety and symptoms are understood as flowing from how one confronts or, more often, evades these givens; therapy helps the person face them authentically.

Exam trap

Keep the existential figures distinct. Frankl = logotherapy, will to meaning, existential vacuum, dereflection and paradoxical intention. Yalom = the four ultimate concerns (death, freedom, isolation, meaninglessness) and the group-therapy factors below. Paradoxical intention (Frankl, an existential technique) is not the paradoxical theory of change (Perls/Gestalt, Beisser); these two "paradox" items are routinely swapped. Rogers = three core conditions; do not attach them to Perls or Frankl.

F. Systemic, Marital, and Group Therapies

Systemic therapies relocate the problem from inside the individual to the pattern of relationships in which the person is embedded; the theory of pathology is that the "identified patient" is merely the symptom-bearer of a dysfunctional system, and the theory of change is that altering the system's structure or process resolves the symptom. The intellectual roots lie in general systems theory and cybernetics (circular causality, feedback loops, homeostasis), which replace the linear "A causes B" of individual models with circular, mutually maintaining patterns.

Minuchin: Structural Family Therapy

Salvador Minuchin developed Structural Family Therapy (out of his work with disadvantaged families and psychosomatic conditions), which maps the family's invisible structure: its subsystems (spousal, parental, sibling), its hierarchy (who holds executive authority), and above all its boundaries, the rules governing who participates and how. Boundaries lie on a continuum:

  • Rigid boundaries produce a disengaged family: members are isolated, autonomous to a fault, and offer little support or warmth.
  • Diffuse boundaries produce an enmeshed family: members are over-involved, over-reactive, and lack individual autonomy; one member's upset instantly engulfs the rest.
  • Clear boundaries (the healthy middle) allow both belonging and autonomy.

Techniques restructure these patterns in the room: joining (the therapist accommodates to the family's style to gain entry), enactment (having the family play out a problematic interaction live so the therapist can observe and alter it on the spot), boundary making (strengthening or loosening boundaries, for example seating to block an over-involved parent from a child), unbalancing (temporarily siding with one member to shift a stuck hierarchy), and reframing (giving a behaviour a new, often more benign or systemic meaning so the family can respond differently).

Worked example. A family presents with a defiant teenage daughter. Minuchin observes that the mother and daughter form an over-close, enmeshed dyad (diffuse boundary), while the father is peripheral and disengaged (rigid boundary), so there is no functioning parental subsystem with shared authority. Through enactment the therapist has the parents jointly set a rule in the session, physically seats the father into the discussion, and makes a boundary between the parental and sibling subsystems, restructuring the hierarchy so the parents lead together and the daughter is released from her over-involved alliance with the mother.

Memory aid

Pair them: rigid boundaries to disengaged (a wall between members, everyone too far apart); diffuse boundaries to enmeshed (no walls, everyone tangled together). Minuchin's flag words are boundaries, subsystems, and enactment.

Bowen: Family Systems Theory

Murray Bowen built a multigenerational systems theory. Its keystone is differentiation of self: the capacity to separate one's intellectual from one's emotional functioning, and to maintain a clear sense of self ("I-position") while staying connected to the family emotional system. Poorly differentiated people fuse with others and are swept along by group emotion; well-differentiated people can stay calm and think clearly under relational pressure. Multigenerational transmission describes how levels of differentiation are passed down the generations, so chronic anxiety and dysfunction accumulate across a family tree (charted on a genogram). Triangulation is the move by which a tense two-person relationship pulls in a third party (often a child) to dilute the anxiety, the basic building block of family emotional process. Other Bowen concepts include the nuclear family emotional process, emotional cut-off, and the family projection process.

Worked example. An adolescent presents with school refusal. The Bowenian therapist notices that whenever the parents' marital tension rises, the mother becomes intensely focused on the child's symptoms and the father withdraws: a classic triangle in which the child's symptom stabilises the marriage by deflecting the couple's anxiety. Therapy works to detriangulate the child and raise the parents' differentiation so they can address their conflict directly rather than routing it through the symptom-bearer. A genogram across three generations reveals the same anxious over-focus passed from the maternal grandmother, the multigenerational transmission at work.

Exam trap

Minuchin versus Bowen. Minuchin = structural, boundaries (rigid/disengaged vs diffuse/enmeshed), subsystems, enactment. Bowen = differentiation of self, multigenerational transmission, triangulation, genogram. The signature phrase "differentiation of self" is always Bowen; "enmeshed/disengaged boundaries" is always Minuchin.

Other strategic and systemic schools

The syllabus's broader systemic field also names schools the paper can quote. Jay Haley's strategic therapy (with roots in Milton Erickson) uses directives and paradoxical prescription, including prescribing the symptom (instructing the family to perform the very behaviour they want gone, exposing its function). The Milan systemic school (Selvini Palazzoli and colleagues) introduced circular questioning (asking each member about the relationship between two others to reveal the system's logic), hypothesising, neutrality, and positive connotation (reframing the symptom as serving the family's cohesion). These are worth a line because items occasionally ask "which school uses circular questioning?" (Milan) or "prescribing the symptom?" (strategic/Haley).

Yalom: group psychotherapy

Irvin Yalom identified eleven therapeutic factors that produce change in group therapy: instillation of hope, universality (realising one is not alone in one's problems), imparting of information, altruism (gaining from helping others), the corrective recapitulation of the primary family group, development of socialising techniques, imitative behaviour, interpersonal learning, group cohesiveness, catharsis, and existential factors. Cohesiveness is to group therapy what the therapeutic relationship is to individual therapy, the precondition for the rest. The group also moves through developmental stages as cohesion builds (an initial orientation/dependency stage, a conflict/storming stage, and a mature cohesive working stage), echoing Tuckman's forming-storming-norming-performing sequence.

High-yield

Yalom's eleven group therapeutic factors are commonly tested by example: "realising other members share your problem" = universality; "benefiting by helping others" = altruism; emotional release = catharsis; the group re-enacting one's original family = corrective recapitulation of the primary family group. Cohesiveness is the group analogue of the therapeutic alliance.

G. Clinical Ethics and Legislative Frameworks

This final block is largely recall of statute and code, and it is reliably tested because the answers are unambiguous: a section number, a percentage, a case name. Master the three Indian Acts (MHCA 2017, RPwD 2016, and the RCI Act that licenses you to practise), the core professional principles, and the landmark duty-to-warn case. Frame the professional principles around the four classical biomedical ethics: autonomy (respecting the client's self-determination), beneficence (acting for their good), non-maleficence (above all, do no harm), and justice (fairness of access and treatment).

The Mental Healthcare Act (MHCA), 2017

The Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 (in force from 2018) replaced the Mental Health Act of 1987 and reframed mental healthcare around the rights and autonomy of the person with mental illness, in line with India's ratification of the UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities. It also guarantees a right to access mental healthcare from services run or funded by the government. Its tested provisions:

  • Advance Directive: a person may make a written directive, in advance, stating how they wish to be treated or not treated for a mental illness and who their representative will be, to take effect during a future crisis when they may lack capacity.
  • Nominated Representative (NR): every person has the right to appoint a representative to take decisions on their behalf and safeguard their interests during treatment.
  • Authorities and Boards: the Act establishes a Central Mental Health Authority and State Mental Health Authorities (to register and regulate establishments and professionals), and Mental Health Review Boards (MHRBs) to review admissions, advance directives, and complaints, protecting patient rights.
  • Capacity-based framework: treatment decisions turn on the person's capacity to make mental-healthcare decisions, and admission categories distinguish independent (voluntary) admission from supported admission with safeguards.
  • Section 115: presumption of severe stress. Any person who attempts suicide is presumed, unless proved otherwise, to be under severe stress, and is not to be tried or punished. This effectively decriminalises attempted suicide (overriding Section 309 of the Indian Penal Code) and obliges the appropriate government to provide care, treatment, and rehabilitation to reduce the risk of recurrence.
  • Prohibitions: the Act bans unmodified ECT (electroconvulsive therapy without muscle relaxants and anaesthesia), bans ECT for minors except with Board permission, prohibits sterilisation of persons with mental illness as a treatment, and bans chaining and seclusion as routine practice.
High-yield

MHCA 2017, Section 115: presumption of severe stress in attempted suicide, decriminalising the act and mandating state-sponsored rehabilitation. This single section is the most heavily tested fact in the entire ethics block. Also remember: advance directive, nominated representative, Mental Health Review Boards, the capacity-based framework, and the ban on unmodified ECT and on sterilisation. It replaced the 1987 Act.

The Rights of Persons with Disabilities (RPwD) Act, 2016

The Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016 (in force 2017) replaced the Persons with Disabilities Act of 1995 and expanded the list of recognised disabilities from 7 to 21. Crucially for clinical psychology, it brought mental illness, autism spectrum disorder, specific learning disabilities, intellectual disability, and chronic neurological conditions under statutory disability classification. It defines a "person with benchmark disability" as someone with not less than 40% of a specified disability as certified by a competent (medical) authority; benchmark-disability status unlocks reservations in higher education and government employment, and other statutory benefits and protections.

Exam trap

The 40% threshold and the term "benchmark disability" belong to the RPwD Act 2016, not the MHCA. The MHCA 2017 deals with treatment rights and capacity (advance directives, NR, Section 115); the RPwD Act deals with disability status, the expanded list of 21 conditions, and the 40% benchmark. Items deliberately attach the 40% figure to the wrong Act, or quote "7 disabilities" (the old 1995 Act) instead of 21.

The Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) Act, 1992

The Rehabilitation Council of India Act, 1992 created the statutory body that regulates the training and registration of rehabilitation professionals, including clinical psychologists, and maintains the Central Rehabilitation Register. This is the Act under which the entrance you are sitting is regulated: it is an offence to practise as a clinical psychologist or to use the title without RCI registration, and the RCI prescribes the recognised qualifications (the M.Phil and the newer RCI-recognised M.A. routes). Knowing that the RCI licenses clinical psychologists (whereas the MHCA governs care and the RPwD governs disability status) is the discriminating point.

Professional ethical guidelines

The RCI code and the APA ethical principles converge on a common core, organised under the four classical principles named above:

  • Informed consent: the client must be given, in understandable terms, the nature, purpose, risks, benefits, alternatives, and limits of the intervention and must agree voluntarily, with special care for children, persons lacking capacity, and diverse populations (assent is sought from those who cannot legally consent).
  • Confidentiality and its exceptions: information is private, but confidentiality is limited and must be broken when there is an immediate risk of serious harm to self or others, in cases of child abuse, or under a valid court order. Knowing these three exceptions verbatim is the tested point.
  • The Duty to Warn / Duty to Protect: established by Tarasoff v. Regents of the University of California (California Supreme Court, 1976). When a client makes a credible threat against an identifiable victim, the therapist's duty of confidentiality yields to a duty to protect the third party (by warning the victim, notifying police, or other reasonable steps). The court's famous formulation: "the protective privilege ends where the public peril begins."
  • Dual (multiple) relationships: a therapist must avoid simultaneous or sequential roles (social, business, sexual, familial) that could impair objectivity or exploit the client; boundaries must be maintained, and sexual relationships with current clients are absolutely prohibited.
  • Cultural competence: the duty to understand and adapt to the client's cultural, linguistic, and social context rather than imposing the therapist's norms.
  • Tele-therapy ethics: digital provision adds duties around data security and privacy, verifying client identity and location, managing emergencies at a distance, obtaining consent specific to the remote modality, and confirming licensure across jurisdictions.
High-yield

The Duty to Warn derives from the Tarasoff case (1976). If a stem describes a client threatening an identifiable third party and asks what overrides confidentiality, the answer is the Tarasoff duty to warn/protect. Pair it with the three standard confidentiality exceptions: risk of harm to self or others, child abuse, and a valid court order. The four guiding principles are autonomy, beneficence, non-maleficence, and justice.

Definition

Dual relationship: any additional relationship with a client (social, financial, sexual, familial) beyond the professional one, prohibited where it risks impaired judgement or exploitation. Benchmark disability: not less than 40% impairment, certified under the RPwD Act 2016. Informed consent: voluntary agreement to treatment after disclosure of its nature, purpose, risks, benefits, alternatives, and limits, in terms the client understands.

6 · Foundational Psychology, Lifespan Development & Social Dynamics

This section teaches the engine room of general psychology: how the mind takes in information (memory and perception), how it changes through experience (learning), how it matures across a lifetime (development), how people behave in groups (social psychology), and how stable individual differences are described (personality). For an RCI clinical-psychology entrance, this is the densest single source of factual one-mark questions: examiners love named theorists, exact stage labels and ages, the four reinforcement schedules, attachment phenotypes, and the classic social-psychology studies. The papers reliably do three things with this material: they ask you to attach a phenomenon to its theorist and year, they ask you to recall an exact number or stage label verbatim, and they set a near-neighbour as the distractor (proactive versus retroactive interference, sensitivity versus bias, assimilation versus accommodation, conformity versus obedience, self-efficacy versus locus of control). We build each concept from the floor up, name the theorist and date, give the mechanism and a worked example, state how it surfaces in the paper, then flag the exact distractor the examiner will use against you. Read each subsection once for the story and again for the numbers and names, because the numbers and names are what is actually marked.

A. Memory: Structural Architectures and Forgetting

Memory is the set of processes by which the nervous system encodes information (turns experience into a storable code), stores it (retains it over time), and retrieves it (brings it back into awareness). The dominant teaching model of the twentieth century treated memory not as one thing but as a sequence of separate stores, each with its own capacity, code, and duration; later models replaced the passive stores with active processing systems. You must hold both pictures, because the paper tests the contrast between them.

Definition

Encoding is converting a stimulus into a memory code (acoustic, visual, or semantic); storage is the maintenance of that code over time; retrieval is bringing it back into consciousness. Forgetting can be a failure at any of the three, and distinguishing an encoding failure from a retrieval failure is itself a tested distinction.

The Atkinson-Shiffrin Multi-Store Model (1968)

Richard Atkinson and Richard Shiffrin proposed that information flows through three structurally distinct stores in series. This is also called the modal model because it became the standard (modal) account. Information enters the sensory register, is transferred by attention to the short-term store, and is transferred by rehearsal to the long-term store; each transfer is a potential point of loss.

  • Sensory memory (sensory register): a very brief, high-capacity, modality-specific buffer that holds a raw, literal copy of incoming sensory input for a fraction of a second. It is pre-attentive: everything that hits the senses is registered, but it decays almost instantly unless attention selects it. Sensory memory is modality-specific, so it has separate sub-stores. Iconic memory is the visual store (the word comes from icon, image); George Sperling's partial-report experiments (1960) showed iconic memory holds roughly nine to twelve items but decays within about 0.25 to 0.5 seconds. Echoic memory is the auditory store (the word comes from echo); it lasts longer, around 2 to 4 seconds, which is why you can still "hear" the end of a sentence you were not attending to.
  • Short-term store (STS): the contents of current awareness. Capacity is famously about seven plus or minus two items (George Miller's "magical number seven", 1956), better described in chunks than raw items. Duration without rehearsal is roughly 15 to 30 seconds. The code is largely acoustic (we tend to confuse similar-sounding items). Maintenance rehearsal (repeating) keeps items in the STS; elaborative rehearsal transfers them onward.
  • Long-term store (LTS): effectively unlimited in capacity and potentially permanent in duration. The code is predominantly semantic (by meaning). Information must pass through the STS to reach the LTS in this model.

Sperling's partial-report procedure is the worked example you should be able to reproduce. He flashed a 3-by-4 grid of letters for 50 milliseconds. In whole report, participants could name only about four or five letters (the rest had faded before they could be read out). In partial report, a tone after the display told the participant which row to report; they could report almost any cued row in full, proving that nearly all twelve letters had been briefly available in iconic memory and had decayed during the readout. The delay between display and tone, plotted against accuracy, traces the decay of iconic memory directly.

StoreCapacityDurationDominant code
Sensory (iconic)9 to 12 items0.25 to 0.5 sVisual, literal
Sensory (echoic)Large2 to 4 sAuditory, literal
Short-term store7 plus or minus 215 to 30 sAcoustic
Long-term storeUnlimitedLifelong (potentially)Semantic
High-yield

Iconic = visual and very brief (about a quarter second); echoic = auditory and longer (a few seconds). The short-term store holds seven plus or minus two items for 15 to 30 seconds. The serial-position curve (better recall of the first items, the primacy effect, reflecting LTS, and of the last items, the recency effect, reflecting STS) is the standard supporting evidence for the multi-store model. These exact numbers are tested verbatim.

Exam trap

Do not swap iconic and echoic. Iconic sounds like "icon = picture" (visual); echoic sounds like "echo = sound" (auditory). Examiners reverse the durations too: iconic is the shorter store, echoic the longer one. A second trap: a delayed distractor task abolishes the recency effect (it empties the STS) while leaving primacy intact, which is the killer evidence that the last and first items come from different stores.

The Baddeley and Hitch Working Memory Model (1974)

Alan Baddeley and Graham Hitch argued the unitary "short-term store" was too simple to explain how we can hold something in mind while manipulating it. They replaced the passive STS with an active, multi-component working memory system: a workspace for temporarily holding and processing information. The clinching evidence is the dual-task logic: two tasks that draw on the same subsystem interfere with each other, while two tasks that draw on different subsystems can be done together without cost, proving the subsystems are separate.

  • Central executive: the attentional control system. It is not a store; it allocates attention, switches between tasks, suppresses irrelevant information, and coordinates the subsystems. It is modality-free and has limited capacity.
  • Phonological loop: handles speech-based (acoustic) information. It has two parts: a phonological store (the "inner ear", holding sound traces for about 2 seconds) and an articulatory rehearsal process (the "inner voice", refreshing the trace by sub-vocal repetition). This is the component you use to hold a phone number by saying it to yourself. Its existence is shown by the word-length effect (lists of short words are recalled better than lists of long words, because long words take longer to rehearse) and by articulatory suppression (repeating "the the the" out loud blocks rehearsal and wipes out the word-length effect).
  • Visuospatial sketchpad: the "inner eye", handling visual and spatial information (the appearance of objects and their locations). You use it to picture a route or rotate a shape mentally. Baddeley split it into a visual cache (form and colour) and an inner scribe (spatial and movement).
  • Episodic buffer: added by Baddeley in 2000 to fix a gap in the original model. It is a limited-capacity, temporary store that integrates information from the phonological loop, the visuospatial sketchpad, and long-term memory into a single multimodal, coherent episode (a "scene"). It binds what something looks like, sounds like, and means into one representation and provides an interface between working memory and long-term memory.
Definition

Working memory is the active system that simultaneously holds and manipulates information in the service of a current task. The phrase "while manipulating" is what separates it from Atkinson-Shiffrin's passive short-term store: a mental-arithmetic problem (hold the numbers and operate on them) cannot be explained by a single passive buffer.

High-yield

The four components: central executive (boss, attention), phonological loop (sound, inner voice), visuospatial sketchpad (vision and space, inner eye), and episodic buffer (the multimodal integrator, added in 2000). The episodic buffer is the most-tested "new" piece: it binds the other stores plus long-term memory into one episode.

Memory aid

"Central Executive Posts Visuals and Episodes": Central executive, Phonological loop, Visuospatial sketchpad, Episodic buffer. Phonological = phone (you rehearse a phone number aloud).

Craik and Lockhart's Levels of Processing (1972)

Fergus Craik and Robert Lockhart offered a rival to the structural stores: instead of asking which box a memory sits in, they argued that retention depends on how deeply the item is processed at encoding. Shallow (structural) processing attends to physical form (is the word in capitals?); intermediate (phonemic) processing attends to sound (does it rhyme with "cat"?); deep (semantic) processing attends to meaning (does it fit the sentence "the ___ ran away"?). Deeper, more semantic, more elaborate processing yields stronger, longer-lasting memories, regardless of how long the item was "held". This is a process account, not a store account, and the paper contrasts the two directly.

Exam trap

Levels of processing (Craik and Lockhart) says depth of encoding determines retention; the multi-store model (Atkinson-Shiffrin) says transfer between stores determines retention. If a stem stresses "semantic versus shallow" or "depth of processing", the answer is Craik and Lockhart, not Atkinson-Shiffrin. Do not attribute the levels-of-processing idea to Baddeley.

Tulving's Classification of Long-Term Memory

Endel Tulving subdivided long-term memory by what kind of content is stored and what kind of awareness accompanies retrieval. The top split is declarative versus non-declarative.

  • Declarative (explicit) memory can be consciously brought to mind and "declared" in words. It divides into:
    • Episodic memory: memory for personally experienced events tied to a specific time and place ("what I ate at my last birthday"). Tulving linked it to autonoetic awareness (self-knowing): you mentally travel back and re-experience the event as having happened to you.
    • Semantic memory: general world knowledge and facts, detached from when you learnt them ("Delhi is the capital of India"). It involves noetic awareness (knowing): you know the fact without re-living its acquisition.
  • Non-declarative (implicit) memory cannot be verbalised and is expressed through performance rather than recollection. The core example is procedural memory: motor and cognitive skills (riding a bicycle, typing). Tulving linked it to anoetic awareness (non-knowing): the skill runs off without any conscious recollection of having learnt it. Other non-declarative forms include priming, classical conditioning, and habituation.

How it is tested: the paper anchors this taxonomy to amnesic patient H.M. (Henry Molaison), whose bilateral medial-temporal-lobe resection (including the hippocampus) left him with a dense anterograde amnesia for new declarative facts and events, yet he could still learn new motor skills (mirror-drawing improved across days) while denying he had ever practised. This double dissociation, declarative impaired and procedural spared, is the classic evidence that these are separate memory systems.

Exam trap

Match the three awarenesses to the three memory types: autonoetic to episodic (re-experiencing the self in an event), noetic to semantic (knowing a fact), anoetic to procedural (no conscious knowing, just doing). The paper loves to pair "semantic" with "autonoetic" as a wrong option. Autonoetic belongs to episodic. A second trap: H.M.'s spared learning is procedural (non-declarative), so "H.M. could not learn anything new" is false.

Forgetting Mechanics

Forgetting is the failure to retrieve information that was once available. Several mechanisms are distinguished, and the paper sets them against one another.

  • The Ebbinghaus forgetting curve: Hermann Ebbinghaus (1885), using himself as the only participant and learning lists of nonsense syllables, plotted retention over time and found memory drops sharply at first and then levels off. Most forgetting happens within the first hour to day; the residue decays slowly. He also discovered savings (relearning is faster than first learning) and the value of spaced over massed practice (the spacing effect).
  • Proactive interference: old learning disrupts the retention of new material. Pro- means forward: previously stored information moves forward in time to block what comes next. Example: after years of an old phone number, you keep retrieving it instead of your new one.
  • Retroactive interference: new learning disrupts the retention of old material. Retro- means backward: new information reaches back and overwrites the earlier code. Example: learning a new password makes you forget the previous one.
  • The Zeigarnik effect: Bluma Zeigarnik (1927, a student of Kurt Lewin) found that interrupted or incomplete tasks are remembered better than completed ones, because an unfinished task maintains a state of tension (a "quasi-need") that keeps it active in memory. This is why a waiter remembers unpaid orders but forgets them once settled.
  • Cue-dependent and decay accounts: Tulving's encoding specificity principle holds that retrieval succeeds when retrieval cues match the cues present at encoding, which is why context-dependent forgetting occurs (you forget in a different room or mood from the one in which you learnt). Decay theory, by contrast, attributes forgetting to the passive fading of a trace over time.
Memory aid
