For interference, anchor on the direction of the prefix. Proactive = the past (old) interferes; retroactive = the recent (new) interferes by reaching back. Old-blocks-new is proactive; new-blocks-old is retroactive.

Exam trap

The single most-reversed pair in the whole memory unit is proactive versus retroactive interference. If the question stem says "old learning hampers new learning" the answer is proactive; if "new learning hampers old learning" the answer is retroactive. Zeigarnik is incomplete tasks remembered better, not worse. Do not confuse interference (competition from other material) with decay (passive fading over time).

B. Learning: Conditioning and Cognitive Models

Learning is a relatively permanent change in behaviour or knowledge that results from experience, distinguished from temporary changes (fatigue, drugs) and from maturation. Two associative paradigms (classical and operant) plus a cognitive tradition dominate the syllabus, and the paper tests both the vocabulary and the contrast between them.

Definition

Classical conditioning teaches an association between two stimuli and acts on a reflexive, involuntary response (salivation, fear). Operant conditioning teaches an association between a behaviour and its consequence and acts on voluntary, emitted behaviour. The cleanest discriminator: in classical conditioning the response is elicited before the outcome; in operant conditioning the behaviour is emitted to produce the outcome.

Classical Conditioning: Pavlov and Watson

Ivan Pavlov, a Russian physiologist studying digestion, discovered that a neutral stimulus, repeatedly paired with one that automatically triggers a reflex, comes itself to trigger that reflex. The vocabulary is fixed and tested:

  • Unconditioned stimulus (UCS): automatically elicits a response (food).
  • Unconditioned response (UCR): the automatic, unlearned reaction (salivation to food).
  • Conditioned stimulus (CS): an originally neutral stimulus (a bell) that, after pairing with the UCS, comes to elicit a response.
  • Conditioned response (CR): the learnt reaction to the CS (salivation to the bell).

John B. Watson, the founder of American behaviourism, extended classical conditioning to emotion. In the "Little Albert" study (1920, with Rosalie Rayner), an infant was conditioned to fear a white rat (CS) by pairing it with a loud noise (UCS). The fear (CR) then generalised to other furry white objects, showing emotional responses are learnt. The clinical bridge is direct: this is the prototype for the conditioning model of phobias, and systematic desensitisation is its therapeutic reversal (extinction plus counter-conditioning).

Key classical-conditioning phenomena:

  • Acquisition: the build-up of the CR as CS and UCS are paired; strongest when the CS slightly precedes the UCS (forward conditioning).
  • Extinction: presenting the CS repeatedly without the UCS weakens and eventually eliminates the CR.
  • Spontaneous recovery: after extinction and a rest period, the CR reappears when the CS is presented again, showing extinction suppresses rather than erases the association.
  • Stimulus generalisation: stimuli similar to the CS also evoke the CR (Albert fearing a rabbit).
  • Stimulus discrimination: learning to respond only to the specific CS and not to similar stimuli.
  • Higher-order (second-order) conditioning: a well-established CS is used to condition a new neutral stimulus, even though that new stimulus is never paired with the original UCS.
Exam trap

Spontaneous recovery is the favourite distractor: after extinction the CR is suppressed, not erased, so it returns after a rest. If the stem says the response "came back after a pause with no further training", the answer is spontaneous recovery, not re-acquisition. Watson, not Pavlov, did Little Albert; Pavlov is the dogs.

The Rescorla-Wagner Model

Robert Rescorla and Allan Wagner (1972) formalised classical conditioning mathematically. The crucial insight: conditioning depends not on simple pairing but on surprise. A UCS only drives learning to the extent it is unpredicted. The change in associative strength of a cue on a given trial is:

Delta VA = alphaA × beta × (lambda − VTotal)

Reading the terms:

  • Delta VA is the change in associative strength of cue A on this trial.
  • alpha (the salience of the CS) and beta (the learning rate set by the UCS) are learning-rate parameters between 0 and 1.
  • lambda (the asymptote) is the maximum associative strength the UCS can support: how much learning is "possible" given this outcome.
  • VTotal is the summed associative strength already held by all cues present on the trial: how much is "already predicted".
  • The bracket (lambda − VTotal) is the prediction error or surprise. When the outcome is fully predicted, lambda equals VTotal, the bracket is zero, and no learning occurs. Large surprise drives large learning.

This single equation explains several phenomena that simple-pairing accounts cannot:

  • Blocking (Kamin): if cue A already fully predicts the UCS (VTotal near lambda), then adding a new cue B in compound (AB) leaves nothing to learn (bracket near zero), so B acquires little or no associative strength. The prior learning "blocks" learning about B. This is the model's flagship prediction.
  • Overshadowing: when two cues of unequal salience are conditioned together, the more salient cue (higher alpha) grabs most of the limited associative strength, "overshadowing" the weaker cue.
  • Uncorrelated contingencies: if a CS does not reliably predict the UCS (the UCS is just as likely without it), no net associative strength accrues, because gains on paired trials are cancelled by losses (extinction) on unpaired trials.
High-yield

Rescorla-Wagner says learning is driven by prediction error / surprise, captured by the term (lambda minus VTotal). Its signature explanation is blocking: a stimulus that adds no new predictive information is not learnt about. When the bracket is zero (outcome fully predicted), Delta V is zero (no learning).

The Garcia Effect (Conditioned Taste Aversion)

John Garcia showed that rats which became nauseous hours after drinking a flavoured solution developed a strong aversion to that taste, even though the illness was delayed by hours and was caused independently (by radiation or a drug). This broke two classical assumptions:

  • Equipotentiality (the behaviourist claim that any CS can be associated equally easily with any UCS) is false: taste readily associates with nausea, but a light or tone does not. The pairing is biologically "prepared", reflecting evolutionary readiness to link food cues with sickness. Garcia's "bright-noisy-water" finding is the worked example: a taste cue paired with sickness produces aversion, but the same sickness paired with an audiovisual cue does not; the reverse holds for shock (audiovisual cues condition readily, taste does not).
  • The contiguity requirement (CS and UCS must be close in time) is violated: taste aversions form across delays of hours, in a single trial.
Exam trap

The Garcia effect is the textbook counter-example to equipotentiality and to the need for temporal contiguity. It demonstrates "biological preparedness" and one-trial, long-delay learning. If the stem mentions taste, nausea, or "preparedness", the answer is Garcia.

Operant Conditioning: Thorndike and Skinner

Operant (instrumental) conditioning concerns learning from the consequences of voluntary behaviour, in contrast to classical conditioning's reflexive responses.

Edward Thorndike placed cats in puzzle boxes and timed how long they took to escape. Escape time fell gradually over trials (trial-and-error learning), leading to the Law of Effect (1898): responses followed by a satisfying consequence are "stamped in" (more likely to recur), while responses followed by discomfort are "stamped out". This is the foundation of all reinforcement theory.

B. F. Skinner systematised this with the operant chamber ("Skinner box") and a precise vocabulary of consequences. The two axes are positive (add a stimulus) versus negative (remove a stimulus), crossed with reinforcement (increase behaviour) versus punishment (decrease behaviour), giving four cells:

Reinforcement (behaviour increases)Punishment (behaviour decreases)
Positive (add a stimulus)Positive reinforcement: add a pleasant stimulus (a treat for a lever press)Positive punishment: add an aversive stimulus (a slap)
Negative (remove a stimulus)Negative reinforcement: remove an aversive stimulus (press a lever to switch off a shock)Negative punishment / omission: remove a pleasant stimulus (take away a toy)

Worked example of negative reinforcement that students get wrong: a person with obsessive-compulsive disorder washes their hands and the anxiety drops. The anxiety reduction removes an aversive state, which strengthens the washing, so washing is negatively reinforced (not punished). This is exactly why compulsions persist, and it is a favourite clinical application of the concept.

Two further operant tools are tested: shaping (reinforcing successive approximations to a target behaviour, used to build complex acts the animal would never emit spontaneously) and the Premack principle (a more probable, preferred behaviour can reinforce a less probable one: "you may play after you finish homework").

Exam trap

"Positive" and "negative" mean add and remove, not good and bad. Negative reinforcement strengthens behaviour (by removing something aversive) and is constantly confused with punishment. Reinforcement always increases behaviour; punishment always decreases it. Escape and avoidance learning are both forms of negative reinforcement, not punishment.

Schedules of Reinforcement

A schedule specifies the rule by which responses are reinforced. The two dimensions are ratio (reinforce after a number of responses) versus interval (reinforce the first response after a period of time), each either fixed (constant) or variable (averaged). Continuous reinforcement (every response rewarded) produces fast acquisition but fast extinction; partial (intermittent) reinforcement produces slower acquisition but far greater resistance to extinction, the partial-reinforcement extinction effect.

ScheduleRuleResponse pattern
Fixed ratio (FR)Reinforce after a set number of responses (e.g. every 10th)High rate with a short post-reinforcement pause
Variable ratio (VR)Reinforce after an unpredictable, averaged number of responsesHighest, steadiest rate; most resistant to extinction
Fixed interval (FI)Reinforce first response after a fixed timeScalloped: slow after reward, accelerating before the deadline
Variable interval (VI)Reinforce first response after an averaged, unpredictable timeModerate, very steady rate

Worked examples: a factory paying per piece is FR; a slot machine is VR (you never know which pull pays, so you keep pulling); a weekly pay cheque or a scheduled exam produces FI scalloping (effort spikes just before the deadline); checking for new email is VI (rewards arrive unpredictably over time).

High-yield

Variable ratio produces the highest, steadiest response rate and is the most resistant to extinction. This is the gambling schedule and is the single most-tested fact about reinforcement schedules. Fixed interval produces the characteristic scalloped curve. Ratio schedules (paid by output) always produce higher rates than interval schedules (paid by clock).

Cognitive Learning

The cognitive tradition argued that learning involves internal representations, not just stimulus-response bonds.

  • Edward Tolman: latent learning and cognitive maps. Rats allowed to explore a maze without reward showed no improvement until a reward was introduced, after which they immediately performed as well as rats rewarded all along. The learning had been latent (present but not expressed) and was revealed only when motivated. Tolman argued the rats had built an internal cognitive map of the maze, demonstrating that learning (acquiring knowledge) is separable from performance (showing it).
  • Wolfgang Kohler: insight learning. Studying chimpanzees (notably Sultan), Kohler observed that they solved problems (stacking boxes or joining sticks to reach a banana) suddenly, after a pause, rather than by gradual trial and error. This sudden reorganisation of the problem field is insight (the "aha" experience), a Gestalt phenomenon contrasting with Thorndike's incremental curve.
  • Albert Bandura: social (observational) learning. Bandura's Bobo doll studies (1961) showed children imitate aggression they merely watch, without direct reinforcement. Observational learning runs through four subprocesses:
    • Attention: you must notice and attend to the model's behaviour.
    • Retention: you must store a memory representation of it.
    • Production (motor reproduction): you must be able to reproduce the action.
    • Motivation: you must have a reason to perform it (often via vicarious reinforcement: seeing the model rewarded).
Exam trap

Tolman = latent learning and cognitive maps (learning without reward, performance separable from learning); Kohler = insight (sudden, Gestalt); Bandura = observational learning (Bobo doll, learning by watching). The Bobo doll experiment is Bandura, not Skinner; the sudden "aha" is Kohler, not Tolman.

Memory aid

Bandura's four steps in order spell "A R P M": Attention, Retention, Production, Motivation. You can only motivate (perform) what you can produce, and only produce what you retained, and only retain what you attended to.

C. Perception and Attention

Perception is the organisation and interpretation of sensory input into a meaningful experience. Attention is the selective allocation of limited processing resources. The syllabus targets signal detection, the Gestalt grouping laws, depth cues, the perceptual constancies and illusions, the bottom-up versus top-down debate, and the classic filter models of attention.

Definition

Sensation is the raw transduction of physical energy by the receptors; perception is the brain's organisation and interpretation of that input into a meaningful percept. Bottom-up (data-driven) processing builds the percept from the sensory features upward; top-down (concept-driven) processing uses prior knowledge, context, and expectation to shape what is perceived. The constancies and illusions show top-down processing at work.

Signal Detection Theory (SDT)

SDT models how an observer decides whether a faint signal is present against a background of noise. On every trial the signal is either present or absent, and the observer responds "yes" or "no", giving a 2-by-2 matrix:

Observer says "Yes"Observer says "No"
Signal presentHitMiss
Signal absentFalse alarmCorrect rejection

SDT separates two independent things that older "threshold" theories confused:

  • Sensitivity (d-prime, written d′): how well the observer can actually tell signal from noise, a measure of perceptual or discriminative ability. It is the standardised distance between the noise and signal-plus-noise distributions. Higher d-prime means the two are more separable: more hits with fewer false alarms. Sensitivity reflects the observer's capacity and is unaffected by their willingness to say yes.
  • Response bias (beta or C): where the observer sets their decision criterion: how much evidence they demand before saying "yes". A liberal criterion (says yes readily) yields many hits but also many false alarms; a conservative criterion yields few false alarms but more misses. Bias reflects motivation, expectation, and the payoff structure, not perceptual ability.

Worked example: a radiologist who is told that missing a tumour is far worse than a false alarm will shift to a liberal criterion: more hits, but also more false alarms, with no change whatever in their underlying ability to see tumours (d-prime is unchanged). This is the classic exam scenario, and it isolates a pure change in bias.

Exam trap

The central distinction is sensitivity (d-prime) versus response bias (beta or C). d-prime = how good your senses are at discriminating; beta = how cautious or liberal your decision rule is. If a question says payoffs or expectations shifted the responses without changing perceptual ability, that is a change in bias, not sensitivity. SDT's whole contribution is replacing the single "absolute threshold" with these two separable measures.

Gestalt Principles of Perceptual Grouping

The Gestalt school (Wertheimer, Koffka, Kohler) held that "the whole is different from the sum of its parts": we perceive organised wholes, not isolated elements. The grouping laws describe how we bundle elements into objects:

  • Proximity: elements close together are grouped (dots near each other seen as a cluster).
  • Similarity: elements that look alike (colour, shape) are grouped.
  • Continuity (good continuation): we perceive smooth, continuous lines rather than abrupt changes.
  • Closure: we mentally fill gaps to perceive a complete figure (seeing a full circle from a broken outline).
  • Common fate: elements moving together in the same direction are grouped (a flock of birds).
  • Figure-ground: we segregate a scene into a figure (the object of attention) and a ground (the background); reversible figures such as the Rubin vase show the assignment is unstable and top-down.
  • Pragnanz (the law of good form / simplicity): the overarching principle that we perceive the simplest, most stable, most regular organisation possible. Pragnanz is German for "conciseness" and is the master Gestalt law.
High-yield

Pragnanz (the law of good form / simplicity) is the umbrella Gestalt principle: the mind organises stimuli into the simplest possible form. Common fate is the one based on shared motion. Closure is filling in the gaps. Figure-ground segregation (the Rubin vase) is the most basic organisation of all.

Perceptual Constancies and Illusions

A perceptual constancy is the tendency to perceive an object as stable in size, shape, colour, or brightness despite changes in the retinal image. Size constancy: a person walking away projects a shrinking retinal image, yet you perceive them as a constant size because the brain scales by perceived distance. Shape constancy: a door swinging open projects a trapezoid, yet you still see a rectangular door. Colour and brightness constancy: a white shirt looks white in sunlight and in shadow despite vastly different light reaching the eye.

Illusions are systematic misperceptions, often the price of these otherwise-useful constancy mechanisms. In the Muller-Lyer illusion, two equal lines appear unequal because of inward versus outward arrowheads; the standard explanation (Gregory) is misapplied size constancy: the fins are read as the near and far corners of a room, and the brain "corrects" for an inferred distance that is not really there. The Ponzo illusion (two equal bars on converging railway lines, the upper looking longer) works by the same depth-cue logic. The moon illusion (the moon looks larger near the horizon) is again a constancy effect driven by apparent-distance cues.

Exam trap

Constancies keep perception stable against changing input; illusions are where that machinery misfires. The Muller-Lyer is explained by misapplied size constancy (depth misreading), not by anything in the eye. Do not confuse a constancy (helpful stability) with an illusion (systematic error), and do not attribute illusions to retinal-level processes when the cause is top-down interpretation.

Depth Perception: Monocular versus Binocular Cues

To perceive a three-dimensional world from two-dimensional retinal images, the brain uses depth cues.

  • Monocular cues require only one eye and convey depth in flat pictures: relative size, interposition (overlap), linear perspective (converging lines), texture gradient, aerial perspective (distant objects hazy), light and shadow, relative height, and motion parallax (nearer objects sweep past faster). These are the "pictorial" cues an artist uses.
  • Binocular cues require both eyes: retinal (binocular) disparity (the two eyes get slightly different images; the brain reads the difference as depth, the basis of stereopsis) and convergence (the inward turning of the eyes for near objects, with the muscular feedback signalling distance).
Exam trap

Retinal disparity and convergence are binocular (two eyes). Everything pictorial (interposition, linear perspective, texture gradient, motion parallax) is monocular. The favourite distractor is calling motion parallax binocular: it is monocular.

Attentional Gating: Filter Models

Three classic models debate where in processing unattended information is filtered out. The evidence comes from dichotic listening (different messages to the two ears, with one to be shadowed) and the cocktail-party effect (you notice your own name in an unattended conversation).

  • Broadbent's early-selection filter (1958): a single filter selects one input channel early, on the basis of physical features (such as which ear), before meaning is analysed. The unattended channel is blocked entirely, so its meaning is never processed. This is the strict "bottleneck" model.
  • Treisman's attenuation model (1964): the unattended channel is not blocked but attenuated (turned down, not off). Its meaning is still weakly analysed, so highly salient items (your own name, the "cocktail-party" effect) can break through because they have low recognition thresholds. The filter weakens rather than eliminates.
  • Deutsch and Norman late-selection model: all inputs are fully processed for meaning, and selection happens late, at the stage of response or memory, on the basis of relevance. The bottleneck is near the output, not the input.
Exam trap

Order them by where the filter sits. Broadbent = early (filter before meaning, all-or-none). Treisman = attenuation (turns the volume down, meaning still leaks; explains hearing your name). Deutsch-Norman = late (everything processed, selection at the end). The cocktail-party / own-name finding is the killer evidence against Broadbent and for Treisman.

D. Lifespan Development I: Cognitive and Sociocultural

Developmental psychology asks how thinking, knowledge, and reasoning change with age. The two giants are Piaget (the child as a solitary constructor of knowledge through stages) and Vygotsky (the child as a social apprentice whose mind is built through culture and interaction). The paper tests their concepts and, above all, the contrast between them.

Piaget's Theory of Cognitive Development

Jean Piaget viewed the child as a "little scientist" who actively constructs knowledge. The building blocks are schemas (organised mental frameworks for understanding the world). Development proceeds through three functional processes:

  • Assimilation: fitting new information into an existing schema (a child who knows "dog" calls a cat "dog").
  • Accommodation: altering an existing schema, or creating a new one, to fit new information that will not assimilate (learning that the cat is a separate category).
  • Equilibration: the drive to balance assimilation and accommodation. When new experience cannot be assimilated, the child feels disequilibrium and adjusts schemas to restore equilibrium; this tension is the engine of cognitive growth.
Definition

A schema is an organised mental structure (a unit of knowledge) used to represent and act on the world. Cognitive growth is the progressive revision of schemas through assimilation (using the existing schema) and accommodation (changing it), kept in motion by equilibration (the search for cognitive balance).

Exam trap

Assimilation = fit the world into your head (no schema change). Accommodation = change your head to fit the world (schema change). Calling a cat a "dog" is assimilation; learning cat is a new category is accommodation. Equilibration is the balancing motivation, not a stage.

Piaget proposed four invariant, universal stages:

StageAgeKey achievements and limits
Sensorimotor0 to 2 yearsKnowing through senses and action; reflex modification; primary, secondary, and tertiary circular reactions; attainment of object permanence (objects exist when out of sight)
Preoperational2 to 7 yearsSymbolic function (language, pretend play); egocentrism, animism, centration; cannot conserve
Concrete operational7 to 11 yearsConservation, decentering, reversibility, transitivity, seriation; logic limited to concrete objects
Formal operational11+ yearsAbstract, hypothetical-deductive reasoning; systematic problem-solving

The key concepts within these stages:

  • Object permanence: the sensorimotor milestone that things continue to exist when not perceived. Before it, "out of sight is out of mind"; its absence is tested by hiding a toy. (The classic A-not-B error, where an infant keeps reaching to the old hiding place, is the transitional sign.)
  • Egocentrism: the preoperational inability to take another's viewpoint, demonstrated by the Three Mountains Task, where the child cannot say what a doll on the opposite side of a model sees and reports their own view.
  • Animism: attributing life and feelings to inanimate objects ("the sun is sleeping").
  • Centration: focusing on one salient dimension while ignoring others, which is why preoperational children fail conservation.
  • Conservation: understanding that quantity stays constant despite changes in appearance (the same water poured into a taller, thinner glass is the same amount). Mastered in the concrete operational stage. The worked example to know: pour water from a short wide glass into a tall thin one; the preoperational child, centring on height alone, says there is "more" water, then the concrete-operational child, decentring and using reversibility, says it is the same.
  • Decentering: attending to multiple dimensions at once (height and width), which enables conservation.
  • Reversibility: mentally undoing an operation (the water could be poured back).
  • Transitivity: inferring relations (if A > B and B > C then A > C).
  • Seriation: ordering items along a dimension (arranging sticks by length).
  • Hypothetico-deductive reasoning: the formal-operational ability to generate hypotheses and test them systematically (the pendulum problem).
High-yield

Lock the stage-to-feature pairs: sensorimotor → object permanence; preoperational → egocentrism (Three Mountains) and failure of conservation; concrete operational → conservation, reversibility, seriation; formal operational → abstract hypothetico-deductive reasoning. The ages (0 to 2, 2 to 7, 7 to 11, 11+) are tested verbatim.

Vygotsky's Sociocultural Theory

Lev Vygotsky stressed that cognition is built through social interaction and culture, in contrast to Piaget's solitary little scientist. For Vygotsky, every higher mental function appears first between people (interpsychological) and only later within the child (intrapsychological): development is the internalisation of social and cultural tools, language above all.

  • Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD): the gap between what a child can do independently and what they can do with guidance from a more knowledgeable other. Learning happens in this zone.
  • Scaffolding: the temporary, tailored support an adult or peer provides within the ZPD, gradually withdrawn as competence grows (like a building scaffold removed once the structure stands). The term is Wood, Bruner, and Ross's, but it operationalises Vygotsky's ZPD.
  • Private speech to inner speech: young children talk aloud to themselves to guide action (private speech); this is not egocentric (Piaget's reading) but a tool for self-regulation that is gradually internalised into silent inner speech (verbal thought).
Exam trap

On self-talk, Piaget and Vygotsky disagree: Piaget called children's talking-to-themselves egocentric speech (immature, fading away), whereas Vygotsky called it private speech (a functional self-guidance tool that becomes inner speech). The ZPD and scaffolding are Vygotsky; conservation, object permanence, and the four stages are Piaget. Piaget emphasised the solitary child and biological maturation; Vygotsky emphasised culture and the more-knowledgeable other.

E. Lifespan Development II: Psychosocial, Moral, and Attachment

This subsection covers three lifespan frameworks the paper draws from almost every year: Erikson's eight psychosocial crises (and how they recast Freud), Kohlberg's three levels of moral reasoning (with Gilligan's critique), and the attachment work of Bowlby and Ainsworth. Each is heavily table-tested.

Erikson's Eight Psychosocial Stages

Erik Erikson recast Freud's psychosexual stages as a lifelong sequence of eight psychosocial "crises", each a tension between two outcomes; resolving each yields a basic virtue (ego strength). The crucial departures from Freud: the stages run across the whole lifespan (not stopping at adolescence), the driver is social and relational (not libidinal), and the ego is given a constructive, identity-building role.

StageConflictAgeVirtue
1Trust vs. MistrustInfancy (0 to 1)Hope
2Autonomy vs. Shame/DoubtEarly childhood (1 to 3)Will
3Initiative vs. GuiltPreschool (3 to 6)Purpose
4Industry vs. InferioritySchool age (6 to 12)Competence
5Identity vs. Role ConfusionAdolescence (12 to 18)Fidelity
6Intimacy vs. IsolationYoung adulthoodLove
7Generativity vs. StagnationMiddle adulthoodCare
8Ego Integrity vs. DespairLate adulthoodWisdom
High-yield

The virtues in order: Hope, Will, Purpose, Competence, Fidelity, Love, Care, Wisdom. Identity vs. Role Confusion (virtue: Fidelity) is the adolescent stage and the most-tested one. Generativity vs. Stagnation (Care) is middle adulthood; Integrity vs. Despair (Wisdom) is old age.

Exam trap

The favourite errors are mismatching the virtue to its stage (Fidelity belongs to Identity vs. Role Confusion, Care to Generativity vs. Stagnation, Wisdom to Integrity vs. Despair) and confusing Erikson's psychosocial stages with Freud's psychosexual ones. Erikson's are eight, lifelong, and social; Freud's are five (oral, anal, phallic, latency, genital), childhood-bounded, and libidinal.

Memory aid

Virtues mnemonic "Hope Will Purpose Competence Fidelity, Love Care Wisdom": think of a person hoping, then willing, with a purpose and competence, staying faithful, finding love, giving care, ending in wisdom.

Kohlberg's Theory of Moral Development

Lawrence Kohlberg studied moral reasoning (the justification, not the verdict) using moral dilemmas, most famously the Heinz dilemma: should a man steal an unaffordable drug to save his dying wife? He identified three levels, each with two stages.

  • Pre-conventional level (morality is external, based on consequences to the self):
    • Stage 1: Punishment and obedience orientation: right is avoiding punishment ("Heinz should not steal, he will be jailed").
    • Stage 2: Instrumental hedonism (naive egoism): right is what serves one's own needs, with simple exchange ("steal it because he needs his wife", a "you scratch my back" morality).
  • Conventional level (morality is conformity to social roles and rules):
    • Stage 3: Good-boy / nice-girl orientation: right is gaining approval and being seen as nice ("a good husband would steal").
    • Stage 4: Law-and-order orientation: right is upholding laws and social duty ("stealing is against the law").
  • Post-conventional level (morality is based on abstract principles transcending laws):
    • Stage 5: Social contract orientation: laws are social agreements that can be changed for the greater good; rights matter.
    • Stage 6: Universal ethical principles: self-chosen, universal principles of justice and human dignity override unjust laws ("life is worth more than property").

Carol Gilligan's critique: Kohlberg's stages were derived from male samples and rank an abstract ethics of justice (rules, rights) as the highest morality. Gilligan argued women more often reason from an ethics of care (responsibility, relationships, avoiding harm to others), which Kohlberg's scheme wrongly scores as less mature. The difference is one of orientation, not inferiority: a gender bias in the theory, not in women.

Exam trap

Kohlberg scores the reasoning, not the answer: two people who both say "steal" can be at very different stages. Pre-conventional = self (punishment, reward); conventional = others (approval, law); post-conventional = principles. Gilligan's contribution is the ethics of care versus ethics of justice gender critique. Do not confuse Stage 4 (law-and-order, obey the law because it is the law) with Stage 5 (social contract, laws can be changed for the greater good).

Attachment Theory

John Bowlby argued attachment is an innate, evolved behavioural system: infants are biologically predisposed to form a bond with a primary caregiver because proximity to a protector enhanced survival. From early interactions the infant builds internal working models: mental representations of the self (am I worthy of care?), of others (are they reliable?), and of relationships, which guide expectations for life. Harry Harlow's rhesus-monkey studies supplied the experimental backbone: infant monkeys preferred a soft cloth "mother" that provided contact comfort over a wire mother that provided milk, refuting the idea that attachment is just learnt feeding (the "cupboard-love" theory).

Mary Ainsworth operationalised attachment quality with the Strange Situation, a standardised laboratory procedure of eight episodes involving separations from and reunions with the caregiver, plus a stranger. The infant's behaviour, especially at reunion, classifies the attachment:

PatternOn separationOn reunion
SecureDistressed (caregiver is a secure base for exploration)Seeks contact and is easily comforted, then resumes play
Insecure-anxious/ambivalent (resistant)Highly distressedSeeks contact but resists it: clings and pushes away, angry, hard to soothe
Insecure-avoidantLittle or no distressAvoids and ignores the caregiver; treats stranger and caregiver similarly
Disorganised/disorientedNo coherent strategyConfused, contradictory, freezing or apprehensive behaviour

The first three were Ainsworth's; the disorganised/disoriented category was added later by Mary Main and Judith Solomon for infants who showed no consistent strategy (approaching then freezing, or appearing fearful of the caregiver). It is most associated with frightening caregiving, maltreatment, or unresolved parental trauma.

Definition

The secure base is Ainsworth's term for the caregiver as a platform from which the infant confidently explores and to which it returns for comfort. The two diagnostic markers in the Strange Situation are the infant's use of the caregiver as a secure base for exploration and its behaviour at reunion; reunion is where the pattern is read.

Exam trap

The reunion behaviour is the key. The avoidant infant shows little distress and ignores/avoids the caregiver at reunion. The ambivalent/resistant infant is very distressed and seeks but angrily resists comfort. Do not swap them. Disorganised is the Main and Solomon addition, linked to trauma. Harlow shows contact comfort beats feeding; do not credit the Strange Situation to Bowlby (it is Ainsworth's).

F. Social Psychology: Attribution, Influence, and Groups

Social psychology studies how people think about, influence, and relate to one another. The paper concentrates on attribution and its biases, cognitive dissonance, the conformity and obedience classics, compliance techniques, group-performance effects, and the bystander effect. These are almost all anchored to named experiments, so learn the study with the concept.

Attribution Theory and Biases

Attribution is how people explain the causes of behaviour, assigning it to internal/dispositional causes (the person's traits) or external/situational causes (the circumstances).

  • Fritz Heider, the founder, described the "naive psychologist" who routinely sorts causes into internal versus external locus.
  • Harold Kelley's covariation model: to attribute a behaviour, people assess whether it covaries with the person, the stimulus, or the occasion across three kinds of information:
    • Consensus: do other people behave this way toward the same stimulus? (high = others do too).
    • Distinctiveness: does the person behave this way only toward this stimulus or toward many? (high = only this one).
    • Consistency: does the person behave this way toward this stimulus across time and situations? (high = always).
    Low consensus, low distinctiveness, high consistency → internal (dispositional) attribution. High consensus, high distinctiveness, high consistency → external (situational) attribution.

Worked example of Kelley's model: Priya laughs at a particular comedian. If no one else laughs at him (low consensus), she laughs at no other comedian (high distinctiveness), and she always laughs at him (high consistency), you attribute it externally to something about the comedian. If everyone laughs at him, she does not laugh at most things, but laughs at him every time, that pattern (high consensus, high distinctiveness, high consistency) again points external; reverse consensus and distinctiveness and the attribution swings to Priya's disposition.

The systematic biases:

  • Fundamental Attribution Error (FAE): the tendency, when explaining others' behaviour, to overweight dispositional causes and underweight situational ones (assuming a late colleague is lazy, not stuck in traffic).
  • Actor-observer bias: we attribute our own behaviour to the situation but others' identical behaviour to their disposition (I'm late because of traffic; you're late because you're disorganised).
  • Self-serving bias: we take credit for successes (internal: "I'm skilled") but blame failures on external factors ("the exam was unfair"), protecting self-esteem.
  • Just-world hypothesis: the belief that the world is fair and people get what they deserve, which leads to blaming the victim (assuming a misfortune was earned).
Exam trap

FAE is about explaining others (over-blame disposition). The actor-observer bias adds the self/other asymmetry (situational for self, dispositional for others). Self-serving bias is the success/failure asymmetry (credit me, blame the world). These three are constantly cross-substituted as distractors.

Festinger's Cognitive Dissonance Theory

Leon Festinger (1957) proposed that holding two inconsistent cognitions (beliefs, attitudes, or a belief and a behaviour) produces an uncomfortable state of cognitive dissonance, a drive-like tension people are motivated to reduce. They reduce it by changing one cognition (attitude change), changing the behaviour, or adding consonant cognitions (rationalisation).

The classic demonstration is Festinger and Carlsmith's forced-compliance / one-dollar versus twenty-dollar study (1959). Participants did a boring task, then were paid either 1 dollar or 20 dollars to tell the next participant it was enjoyable. Counter-intuitively, the 1-dollar group later rated the task as more enjoyable. With 20 dollars, the large payment is sufficient external justification for lying, so no dissonance arises. With only 1 dollar, there is insufficient justification, so the dissonance between "I said it was fun" and "it was boring" is resolved by genuinely changing the attitude ("it actually was a bit fun").

Definition

Cognitive dissonance is the aversive tension arising from holding two psychologically inconsistent cognitions. The drive to reduce it can change attitudes to match behaviour, which is why dissonance theory predicts attitude change follows behaviour (the opposite of the common-sense direction).

High-yield

In the forced-compliance study, the smaller reward (1 dollar) produced the greater attitude change, because insufficient external justification forces internal (attitude) change to reduce dissonance. This counter-intuitive direction is the whole point and is heavily tested.

Social Influence: Conformity and Obedience

Solomon Asch's conformity studies (1951): participants judged which of three comparison lines matched a standard, an easy perceptual task. When confederates unanimously gave an obviously wrong answer, about a third (roughly 33 to 37 percent) of responses conformed to the wrong majority. Asch showed conformity to group pressure even against clear perceptual evidence. Two mechanisms drive conformity:

  • Informational social influence: conforming because you believe the group is right (you use others as a source of information), leading to genuine private acceptance; strongest in ambiguous situations.
  • Normative social influence: conforming to be liked / accepted and to avoid rejection, leading to public compliance without private change; this drove most of Asch's effect (the task was unambiguous).

Stanley Milgram's obedience studies (1963): participants ("teachers") were ordered by an experimenter to deliver escalating "shocks" to a learner for wrong answers, up to a labelled 450 volts. About 65 percent obeyed to the maximum, demonstrating that ordinary people obey a legitimate authority even against their conscience. Key modifiers reduced obedience:

  • Proximity of the victim (obedience fell as the learner became more present) and of the authority (obedience fell when the experimenter gave orders by phone).
  • Prestige / legitimacy of the setting and authority (obedience fell when moved from prestigious Yale to a run-down office, or when the experimenter wore ordinary clothes).

Milgram explained obedience by the agentic state: the person stops seeing themselves as an autonomous agent and becomes an instrument carrying out another's wishes, shifting responsibility to the authority.

Exam trap

Asch = conformity to a peer group (no one orders you); about a third conform. Milgram = obedience to an authority figure (a direct command); about 65 percent fully obey. For Asch's two motives: informational = "they must be right" (ambiguity, private acceptance); normative = "I want to fit in" (public compliance). Do not confuse the two influence types, and do not swap the 33 percent (Asch conformity) with the 65 percent (Milgram obedience).

Compliance Techniques

  • Foot-in-the-door: get agreement to a small request first, then the larger target request is more likely accepted (consistency with the prior small commitment).
  • Door-in-the-face: make a large request expected to be refused, then the smaller real request looks reasonable by contrast (reciprocal concession).
  • Low-balling: get a commitment to an attractive offer, then reveal a higher cost; people often stay committed despite the worsened terms.
Memory aid

Foot-in-the-door goes small then big (you let the foot in, then the whole person). Door-in-the-face goes big then small (the big request gets the door slammed, the small one then gets in). Low-balling = bait with a low price, then raise it.

Group Performance and Decision Traps

  • Social facilitation: the presence of others improves performance on simple or well-learned tasks (Triplett's cyclists rode faster with others).
  • Social inhibition: the presence of others impairs performance on complex or novel tasks. Zajonc explained both: others raise arousal, which strengthens the dominant response, which is correct on easy tasks and wrong on hard ones.
  • Social loafing: people exert less effort when their contribution is pooled and not individually identifiable (Ringelmann's rope-pulling), through diffusion of responsibility.
  • Groupthink (Janis): in a cohesive group, the desire for harmony and consensus suppresses dissent and critical evaluation, producing poor decisions (symptoms include illusions of invulnerability, self-censorship, and pressure on dissenters).
  • Group polarisation: group discussion shifts members toward a more extreme version of their initial leaning (a cautious group gets more cautious; a risky group takes the "risky shift").
Exam trap

Social facilitation helps on easy/well-learned tasks; the same audience hurts on hard/novel tasks (social inhibition). Social loafing is reduced effort when pooled; groupthink is suppressed dissent for harmony; group polarisation is the shift to extremes. Polarisation makes groups more extreme, not more moderate. Zajonc's mechanism is "arousal strengthens the dominant response", which is why the same crowd can help or hurt depending on task difficulty.

The Bystander Effect

Darley and Latane (1968), prompted by the Kitty Genovese case, found that the more bystanders present at an emergency, the less likely any one of them is to help, and the slower the help. Two processes drive it:

  • Diffusion of responsibility: with others present, each person feels less personal obligation to act ("someone else will").
  • Pluralistic ignorance: people look to others to define the situation; if everyone stays calm (each thinking the others know it is not an emergency), the group collectively misreads the event as a non-emergency.
High-yield

The bystander effect: helping decreases as the number of bystanders increases. Mechanisms: diffusion of responsibility (no one feels obliged) and pluralistic ignorance (everyone's calm cues read as "no emergency"). Latane and Darley's five-step decision model (notice, interpret as emergency, take responsibility, decide how to help, act) is the supporting framework.

G. Personality: Trait, Type, and Social-Cognitive Theories

Personality is the characteristic, relatively enduring pattern of thinking, feeling, and behaving that distinguishes one person from another. Trait theories describe its stable dimensions; type and biological theories root them in temperament; social-cognitive theories stress the interaction of person and situation. The paper tests theorist-to-construct pairings above all, so keep each name welded to its idea.

Definition

A trait is a relatively stable, enduring disposition to behave in a consistent way across situations and time (the nomothetic, dimensional approach). A type is a discrete category a person belongs to (the older, typological approach). Modern personality psychology favours continuous traits over discrete types.

Trait Theories

  • Gordon Allport distinguished three levels of traits by how pervasively they shape a person:
    • Cardinal traits: a single dominant trait that organises a whole life (rare; "Machiavellian", "narcissistic"). Most people have none.
    • Central traits: the handful of core characteristics that describe a person (honest, kind, anxious): the basic units of personality.
    • Secondary traits: situation-specific preferences and attitudes that appear only in particular contexts (gets impatient in queues).
  • Raymond Cattell used factor analysis to reduce surface descriptions to underlying dimensions:
    • Surface traits: clusters of observable behaviours that go together.
    • Source traits: the deeper, underlying factors (the "source" of surface traits) isolated by factor analysis. Cattell identified 16 source traits, operationalised in the 16PF (Sixteen Personality Factor) Questionnaire.
  • Hans Eysenck's PEN model proposed a small set of biologically based super-factors:
    • Psychoticism (toughminded, aggressive, antisocial), Extraversion (sociable, outgoing), and Neuroticism (emotionally unstable, anxious).
    • Eysenck rooted Extraversion in cortical arousal: introverts have higher baseline arousal via the Reticular Activating System (RAS), so they are already over-stimulated and seek quiet; extraverts are chronically under-aroused and seek stimulation. Neuroticism he linked to a labile autonomic (limbic) nervous system.
  • Costa and McCrae's Five-Factor Model, measured by the NEO-PI-R, is the modern consensus: five broad dimensions, the OCEAN traits:
    • Openness to experience (imaginative, curious), Conscientiousness (organised, dependable), Extraversion (sociable, energetic), Agreeableness (cooperative, warm), Neuroticism (anxious, emotionally reactive).

How it is tested: the paper asks you to count and name (Eysenck = 3, Cattell = 16, Costa and McCrae = 5), to attach the instrument to the theorist (16PF = Cattell, NEO-PI-R = Costa and McCrae, EPQ = Eysenck), and to recall Eysenck's counter-intuitive arousal claim.

Memory aid

The Big Five spell OCEAN (Openness, Conscientiousness, Extraversion, Agreeableness, Neuroticism). Eysenck's three spell PEN (Psychoticism, Extraversion, Neuroticism). Cattell's number is 16 (the 16PF). Allport's three are Cardinal, Central, Secondary.

Exam trap

Counter-intuitively, Eysenck held that introverts have higher baseline cortical arousal (via the RAS), so they avoid extra stimulation, while extraverts are under-aroused and seek it. Do not say extraverts are the highly aroused ones. Cattell = 16PF and the surface/source trait distinction; Costa and McCrae = the Big Five (NEO-PI-R, OCEAN); Eysenck = PEN. Do not confuse Cattell's 16 source traits with the Big Five's 5 factors.

Psychodynamic and Humanistic Glances

Two further families round out the personality menu and supply distractors. The psychodynamic tradition (Freud) explains personality through unconscious conflict among the id (pleasure principle, instinctual drives), ego (reality principle, the executive mediator), and superego (the internalised moral standard), with defence mechanisms managing anxiety. The humanistic tradition rejects both psychodynamic determinism and behaviourism: Carl Rogers centres the drive toward self-actualisation, the gap between the real self and the ideal self (incongruence), and the therapeutic value of unconditional positive regard; Abraham Maslow arranges motives in a hierarchy of needs (physiological, safety, love and belonging, esteem, self-actualisation), the lower needs being prepotent. These are the optimistic, growth-oriented "third force" against the deterministic first two forces.

Exam trap

Keep the structural model straight: the ego runs on the reality principle (mediator), the id on the pleasure principle, the superego is conscience. Self-actualisation appears in both Maslow (top of the hierarchy) and Rogers (the actualising tendency), but unconditional positive regard and real-versus-ideal-self incongruence are specifically Rogers.

Social-Cognitive Theories

  • Albert Bandura's reciprocal determinism: behaviour is not caused by the person or the situation alone; instead three factors continuously influence each other in interlocking loops: behaviour, personal/cognitive factors (beliefs, expectations), and the environment. Each shapes and is shaped by the others (a triadic, two-way causation).
  • Self-efficacy: Bandura's term for a person's belief in their own capacity to execute the actions needed to achieve a goal. High self-efficacy predicts greater effort, persistence, and resilience; it is built through mastery experiences, vicarious experience (modelling), social persuasion, and managing physiological arousal.
  • Julian Rotter's locus of control: a generalised expectancy about the source of reinforcement. People with an internal locus believe outcomes follow from their own actions and effort; those with an external locus believe outcomes are governed by luck, fate, or powerful others. Internals tend to take more responsibility and cope more actively.
High-yield

Reciprocal determinism = the triadic, mutual causation of behaviour, person (cognition), and environment (Bandura). Self-efficacy = belief in one's capability to succeed (Bandura). Locus of control = internal (I control outcomes) versus external (luck/fate controls them) (Rotter). Keep self-efficacy (Bandura) and locus of control (Rotter) attributed to the right theorist.

Exam trap

Self-efficacy and locus of control sound similar but differ: self-efficacy is task-specific belief in your ability to perform (Bandura); locus of control is a global expectancy about where reinforcement comes from, internal versus external (Rotter). The paper will offer the wrong theorist for each as a distractor.

Appendix A · PYQ Depth: Off Your Own Paper

Everything before this part taught the syllabus from the ground up. This part is different: it is the highest-signal section in the book because it is not scraped from forums or inferred from a syllabus. It is your own Master Concept List recalled straight off the Amity entrance you actually sat. That makes it ground truth about the real depth the paper goes to: the named theorists, the exact experiments, the dated firsts, and the trap pairs the examiners like. Each concept below is widened the way you learn best: the answer, the why it is the answer, and the distractor they dangle next to it. Where a concept was already taught in full earlier, the cross-reference tells you where to go deep; here it is compressed into exactly the form the paper tested it.

How to use this part

Read it last, after the teaching parts, and use it as your final-week drill. Cover the answer column and recite. If any item here feels shaky, jump back to the part that taught it (the links are in the text) and re-read that section. These are the concepts the paper has already proven it cares about, so they are the safest marks in the book.

A · Social & Group Dynamics (off your paper)

Full teaching in Section 6. The paper tested these as single-fact and trap-pair items.

Kurt Lewin carries two "father of" titles. Kurt Lewin (1890 to 1947) was a German-American psychologist who is named father of modern social psychology and father of action research. His central idea was that behaviour cannot be predicted from the person alone or the situation alone, but from their interaction, which he compressed into the formula B = f(P, E): behaviour is a function of the person and the environment, considered together as one psychological field. This is field theory, borrowed deliberately from physics: a person sits in a "life space" of forces (driving forces pushing toward a goal, restraining forces holding back), and you analyse any situation by mapping those forces, a method he called force-field analysis. From the same field thinking he derived his model of planned organisational change, unfreeze (loosen the old equilibrium) then change (move) then refreeze (lock the new state in); the action research cycle of Plan then Act then Observe then Reflect; and his classic studies on leadership climate, which isolated three styles, autocratic, democratic, and laissez-faire, with the democratic climate producing the best morale and sustained output.
Trap: they offer Gordon Allport or Floyd Allport for "father of social psychology." On this paper Lewin is the one tagged, and he owns action research outright; do not surrender the action-research label to anyone else.

Norman Triplett (1898), the fishing-reel experiment. This is the first social-psychology experiment ever conducted, and both the date and the apparatus are testable. Triplett, an American psychologist, had noticed that racing cyclists rode faster when paced by or competing against others than when riding alone against the clock. To test this in the laboratory he had children wind line onto a fishing reel, sometimes alone and sometimes alongside another child doing the same task, and found they wound faster in company. This is the founding demonstration of social facilitation, the improvement of performance in the presence of others, though Triplett himself framed it as "dynamogenesis," a competitive instinct releasing latent energy.
Trap: the date (1898) and the apparatus (fishing reel) are both directly examined. Do not confuse it with Ringelmann's rope-pulling study (1913), which demonstrated social loafing, the opposite effect, and was actually conducted earlier in data but published as the loafing phenomenon.

Social facilitation vs social inhibition, Zajonc's drive theory. Why does an audience sometimes help performance and sometimes wreck it? Robert Zajonc (1965) resolved the apparent contradiction with a drive theory built on three steps. First, the mere presence of others raises general physiological arousal (drive). Second, heightened arousal strengthens the dominant response, the response most likely to be emitted in that situation. Third, whether that helps depends entirely on the task. On an easy or well-learned task the dominant response is the correct one, so arousal pushes performance up: this is social facilitation. On a hard, novel, or not-yet-mastered task the dominant response is usually wrong, so arousal pushes performance down: this is social inhibition (sometimes called social interference). The single insight to carry into the exam is that the audience never simply "helps" or "hurts"; it amplifies whatever response is already dominant.
Trap: "the presence of others improves performance" stated baldly is wrong, because it only boosts the dominant response, which fails on hard tasks. The rival explanation the examiners may name is Cottrell's evaluation-apprehension theory, which argues it is not mere presence but the learned expectation of being judged that drives the arousal.

Definition

The dominant response is the response that is most probable for a person in a given situation, given their habits and skill level. Zajonc's whole account rests on it: arousal does not improve or impair performance directly, it strengthens the dominant response, which is correct on easy tasks (so performance rises) and wrong on hard tasks (so performance falls).

Social loafing vs social striving. Social loafing is the tendency for individuals to exert less effort when working in a group than when working alone, because individual contribution is pooled and unidentifiable, so responsibility diffuses and motivation slackens. Its founding evidence is the Ringelmann effect: in rope-pulling, the total force of a group was less than the sum of the members' individual maximum pulls, and the per-person force fell as group size rose. Loafing was long treated as universal, but the cross-cultural work is the advanced half they test. In some collectivist cultures the effect can reverse into social striving (social compensation), where effort rises in the group because the group's success is internalised as a personal and social value, especially when the task matters and individual slacking would let the team down. Loafing is shrunk wherever contribution is made identifiable, the task feels meaningful, or members value the group's goal.
Trap: assuming loafing is universal and human-nature-fixed. The collectivist reversal into striving is precisely the nuance the paper rewards; Ringelmann is an individualist-culture finding, not a law.

Tuckman's stages of group development. Bruce Tuckman (1965) proposed that small groups pass through a fixed developmental sequence: Forming, Storming, Norming, Performing, to which he and Mary Ann Jensen added a fifth stage, Adjourning, in 1977. In Forming members are polite, dependent on the leader, and orienting to the task. In Storming, stage 2, conflict erupts: members jostle for status, resist the structure, and challenge each other and the leader; this is the stage your list flagged specifically, and it is the one most often blanked out in an exam item. In Norming the group settles, agrees roles and rules (norms), and cohesion grows. In Performing the group works productively and autonomously on the task. In Adjourning the group disbands and members disengage.
Trap: "Adjourning" was added later (1977), so older textbooks stop at four stages; and Storming is stage 2, not stage 1. A favourite distractor places conflict at the very start, but Forming (the polite orientation) comes first.

Conformity, obedience, and the bystander effect (the named studies). Social-dynamics items on this paper cluster around three classic experiments whose author, finding, and apparatus are each examinable. Solomon Asch (1951) studied conformity using a line-judgement task: a single naive participant gave answers after a group of confederates who deliberately called an obviously shorter line "equal," and about a third of responses conformed to the wrong majority, showing normative social influence (going along to be accepted) even against the plain evidence of the eyes. Stanley Milgram (1963) studied obedience to authority: participants instructed by an experimenter to deliver escalating "shocks" to a confederate continued to the maximum 450 volts at strikingly high rates, demonstrating how ordinary people defer to legitimate authority (Milgram explained it as a shift into an "agentic state"). Latane and Darley (1968) studied the bystander effect, in which the presence of others reduces the likelihood any individual helps in an emergency, through diffusion of responsibility and pluralistic ignorance; it was framed around the Kitty Genovese case.
Trap: match the name to the phenomenon and do not let them slide: Asch = conformity (lines), Milgram = obedience (shocks), Latane & Darley = bystander/diffusion. Asch is normative influence; Sherif's autokinetic study is the informational conformity classic offered as the distractor.

Memory aid

Tuckman in order: "Form a band, Storm off in a row, agree the Norms, Perform the gig, then Adjourn to the pub." Five stages, the fifth bolted on in 1977. For Lewin, "B = f(P,E)" and "unfreeze, change, refreeze" are the two formulas worth holding verbatim. For the named studies: "Asch sees lines, Milgram makes shocks, the Bystanders do nothing."

B · Motivation & Organisational (off your paper)

Full teaching in Section 6.

Herzberg, two-factor theory. Frederick Herzberg (1959) interviewed accountants and engineers about times they felt exceptionally good or bad at work, and found that the causes of satisfaction and the causes of dissatisfaction were different sets of factors, not opposite ends of one scale. Hygiene factors (also called maintenance factors) are extrinsic conditions: pay, company policy, working conditions, job security, supervision, and interpersonal relations. When these are poor they cause dissatisfaction; when they are good they merely remove dissatisfaction, returning the worker to a neutral, un-dissatisfied state, but they do not motivate. Motivators are intrinsic to the work itself: achievement, recognition, responsibility, advancement, growth, and the nature of the work. These are what produce genuine satisfaction and drive effort. The decisive structural claim is that the opposite of dissatisfaction is not satisfaction but no dissatisfaction, and the opposite of satisfaction is no satisfaction; the two run on separate continua. The practical prescription is job enrichment, loading the work with more motivators rather than merely improving hygiene.
Trap: treating "remove dissatisfaction equals create satisfaction." It does not, and that error is the entire point the theory was built to correct. Pay and conditions are hygiene, not motivators.

McClelland, need for achievement (nAch). David McClelland's acquired needs theory (also called the three needs theory or learned needs theory) holds that three motives are learned through life experience and culture rather than innate, and that one tends to dominate in any given person: the need for achievement (nAch), the need for affiliation (nAff), and the need for power (nPow). People high in nAch prefer tasks of moderate difficulty (where success reflects skill, not luck or impossibility), seek personal responsibility for outcomes, and want concrete, frequent feedback on how they are doing. Because these motives are largely unconscious, McClelland measured them with the Thematic Apperception Test (TAT), a projective test in which the respondent invents stories about ambiguous pictures and the achievement, affiliation, or power imagery in those stories is scored.
Trap: confusing it with Maslow's hierarchy (a fixed innate ladder of needs, a different model entirely) or with Henry Murray, who originated both the broad list of psychogenic needs that McClelland drew his three from and co-created the TAT itself. McClelland built on Murray; do not credit the whole framework to either name alone.

Maslow's hierarchy and the Maslow vs Herzberg link. Abraham Maslow's hierarchy of needs arranges human motives in an ascending order in which lower needs must be substantially met before higher ones become active: physiological (food, water, sleep), then safety (security, stability), then love and belonging (affiliation), then esteem (recognition, status), and at the apex self-actualisation (realising one's full potential), with self-transcendence added in his later writing. The first four are deficiency needs (D-needs), motivating by their absence; self-actualisation is a growth or being need (B-need) that is never fully satiated. The examined connection is the mapping onto Herzberg: Maslow's lower deficiency needs correspond broadly to Herzberg's hygiene factors, and the upper esteem and self-actualisation needs correspond to Herzberg's motivators.
Trap: Maslow's pyramid is a single ascending scale of innate needs; Herzberg's is two separate dimensions; McClelland's three needs are learned. The paper sets these three theorists against each other, so keep "Maslow = ladder, Herzberg = two factors, McClelland = three learned needs" cleanly separated.

High-yield

Herzberg: hygiene factors (pay, conditions, security, supervision) prevent dissatisfaction but never motivate; motivators (achievement, recognition, the work, growth) create satisfaction. They are separate dimensions. McClelland: three learned needs (achievement, affiliation, power), high-nAch seeks moderate risk plus feedback, measured by the TAT, which traces back to Murray. Maslow: a five-level innate hierarchy, physiological to self-actualisation, deficiency needs below and growth need at the top.

C · Developmental & Attachment (off your paper)

Full teaching in Section 6.

Bowlby & Ainsworth, attachment. The division of labour between these two names is the testable point. John Bowlby built the theory: attachment is an innate, evolved behavioural system whose biological function is to keep the infant close to a caregiver for protection, selected because proximity raised survival in ancestral environments. From repeated interactions the infant forms an internal working model, a mental template of whether the self is worthy of care and whether others are reliable, which then guides relationships across the lifespan. Bowlby also described attachment as developing through phases and emphasised that prolonged separation or loss in early childhood (maternal deprivation) had lasting effects. Mary Ainsworth supplied the method that operationalised and measured Bowlby's theory: the Strange Situation, a structured laboratory procedure of eight episodes involving separations from and reunions with the caregiver and the entry of a stranger, scored chiefly on the infant's reunion behaviour.
Trap: crediting the Strange Situation to Bowlby. The theory is Bowlby's; the Strange Situation procedure is Ainsworth's method. A clean way to hold it: Bowlby theorised, Ainsworth experimentally classified.

The four attachment styles. Ainsworth's Strange Situation originally yielded three infant classifications. Secure (Type B): the infant explores using the caregiver as a base, is distressed at separation, and is readily comforted on reunion (the marker of healthy attachment). Insecure-avoidant (Type A): the infant shows little distress at separation and actively avoids or ignores the caregiver on reunion, having learned that bids for comfort are rebuffed. Insecure-ambivalent or resistant (Type C): the infant is intensely distressed at separation and on reunion seeks contact yet simultaneously resists and cannot be soothed (clingy and angry at once). A fourth category, disorganised or disoriented (Type D), was added later by Mary Main and Judith Solomon for infants showing contradictory, fearful, or freezing behaviours that fit none of the original three, often linked to frightening or maltreating care.
Trap: the original Strange Situation classified three; disorganised is the add-on (Main & Solomon). Do not confuse these infant types with the adult attachment relabels (secure, dismissing, preoccupied, fearful-avoidant), which come from a separate framework (the Adult Attachment Interview and Bartholomew's model).

High-yield

Attachment owners: Bowlby theory, Ainsworth Strange Situation, Harlow contact comfort (cloth monkey), Lorenz imprinting, Main & Solomon the disorganised fourth type. Original Strange Situation = three types (secure, avoidant, ambivalent); the fourth (disorganised) was added later. Harlow proved attachment runs on comfort, not feeding.

Harlow's monkeys and contact comfort. Harry Harlow's surrogate-mother experiments (1958) are the most-cited attachment evidence on these papers, because they refuted the older "cupboard love" view that infants bond to whoever feeds them. Infant rhesus monkeys were raised with two artificial mothers: a bare wire mother fitted with a feeding bottle, and a soft cloth-covered mother with no food. The infants clung to the cloth mother almost all the time and went to the wire mother only briefly to feed, returning at once to the cloth. When frightened they ran to the cloth mother, and used her as a secure base from which to explore. The conclusion, contact comfort, is that attachment is driven by tactile, comforting contact rather than by feeding, which is exactly the evidence Bowlby drew on.
Trap: the answer is contact comfort, not nourishment; the wire mother provides the food but the infant bonds to the soft cloth one. Do not credit this to Bowlby or Ainsworth (it is Harlow), and do not confuse it with Lorenz's imprinting in geese, the other ethological attachment classic offered alongside.

Definition

An internal working model is the mental representation of self and others that an infant builds from early attachment experiences (am I worthy of care; are others reliable), and which then shapes expectations and behaviour in later relationships. It is the mechanism by which Bowlby's early bond is carried forward into adult life.

D · Memory & Cognition (off your paper)

Full teaching in Section 6.

Proactive vs retroactive interference. Interference theory explains forgetting not as decay over time but as competition between memories, and the two directions are routinely flipped in distractors. Proactive interference is when OLD learning blocks the retrieval of NEW learning: your long-memorised old phone number keeps intruding when you try to recall the new one. The old material "proacts" forward in time onto the new. Retroactive interference is the reverse, when NEW learning blocks the retrieval of OLD: after drilling the new number, you can no longer dredge up the old one, because the new material "reaches back" onto the old. The key to keeping them straight is the direction of the arrow in time: in proactive interference the disruption travels forward (earlier disrupts later); in retroactive interference it travels backward (later disrupts earlier).
Trap: they simply swap the two definitions and hope you pick the wrong one. Mnemonic to lock it: PRO = forward in time (the old pushes forward onto the new); RETRO = backward in time (the new reaches back onto the old). "Retro" means going back, so the new harms the old.

Johari window. A model of self-awareness and interpersonal disclosure devised by Joseph Luft and Harrington Ingham (1955); the name is simply "Jo" plus "Hari." It maps what is known and unknown about a person across two axes (known to self versus not known to self; known to others versus not known to others) into four quadrants. The Open (arena) quadrant is known to self and known to others. The Blind (blind spot) quadrant is known to others but not to the self (your unconscious mannerisms). The Hidden (facade) quadrant is known to the self but kept from others (private feelings). The Unknown quadrant is known to neither (latent potential, repressed material). The model is dynamic: feedback from others shrinks the Blind area (they tell you what you could not see), and self-disclosure shrinks the Hidden area (you reveal what you were holding back), both enlarging the Open arena and improving communication.
Trap: matching the quadrant to who-knows-what is the whole trick. Open = known to self and others; Blind = others know, you do not; Hidden = you know, others do not; Unknown = nobody knows. Feedback reduces the Blind spot; disclosure reduces the Hidden.

The Atkinson-Shiffrin model and the memory stores. The multi-store (modal) model of Atkinson and Shiffrin (1968) is the spine most memory questions hang from, so its three stores and their parameters are examinable. Sensory memory holds an unprocessed sensory trace very briefly: the visual register (iconic memory, demonstrated by Sperling) lasts well under a second, and the auditory register (echoic memory) a few seconds. Attended material passes to short-term memory (STM), of limited capacity (Miller's "magical number seven, plus or minus two" chunks, 1956) and a duration of roughly 15 to 30 seconds unless rehearsed, with loss by decay and displacement. Rehearsal transfers material to long-term memory (LTM), of effectively unlimited capacity and duration. Baddeley and Hitch (1974) later replaced the passive STM with a working memory model of an active central executive directing a phonological loop and a visuospatial sketchpad (the episodic buffer added in 2000).
Trap: capacity belongs to STM (seven plus or minus two chunks, Miller); the very brief unlimited-capacity store is sensory memory, not STM. Iconic = visual, echoic = auditory; do not swap them. Working memory (Baddeley) is the active update of STM, not a synonym for LTM.

Memory aid

Johari quadrants by who knows: Open = both know; Blind = only others know (it is your blind spot); Hidden = only you know (your facade); Unknown = neither knows. "Feedback fights the Blind spot; disclosure dissolves the hidden." For interference: PRO = old projects forward; RETRO = new retrospects backward. Memory stores: "Iconic eyes, Echoic ears; 7 in the short term; forever in the long."

E · Indian Psychology (off your paper)

Full teaching in Appendix B. This is the cluster that has cost you across every mock, and Amity weights it heavier than NIMHANS does, so over-prepare it.

Trigunas. In the Sāṁkhya philosophical system the three gunas are the fundamental qualities or strands out of which all of nature (Prakriti) is woven, and every mind and object is a shifting mixture of them. Sattva is the quality of purity, clarity, harmony, knowledge, and balance (the calm, lucid mind). Rajas is the quality of activity, passion, restlessness, desire, and movement (the agitated, driven mind). Tamas is the quality of inertia, dullness, heaviness, ignorance, and darkness (the lethargic, clouded mind). The three are always present together in differing proportions, and their changing balance accounts for the variety of temperaments and states; the spiritual aim in this tradition is to raise sattva and reduce rajas and tamas. Formal psychometric guna scales exist and are studied in the Indian-approaches paper, which is why the exact qualities matter.
Trap: the examiners test which guna equals which quality. Tamas is the inert, dull, dark one, and it is the distractor they pair with "laziness, sleep, and ignorance." Do not let Tamas drift toward "activity" (that is Rajas) or "purity" (that is Sattva).

Pancha kosha. The five sheaths (koshas), from the Taittiriya Upanishad, are concentric layers wrapping the true Self, running from the grossest and outermost to the subtlest and innermost. Annamaya kosha is the food sheath, the physical body sustained by food (anna). Pranamaya kosha is the vital-energy or breath sheath (prana). Manomaya kosha is the mental or sensory-mind sheath (manas), the seat of ordinary thought and emotion. Vijnanamaya kosha is the intellect or wisdom sheath (vijnana, buddhi), the faculty of discrimination and judgement. Anandamaya kosha is the bliss sheath (ananda), the subtlest layer, closest to the Self. The ordering is the load-bearing fact: it runs body to bliss, gross to subtle (Annamaya, Pranamaya, Manomaya, Vijnanamaya, Anandamaya).
Trap: getting the order wrong, or swapping Manomaya (mind) with Vijnanamaya (intellect). Mind (manas, the sensory processor) sits below intellect (vijnana, the discriminating faculty) on the path inward.

Purusha vs Prakriti. This is the central dualism of Sāṁkhya, and the contrast is exactly what the paper probes. Purusha is pure consciousness, the eternal, passive, unchanging witness (sakshi); it is aware but does nothing and has no qualities. Prakriti is primordial matter or nature, the active, unconscious creative principle out of which the entire material and mental world (including the mind and intellect) evolves; crucially, Prakriti is what carries the three gunas. Suffering arises from the mistaken identification of the witnessing Purusha with the activities of Prakriti; liberation (kaivalya) is the discriminative knowledge that separates the two, so that consciousness recognises it was only ever the witness, never the doer.
Trap: assigning the gunas to Purusha. The gunas belong to Prakriti (matter/nature), never to the pure consciousness of Purusha. Purusha is qualityless and inactive; everything that has qualities or acts is Prakriti.

The four avasthas. The four states of consciousness, set out in the Mandukya Upanishad alongside the syllable Aum. Jagrat is the waking state, awareness turned outward to the gross external world. Swapna is the dream state, awareness turned inward to a subtle world of its own making. Sushupti is deep, dreamless sleep, a state of undifferentiated bliss with no objects and no duality, but still touched by ignorance. Turiya, literally "the fourth," is not a fourth experience alongside the others but the pure consciousness that underlies and transcends all three, the witness present in waking, dreaming, and deep sleep alike; it is the goal, the Self itself.
Trap: forgetting that Turiya is the fourth, transcendent state, not merely another, deeper stage of sleep. Sushupti is dreamless sleep; Turiya is the pure awareness beyond even that, and treating Turiya as "very deep sleep" is the classic error.

Antahkarana. The antahkarana is the "inner instrument" or inner organ, the subtle psychological apparatus through which consciousness operates, classically analysed into four functions. Manas is the sensory or processing mind, which receives and coordinates the input of the senses and generates doubt and deliberation. Buddhi is the intellect, the faculty of discrimination, decision, and judgement that resolves doubt into determination. Ahamkara is the ego or "I-maker," the function that appropriates experience to a sense of self ("I am doing this"). Chitta is the storehouse of memory and accumulated impressions (samskaras). Together they form the instrument of mind, but they are emphatically not the Self; they are the apparatus that the Self (Atman) illumines and operates through.
Trap: confusing Ahamkara (the ego, the I-maker) with Atman (the true Self). The antahkarana, ego included, is the instrument; the Atman is the consciousness behind it. The ego is a function of the apparatus, not the witness.

RCI 1986 vs 1993. The Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) matters to you personally, because it is the statutory authority that regulates and licenses clinical psychologists in India, and the date pair is the examined fact. The RCI was first set up as a registered society in 1986, an administrative body without statutory teeth.
