It was then given statutory status under the Rehabilitation Council of India Act, 1992 (which came into force in 1993), and a 2000 amendment widened its remit. As a statutory body it maintains the Central Rehabilitation Register, prescribes minimum standards for rehabilitation professionals (including clinical psychologists via the M.Phil and equivalent qualifications), and can take action against unqualified practitioners.
Trap: the 1986 (registered society) versus 1992/1993 (statutory body under the RCI Act) distinction. The society predates the Act by six years; the examiners offer 1986 and 1992 side by side to see whether you know which event each year names.

Exam trap

The Indian-psychology cluster is built almost entirely from one-to-one matching traps: Tamas equals inertia (not activity); the gunas belong to Prakriti (not Purusha); Turiya is transcendent consciousness (not deep sleep, which is Sushupti); Ahamkara is the ego-function (not the Atman); and RCI was a society in 1986 but a statutory body in 1992/1993. Learn each pairing in both directions, because the distractor is always the adjacent term in the same list.

If your full list comes through

This is the part of your Master Concept List that came across before it cut off (it stopped mid-section). When you send the rest, every new concept gets the same answer / why / trap widening and drops straight in here, so this part stays the truest map of what the paper actually asks.

Appendix B · Indian Psychology (off your own paper)

High-yield

Read this whole part twice. On every Amity mock so far this has been your single weakest cluster, and Amity weights indigenous/Indian psychology more heavily than any other entrance you are sitting. That combination, hardest for you and worth the most marks, means you cannot afford to merely recognise the terms. You must over-learn them: know what each concept is, which text or darshana it comes from, its internal structure, an example, and the exact trap the paper sets. Treat the tables in this part as things to reproduce from memory, not just to read.

7.1 Orientation: the six darshanas and where psychology lives

Before any single concept, fix the map. "Indian psychology" in this paper is not a modern empirical discipline with a laboratory; it is the body of doctrine about the self, the mind, consciousness, and personality that lies embedded inside classical Indian philosophy. To find that doctrine and to answer questions about its source (the examiners love "which darshana/which text does this come from?"), you must know the architecture of the six systems first. Everything later in this part hangs off this scaffold, so build it carefully.

Classical Indian thought is organised into six orthodox philosophical systems, the shad darshanas ("six visions"; darshana literally means "a way of seeing", from the root drish, to see). They are āstika ("orthodox", literally "it-exists-ist") schools because they accept the authority (pramana) of the Vedas, as opposed to the nāstika ("heterodox") systems such as Buddhism, Jainism, and the materialist Charvaka, which reject Vedic authority. The single dividing criterion between āstika and nāstika is not belief in God; it is acceptance of the Veda as a valid source of knowledge. This is itself a tested point, because candidates assume "orthodox" means "theistic", which is wrong (Samkhya, for instance, is orthodox yet classically non-theistic).

Definition

A darshana is a systematic philosophical viewpoint or "vision" of reality. The shad darshanas are the six orthodox (āstika) systems that accept the Vedas. The line between āstika and nāstika is drawn by acceptance versus rejection of Vedic authority, not by belief in a personal God.

The six are traditionally studied in three allied pairs, each pair sharing a method or subject:

PairThe two darshanasShared concern
Logic and atomismNyaya and VaisheshikaEpistemology, valid reasoning (Nyaya); the atomic constitution of matter and its categories (Vaisheshika)
Enumeration and disciplineSamkhya and YogaThe metaphysics of Purusha, Prakriti, and the gunas (Samkhya); the practical method of stilling the mind (Yoga)
Ritual and the end of the VedaMimamsa and VedantaInterpretation of Vedic ritual action (Purva Mimamsa); the philosophy of the Upanishads, the self, and Brahman (Uttara Mimamsa, i.e. Vedanta)

You do not need to master all six to the same depth. What you need is to know which schools carry the psychological content, the material about the self, mind, consciousness, and personality. That content sits in three of them, and you should be able to attribute any concept in this part to the right one at a glance.

  • Samkhya (traditionally attributed to the sage Kapila, its classical text being the Samkhya-karika of Ishvara Krishna) gives the metaphysical scaffolding: the two ultimate categories Purusha and Prakriti, and the three gunas that make up everything in nature, including the mind. The word samkhya means "enumeration", because the system counts out the twenty-five evolutes of reality.
  • Yoga (the system of Patanjali, whose text is the Yoga Sutras) turns Samkhya's map into an applied psychology: a practical, eight-limbed method for controlling and stilling the mind. Samkhya is the theory; Yoga is the technique. They share a metaphysics, which is why they are paired.
  • Vedanta (the philosophy distilled from the Upanishads, the closing portion of the Veda, hence "Vedanta" = "end of the Veda") gives the model of the self in layers (the koshas, from the Taittiriya Upanishad), the four states of consciousness (the avasthas, from the Mandukya Upanishad), the inner instrument (antahkarana), and the identity of Atman and Brahman.

A concrete way to use this map: when a question quotes a concept and asks for its origin, run the three-way sort. Is it about the gunas or the two ultimate principles? Samkhya. Is it about a practical discipline, the eight limbs, breath control, or "stilling the mind"? Yoga. Is it about sheaths, states of consciousness, the inner instrument, or Self-and-absolute identity? Vedanta/Upanishads. That single reflex answers a large share of the source-attribution items.

One more relationship is worth fixing now, because the paper tests it directly: Samkhya and Yoga are a matched pair that share one metaphysics. Samkhya supplies the worldview (Purusha, Prakriti, the gunas, the twenty-five tattvas) but is essentially atheistic and theoretical; Yoga adopts that very worldview and adds two things, a practical method (the eight limbs) and the acceptance of Ishvara (a special, untainted Purusha, a focus for devotion), which makes Yoga theistic. So a clean way to remember the difference is: same map, but Yoga adds the method and adds Ishvara. Vedanta, by contrast, stands apart from both: it is non-dualist (one consciousness) where Samkhya-Yoga are dualist (consciousness separate from matter), a divergence taken up fully in 7.7. Holding these relations now means you will rarely misattribute a concept later.

Exam trap

Three snares cluster here. (1) Āstika does not mean theistic. It means Veda-accepting; Samkhya is āstika yet classically atheistic. (2) Vedanta is the same darshana as Uttara Mimamsa; do not treat "the Upanishadic philosophy" and "Vedanta" as two different schools. (3) Buddhism, Jainism, and Charvaka are nāstika (heterodox), so any psychological doctrine the paper draws from the orthodox six is NOT Buddhist; a question may dangle "Buddhism" as the source of the koshas or gunas, which is wrong (they are Vedantic and Samkhya respectively).

Memory aid

The three pairs: "Nyaya-Vaisheshika, Samkhya-Yoga, Mimamsa-Vedanta." For the psychology, remember S-Y-V: Samkhya (the map), Yoga (the method), Vedanta (the Self). Theory, technique, transcendence.

7.2 The Trigunas (from Sāṁkhya): the three strands of nature

The trigunas are the most heavily tested single topic in this whole part, because they double as a metaphysics and as a personality model, and because the matching format ("which guna is which quality?") is so easy for the examiners to set. Learn them to the point of reflex.

Samkhya holds that all of manifest nature, Prakriti (primordial matter; see 7.3), is woven from three fundamental qualities or constituents called the gunas. The word guna literally means "strand" or "rope", and the image is exactly that: three strands twisted together into the single rope of experienced reality. They are not parts you can separate out; they are co-present constituents whose changing proportions give every object and every mind its character. The three are Sattva, Rajas, and Tamas. They are described in the Samkhya-karika and are given an extended psychological and ethical treatment in the Bhagavad Gita (especially chapters 14, 17, and 18), which classifies food, action, knowledge, charity, and even faith by their dominant guna; that Gita material is itself examinable.

The three qualities, in full

Sattva is the quality of purity, clarity, harmony, balance, and knowledge. When sattva dominates, the mind is calm, luminous, and discerning; it is associated with wisdom, contentment, equanimity, and lightness. Its characteristic colour in the tradition is white, and its experiential signature is illumination (it is the guna that reveals). Rajas is the quality of activity, passion, movement, and restlessness. When rajas dominates, there is drive, ambition, desire, agitation, and emotional turbulence; it is the principle of motion, craving, and effort, the engine that sets things going. Its colour is red, and its signature is activity (the guna that moves). Tamas is the quality of inertia, dullness, heaviness, and darkness. When tamas dominates, there is laziness, sleep, lethargy, confusion, ignorance, and resistance to change; it is the principle that obscures and weighs down. Its colour is black, and its signature is restraint and concealment (the guna that veils). The neat triad is therefore: sattva reveals, rajas activates, tamas restrains.

The doctrine the paper tests

The crucial doctrinal point, which the paper loves to test, is that everyone has all three gunas in varying proportion. The gunas are not three rigid types of people; they are three ingredients present in every mind and every object, and a person's temperament reflects which one currently predominates. They are also in constant flux and in mutual opposition: when one rises, the others are suppressed, so a single person is more sattvic in calm, clear moments, more rajasic when driven or angry, more tamasic when dull or sleepy. In Prakriti's unmanifest state the three are in perfect equilibrium; manifestation begins only when that equilibrium is disturbed. Because the gunas belong to Prakriti, they characterise the body and mind, never the pure witnessing consciousness (this is the load-bearing link to 7.3 below: gunas are of Prakriti, not Purusha).

A worked example

Take one student across a single day. At dawn, after rest, she reads with a quiet, focused, contented mind: sattva predominates. By midday a deadline grips her; she is driven, anxious, irritable, unable to sit still: rajas has risen and suppressed sattva. After a heavy lunch she is foggy, sleepy, and cannot be bothered to start: tamas now dominates. Same person, same day, three guna-states. That is precisely how the tradition (and the Gita) wants you to see temperament: not a fixed label but a shifting balance.

The modern-psychology bridge

Indian-approaches research has operationalised this into formal psychometrics. The Guna scales (for example, instruments developed in the Indian psychology literature to measure sattva, rajas, and tamas as personality dimensions) attempt to assess a person's guna profile much as Western inventories assess traits. You should recognise that the trigunas are not only a philosophical idea but a measured personality model in the formal study of Indian approaches to personality, the indigenous counterpart to a Western trait inventory. If a question asks "the Indian/indigenous model of personality based on three innate qualities", the answer is the triguna model.

GunaCore qualityPsychological expressionTypical distractor it is confused with
SattvaPurity, clarity, harmony, knowledge, balanceCalm, wise, content, luminous, discerningMislabelled as the "active" one (that is Rajas)
RajasActivity, passion, restlessness, movementDriven, ambitious, craving, agitated, emotionalMislabelled as the "pure" one (that is Sattva)
TamasInertia, dullness, darkness, heavinessLazy, sleepy, confused, ignorant, resistantMislabelled as "active" or "passionate" (it is the opposite)
Definition

The trigunas are the three constituent qualities of Prakriti (manifest nature): Sattva (clarity, that which reveals), Rajas (activity, that which moves), and Tamas (inertia, that which veils). They are co-present in everything in varying, ever-shifting proportions; temperament is the ratio currently dominant.

Exam trap

The matching item, "which guna corresponds to which quality", is the most common form. Tamas is the inert, dull, dark, sleepy one. The paper plants laziness/sleep/heaviness as distractors and tries to attach them to the wrong guna, or asks you to identify "the quality of inertia" and offers Rajas as a tempting decoy because Rajas also sounds negative. Lock it down: Sattva = clarity, Rajas = activity, Tamas = inertia. Sattva and Rajas are NOT interchangeable; Sattva is the calm/pure one, Rajas is the restless/active one. A further snare: "the gunas are types of people" is false; they are ingredients present in everyone in varying proportion.

Memory aid

Alphabetical S-R-T runs clarity → activity → inertia, i.e. best → middle → heaviest. "Sattva = Serene, Rajas = Restless, Tamas = Tired." Three Ts for Tamas: Tired, Torpid, dark (Tamas itself literally means darkness). Colours: white, red, black, top to bottom.

7.3 Purusha vs Prakriti: Sāṁkhya dualism

This is the metaphysical core of Samkhya and the hinge on which the guna doctrine and much of Yoga turn. The whole system is a dualism of two ultimate, irreducible categories, and almost every Samkhya trap is a swap of one principle's attributes onto the other. Teach yourself the two columns until the swap is instantly visible.

Purusha: the inactive witness

Purusha (literally "person" or "spirit") is pure consciousness, the witness (sakshi). It is inactive, attributeless (nirguna, "without gunas"), changeless, and merely observes. It does nothing; it simply illuminates, the way a lamp lights a room without itself acting on the furniture. Importantly, in classical Samkhya Purusha is plural: there are many purushas, one for each conscious being, which sharply distinguishes Samkhya from Vedanta's single universal consciousness (a contrast the paper exploits, see 7.7). Purusha is conscious but does nothing; it is the seer that never becomes the seen.

Prakriti: the active matrix

Prakriti (literally "nature", "the primary matter") is primordial matter or nature, the active, creative principle. Prakriti is unconscious but dynamic: a paradox worth stating clearly, because it is the exact inverse of Purusha (which is conscious but static). Prakriti carries the three gunas described above. In its unmanifest state (mula-prakriti, "root-nature") the three gunas are in perfect equilibrium and nothing is manifest; when that balance is disturbed (in the presence of Purusha), Prakriti begins to evolve and unfolds, in a fixed order, into the whole manifest world: first mahat/buddhi (the cosmic intellect), then ahamkara (ego), then manas (mind) together with the senses and the subtle and gross elements, twenty-three evolutes in all. Everything you can perceive, including your own mind, intellect, ego, and personality, is a product of Prakriti. Counting Purusha and Prakriti themselves with the twenty-three evolutes gives Samkhya's famous twenty-five tattvas (principles).

How bondage and liberation work

The manifest world arises from the interaction of these two. Purusha's mere presence, like a magnet near iron filings (or, in the classical simile, like a lame man of clear sight riding a blind man of strong legs), disturbs Prakriti's equilibrium and sets evolution in motion; consciousness gets "reflected" in the evolving intellect (buddhi), producing the illusion that the witness is the doer. Bondage is precisely this confusion: pure consciousness mistaking itself for the activities of Prakriti, identifying "I am acting, I am suffering" when in truth only Prakriti acts and only Purusha witnesses. Liberation (kaivalya, "aloneness/isolation") is achieved through discriminative knowledge (viveka), the clear discernment that separates Purusha from Prakriti, so that consciousness recognises it was never the doer, the gunas cease to bind, and Purusha rests, isolated, in its own nature. Note the word: liberation in Samkhya is kaivalya (isolation of the witness), not the union/merger implied by Vedantic moksha. That precise term is examinable.

PurushaPrakriti
What it isPure consciousness, the witnessPrimordial matter / nature
Active or inactive?Inactive, only observesActive, creative, evolves
Conscious?Conscious (pure awareness)Unconscious but dynamic
Carries the gunas?No (nirguna, attributeless)Yes, the three gunas belong here
NumberPlural (many)One
LiberationKaivalya: viveka separating it from PrakritiCeases to bind once discrimination dawns

A worked illustration of the confusion

Picture a clear mirror (Purusha) set before a moving film (Prakriti). The mirror does nothing; it only reflects. But the reflection of the moving film in the mirror makes it look as though the stillness itself is moving, as though the witness is the actor. That false transfer (movement and agency mistakenly attributed to the motionless reflector) is bondage. When discrimination (viveka) dawns and you see that the mirror was always still and only the film moved, the misidentification dissolves: that is kaivalya. The film keeps running; you simply stop calling it "I". This is the exact reasoning a question expects when it asks "what, in Samkhya, is the cause of bondage?" (answer: non-discrimination, the confusion of Purusha with Prakriti) and "what removes it?" (answer: viveka, discriminative knowledge).

The modern-psychology bridge

The Samkhya split maps loosely onto the distinction between pure awareness and the contents of mind. Where Western psychology generally treats consciousness as a product of the mental apparatus (a function of the brain/mind), Samkhya inverts the relation: the mental apparatus (buddhi, ahamkara, manas) is unconscious machinery, and consciousness (Purusha) is a separate, prior witness merely reflected in it. This "consciousness as witness, not as content" stance is what makes Indian psychology distinctive in a comparative question, and it is the conceptual seed of the meditative observer stance that Yoga then operationalises.

Exam trap

The headline trap: the three gunas belong to PRAKRITI, not Purusha. A question will say "the gunas are qualities of ___" and offer Purusha as the wrong answer. Purusha is pure, attributeless (nirguna) witnessing consciousness; it has no gunas, no activity, and no change. A second trap pairs the wrong adjective with the wrong principle: Purusha is the inactive conscious witness; Prakriti is the active unconscious matter. If you ever read "active consciousness" or "inactive matter", a swap has been performed. A third trap: Purusha is plural in Samkhya (many witnesses) and liberation is kaivalya (isolation), not merger; the single-consciousness, merger picture belongs to Advaita Vedanta, not Samkhya.

Memory aid

"Purusha is pure and passive; Prakriti is practical and productive." Two P-words each. The witness is conscious-but-still; matter is active-but-blind. Gunas live with the active one (Prakriti).

7.4 The Pancha Kosha: the five sheaths of the self

The doctrine of the five sheaths comes from the Taittiriya Upanishad (in its Brahmananda Valli section) and is a central Vedantic model of the self. The idea is that the true Self (Atman) is wrapped in five concentric coverings or koshas ("sheaths"; the word means a scabbard or husk), arranged from the grossest and most external to the subtlest and most internal, like the layers of an onion or the husks around a kernel. The Sanskrit suffix -maya means "made of" or "consisting of", so each name reads "the sheath consisting of ___". Spiritual realisation involves seeing through each sheath in turn (the method of neti neti, "not this, not this", negating identification with each layer) until the Atman, which is none of them, is realised beyond and through them.

The five sheaths, gross to subtle

The five, in order from gross to subtle, are: Annamaya kosha, the food sheath, the physical body built from and sustained by food (anna = food); it is the densest covering, born of food, growing by food, returning to food. Pranamaya kosha, the vital-energy sheath, the layer of breath and life-force (prana) that animates the body and drives respiration, circulation, and the physiological functions; it is what makes the food-body a living body. Manomaya kosha, the mental sheath, the layer of mind and emotions (manas = mind), of sensory processing, feeling, desire, doubt, and ordinary discursive thought; it is the seat of the wavering, perceiving, emoting mind. Vijnanamaya kosha, the wisdom/intellect sheath, the layer of vijnana: intellect, discrimination, judgement, the higher knowing and deciding faculty (it corresponds to buddhi); it is the layer that discerns and resolves rather than merely processes. Anandamaya kosha, the bliss sheath, the subtlest and innermost layer, associated with deep peace and bliss (ananda), the causal-body covering closest to the Self, experienced most directly in deep dreamless sleep.

The trajectory, and a Western anchor

Note the trajectory carefully: it runs body → bliss, gross → subtle, outer → inner. The physical body is outermost because it is the densest; bliss is innermost because it is the subtlest and nearest to Atman. A useful (loose) mapping for revision: the five sheaths roughly track physical body, physiology/vitality, mind-and-emotion, intellect/cognition, and a deep affective ground, a layered model of the person not unlike a tiered biopsychosocial picture, except that the Vedantic point is the opposite of a hierarchy of needs: it is a hierarchy of identifications to be transcended. The Atman itself is not a sixth sheath; it is the consciousness that the sheaths cover, realised when one no longer identifies with any of them. The koshas are also grouped into the three bodies (sharira): Annamaya is the gross body (sthula sharira); Pranamaya, Manomaya, and Vijnanamaya make up the subtle body (sukshma sharira); and Anandamaya is the causal body (karana sharira). That three-body grouping is a step-up fact the harder papers reach for.

#KoshaSheath ofMeaning / root
1 (grossest)AnnamayaFood / physical bodyanna = food; the body made of and fed by food
2PranamayaVital breath / energyprana = life-force; breath and vitality
3ManomayaMind / emotionsmanas = mind; sensory processing, feeling, thought
4VijnanamayaIntellect / wisdom / discriminationvijnana = higher knowing, judgement (buddhi)
5 (subtlest)AnandamayaBlissananda = bliss; nearest to Atman
Definition

The pancha kosha (five sheaths), from the Taittiriya Upanishad, are five concentric coverings of the Atman, from gross to subtle: Annamaya (food/body), Pranamaya (vital energy), Manomaya (mind/emotion), Vijnanamaya (intellect/discrimination), Anandamaya (bliss). The suffix -maya means "made of". The Atman is not a sheath; it is what the sheaths cover.

Exam trap

Two traps live here. First, ordering: the paper scrambles the five and asks for the gross-to-subtle sequence. Memorise Anna → Prana → Mano → Vijnana → Ananda (food, breath, mind, intellect, bliss). Second, the Manomaya/Vijnanamaya swap: Manomaya is the mind/emotion sheath (lower, processing, feeling); Vijnanamaya is the intellect/discrimination/wisdom sheath (higher, judging). The paper will define "the sheath of discriminative intellect" and offer Manomaya as the decoy, or define "the sheath of mind and emotion" and offer Vijnanamaya. Mind is below intellect, just as Manomaya (3rd) is below Vijnanamaya (4th). A third trap: the source is the Taittiriya Upanishad, not the Mandukya (the Mandukya gives the four states, 7.5); and Atman is not a sixth kosha.

Memory aid

"A Person May View Atman", Anna, Prana, Mano, Vijnana, Ananda. The order also climbs a person: body, breath, mind, intellect, bliss, from the physical you can touch up to the bliss nearest the Self.

7.5 The Four Avasthas: states of consciousness

The Mandukya Upanishad, the shortest of the principal Upanishads (just twelve verses, yet traditionally said to suffice for liberation on its own), sets out four states (avasthas) of consciousness and maps them onto the sacred syllable AUM (OM). This is one of the most elegant testable structures in Indian psychology, because it links a phenomenology of consciousness (waking, dreaming, sleeping, and the ground beyond) to the three phonemes of OM plus the silence that follows. The first three are familiar experiential states; the fourth is the transcendent ground in which the other three appear and disappear.

The four states, in full

Jagrat is the waking state, in which consciousness is turned outward to the gross physical world through the senses; the knower here is called vaishvanara or vishva, the experiencer of gross objects, and it corresponds to the Annamaya/gross body. Swapna is the dream state, in which consciousness is turned inward and experiences a subtle world of mental impressions and images, with no external sensory input; the knower is taijasa ("the luminous one"), the experiencer of subtle objects, corresponding to the subtle body. Sushupti is deep dreamless sleep, a state of undifferentiated rest in which there are no dreams and no duality of subject and object, experienced as peace but without articulated awareness or content; the knower is prajna ("the knower"), associated with the causal body and the bliss sheath (this is why Anandamaya is linked to deep sleep). And Turiya, literally "the fourth" (from turiya = fourth), is pure consciousness itself, the substratum that underlies and transcends the other three. Turiya is not a fourth experience alongside waking, dreaming, and sleeping; it is the silent, changeless awareness that is present in and beyond all of them, the witness that knows the waker, the dreamer, and the sleeper alike. It is described apophatically: not outward-knowing, not inward-knowing, not a mass of cognition, ungraspable, the cessation of phenomena, peace, the Self.

The AUM mapping and a worked illustration

In the AUM mapping, the three sounds A-U-M correspond to waking, dream, and deep sleep respectively: A (the open sound made at the back, the first and grossest) is Jagrat; U (the sound that rolls forward, intermediate and subtle) is Swapna; M (the closing hum with the lips shut, the resolution) is Sushupti. Turiya is the silence after the sound of OM: the soundless awareness in which the whole syllable arises, sounds, and subsides. A way to feel the logic: chant a single OM. The voice opens (A), rolls (U), closes to a hum (M), and then there is a pause of pure attentive silence before the next breath. That silence is not a fourth noise; it is the stillness that held all three. Turiya stands to the three states exactly as that silence stands to the three sounds.

The modern-psychology bridge

The first three avasthas line up neatly with constructs Western sleep science also recognises: Jagrat with the waking EEG, Swapna with REM/dreaming sleep, and Sushupti with deep slow-wave (NREM stage 3) dreamless sleep. The decisive difference is Turiya, which has no Western sleep-stage analogue at all, because it is not a physiological state of the brain but a claim about a witnessing consciousness that persists across states. That is precisely why it is the favourite trap: the examiner offers Turiya as if it were a deeper kind of sleep, when the doctrine insists it is wakeful awareness underlying sleep itself.

StateWhat it isConsciousness directedKnowerAUM correspondence
JagratWakingOutward, to the gross worldVaishvanara / VishvaA
SwapnaDreamInward, to subtle impressionsTaijasaU
SushuptiDeep dreamless sleepUndifferentiated rest, no objectsPrajnaM
Turiya"The fourth": pure consciousnessTranscends all three; the witnessThe Self / AtmanThe silence after OM
Definition

The four avasthas, from the Mandukya Upanishad, are states of consciousness mapped onto OM: Jagrat (waking, A), Swapna (dream, U), Sushupti (deep dreamless sleep, M), and Turiya ("the fourth", the silence after OM): pure witnessing consciousness underlying and transcending the other three.

Exam trap

Turiya is the fourth, transcendent state, not a stage of sleep. The paper exploits the fact that three of the four are ordinary daily states and tries to make you treat Turiya as just "very deep sleep" or another sleep phase. It is the opposite: it is pure wakeful awareness underlying everything, the witness of waking, dreaming, and sleeping alike. Also do not confuse Sushupti (deep dreamless sleep) with Swapna (the dream state). And keep the AUM mapping the right way round: A = waking, U = dream, M = deep sleep, silence = Turiya; reversing A and M (waking versus deep sleep) is a standard distractor. If a question says "the state associated with the silence after OM" or "pure consciousness beyond the other three", the answer is Turiya.

Memory aid

"Awake, dUream, sleeM", then silence. A-U-M reads top to bottom as waking → dream → deep sleep, gross → subtle → causal, exactly the kosha order of bodies; Turiya is the quiet that holds the whole syllable. Swapna has a "p" like sleeP-dream; Sushupti is the deepest "shhh".

7.6 Antahkarana: the inner instrument of mind

Antahkarana (from antah = inner, and karana = instrument or organ) means the "inner instrument", the internal apparatus of mind in Vedantic and Samkhya-derived psychology. It is the mental machinery through which consciousness operates: the tool that perceives, thinks, decides, claims, and remembers. The single most important conceptual point, the one the whole doctrine is built around, is that the antahkarana is an instrument, a product and evolute of Prakriti (primordial nature), and therefore not the conscious Self that wields it. Pure consciousness (Purusha, or in Vedanta the Atman) is the user; the antahkarana is the used. When the Upanishads speak of mind as a chariot's reins and the senses as horses, the reins are antahkarana, and the rider beyond them is the Self. This builds directly on the Purusha-versus-Prakriti split: everything you can observe about your own inner life, the very fact that you can watch a thought arise, decide, or recall a memory, is the activity of an instrument, watched by a witness that is not itself an instrument.

The antahkarana is classically analysed into four functions or aspects. They are not four separate organs sitting in different places; they are four modes or operations of one inner instrument, distinguished by what each does to a piece of experience as it passes through. The four are Manas, Buddhi, Ahamkara, and Chitta.

Manas (from manas = mind) is the sensory and processing mind. It is the faculty that receives raw sense impressions from the five sense organs, coordinates and synthesises them into a coherent percept, and runs the back-and-forth of ordinary thinking: doubt, deliberation, desire, wishing, and wavering. Manas is characteristically indecisive; it presents options and oscillates ("shall I, shall I not"), but it does not settle them. Buddhi (from budh = to know, to awaken) is the intellect, the higher faculty of discrimination (viveka), judgement, determination, and decision. Where Manas wavers, Buddhi concludes: it is the function that resolves doubt, discriminates true from false and right from wrong, and issues the final determination. It is the closest of the four to pure consciousness and the one most capable of reflecting the light of the Self clearly. Ahamkara (from aham = I, and kara = maker) is the ego or "I-maker", the function that appropriates experience to a self. It takes the impersonal stream of perception and decision and stamps it with "I" and "mine": "I am seeing this", "this is my thought", "I am the doer". It is Ahamkara that generates the felt sense of being a separate individual agent and that mistakenly identifies the witnessing Self with the body and mind. Chitta (from cit = to be conscious, here in the sense of the mind-field) is the memory store or "mind-stuff", the reservoir in which impressions (samskaras) left by past experience are deposited and from which they later resurface as memory, habit, and disposition. Chitta is the substrate on which the other three operate; in Patanjali's Yoga the entire mind-field that yoga sets out to still is called chitta, which is why you will meet the word again in the next subsection but one.

Definition

The antahkarana ("inner instrument") is the fourfold inner apparatus of mind: Manas (sensory/processing mind, doubt and desire), Buddhi (intellect, discrimination and decision), Ahamkara (ego, the "I-maker"), and Chitta (memory store of impressions). It is an evolute of Prakriti, an instrument used by consciousness, never consciousness itself.

FunctionRoleSanskrit rootOne-word handle
ManasSensory/processing mind; receives impressions, coordinates senses, doubts, desires, waversmanas = mindMind
BuddhiIntellect; discrimination (viveka), judgement, determination, decision; resolves the doubt Manas raisedbudh = to knowDecide
AhamkaraEgo, the "I-maker"; appropriates experience, generates the sense of I and mine, identifies Self with body-mindaham = IEgo
ChittaMemory store, mind-stuff; reservoir of impressions (samskaras) from which memory and habit resurfacecit = consciousness/mind-fieldMemory

A concrete worked example makes the division clear. Suppose you are walking at dusk and see a coiled shape on the path. Manas takes in the visual impression and presents the raw, unresolved data, registering "something coiled, long, on the path", and immediately wavers ("snake? rope?"). Buddhi then discriminates and decides: after a second look it concludes "that is a rope, not a snake". Ahamkara personalises the whole episode: "I was frightened", "I saw it", "my heart is pounding". And Chitta stores the impression, so that next time you pass that spot at dusk a faint memory-trace of fear stirs before you even look. One event, four operations, one instrument.

High-yield

Manas doubts/processes, Buddhi decides/discriminates, Ahamkara claims (ego, "I"), Chitta remembers (stores samskaras). The Manas-versus-Buddhi split (wavering mind below, deciding intellect above) is the same mind-versus-intellect distinction you met in the koshas (Manomaya below Vijnanamaya). Ahamkara is the ego-function, NOT the true Self.

Modern-psychology mapping. The antahkarana is, in effect, a faculty model of the mind that predates and parallels Western componential accounts. Manas, as the receiver and integrator of sense data that then hesitates, maps loosely onto sensory registration and the deliberative, conflict-laden side of decision-making. Buddhi, as the executive that discriminates and commits to a choice, maps onto executive function and judgement (the prefrontal "decider"). Ahamkara, the I-maker, anticipates modern constructs of the self-concept, ego, and the narrative sense of agency (compare the psychoanalytic ego that mediates and identifies, and contemporary work on the "self as object"). Chitta, the reservoir of impressions that shapes later behaviour without being consciously retrieved, maps onto long-term and implicit memory and onto the way stored dispositions (samskaras) function like learned habits or schemas. You can present the four-function model in the paper as an indigenous faculty psychology that distinguishes perception, executive judgement, self-reference, and memory, the same broad divisions a modern cognitive account would draw, but framed around an instrument used by a witnessing consciousness.

How it is tested. The commonest item is a one-to-one match: "which component of the antahkarana is the intellect/decision faculty?" (Buddhi), "which is the ego/I-maker?" (Ahamkara), "which is the memory store?" (Chitta), "which receives sense impressions and doubts?" (Manas). A second form asks you to list the four components of the antahkarana, where the trap is to slip in Atman or Purusha as a supposed fifth. A third form gives a behavioural vignette (like the rope-snake above) and asks which faculty performs the discrimination.

Exam trap

Do not confuse Ahamkara (ego) with Atman (the true Self). Ahamkara is the false "I", the ego-function that mistakes itself for the Self; Atman is the real Self that the ego obscures. The whole point of the antahkarana doctrine is that this fourfold inner instrument is the apparatus of mind, a product of Prakriti, and the Self merely witnesses it. A question may ask "which is the I-maker / ego function?" (answer: Ahamkara) and dangle Atman as a trap, or ask "which is NOT a function of the antahkarana?" with Atman (or Purusha) as the correct exclusion. Also keep Manas (the wavering, processing mind) distinct from Buddhi (the deciding intellect): the paper defines "the faculty of discrimination and decision" and offers Manas as the decoy, exactly the same mind-versus-intellect swap it plays with Manomaya and Vijnanamaya in the koshas.

Memory aid

"Mind Buys A Car": Manas, Buddhi, Ahamkara, Chitta. Manas senses and wavers, Buddhi decides, Ahamkara claims it as "mine", Chitta remembers it. Read in that order it is also a tiny story: you perceive, you judge, you own, you store.

7.7 Atman and Brahman: the Vedantic identity

Vedanta (literally "the end/culmination of the Vedas", the body of teaching distilled from the Upanishads) turns on two terms and the claim that they are one. Atman (from an/at, breath/self) is the individual true Self, the innermost, unchanging consciousness, what you really are beneath the five koshas and behind the fourfold antahkarana. It is not the body, not the breath, not the mind, not the intellect, and not the ego; it is the pure awareness that illuminates all of these and is the constant witness of them. Brahman (from brh = to grow great, to expand) is the ultimate, infinite, attributeless reality, the absolute ground and source of everything that exists. Brahman is classically described as Sat-Chit-Ananda: sat (pure being/existence), chit (pure consciousness), and ananda (pure bliss). The decisive teaching of the Upanishads is that Atman and Brahman are, at the deepest level, identical: the consciousness at the core of the individual is numerically the same as the consciousness that is the ground of the cosmos. The wave is not a separate thing from the ocean; it is the ocean in a particular form.

This identity is crystallised in the mahavakyas, the "great sayings", short Upanishadic declarations each drawn from one of the four Vedas, which the tradition treats as the most condensed statements of the truth. The four standardly cited are:

MahavakyaTranslationSource (Veda / Upanishad)What it asserts
Tat Tvam Asi"That thou art" / "You are That"Sama Veda (Chandogya Upanishad)The individual Self is the universal reality
Aham Brahmasmi"I am Brahman"Yajur Veda (Brihadaranyaka Upanishad)The Self, from the first person, is the absolute
Prajnanam Brahma"Consciousness is Brahman"Rig Veda (Aitareya Upanishad)Pure consciousness is the ultimate reality
Ayam Atma Brahma"This Self is Brahman"Atharva Veda (Mandukya Upanishad)The very Self here is Brahman

Of these, "Tat Tvam Asi" ("That thou art") is by far the most tested. It comes from the dialogue in the Chandogya Upanishad in which the sage Uddalaka repeatedly tells his son Shvetaketu, "tat tvam asi", that subtle essence which is the Self of all this, that thou art. The phrase compresses the entire non-dualist position into three words: the "That" (Brahman, the universal) and the "thou" (Atman, the individual) are joined by "art" (are identical).

Definition

Atman = the individual true Self, the innermost witnessing consciousness. Brahman = the ultimate, infinite, attributeless reality (Sat-Chit-Ananda: being, consciousness, bliss). Vedanta's core claim is their identity, expressed in the mahavakya "Tat Tvam Asi" ("That thou art").

The school that takes this identity most strictly is Advaita Vedanta ("non-dualism", literally "not-two"), systematised by Shankara (Adi Shankaracharya, c. 8th century CE). Advaita holds that there is only one reality without a second: Atman is Brahman, fully and without remainder, and the apparent multiplicity of the world, the many separate selves and objects, is maya, an appearance produced by avidya (ignorance) superimposed on the one reality. The stock analogy is the rope mistaken for a snake (the same image used for Buddhi above): the snake was never really there, only the rope; likewise the manifold world is a misperception of the one Brahman. Bondage is this ignorance; liberation (moksha) is not the production of anything new but the direct realisation (not merely the belief) that the Self always already was Brahman. This is sometimes glossed as the difference between knowing the fact intellectually and awakening to it.

Modern-psychology mapping. Atman as the constant witnessing awareness behind all changing mental contents anticipates the distinction between the "I" (the self-as-knower, the pure subject) and the "Me" (the self-as-known, the empirical self-concept) drawn by William James, with Atman corresponding to the witnessing "I" and the koshas/antahkarana corresponding to the constructed "Me". The Advaitic claim that suffering is rooted in mistaken self-identification (taking oneself to be the body-mind) and that relief comes through dis-identifying the witness from its contents resonates strongly with the stance-shift cultivated in mindfulness-based and acceptance-based therapies (observing thoughts as passing events rather than as the self). Transpersonal psychology has drawn on Atman-Brahman identity directly as a model of self-transcendence.

High-yield

Atman = individual Self; Brahman = universal absolute; they are identical. The headline mahavakya is "Tat Tvam Asi" ("That thou art") from the Chandogya Upanishad. Advaita Vedanta (non-dualism) was systematised by Shankara; the world's multiplicity is maya; liberation is realising Atman = Brahman.

Exam trap

Note the contrast with Samkhya, which the paper deliberately blurs. Samkhya is dualist (many separate purushas, eternally distinct from prakriti; liberation is kaivalya, isolation). Advaita Vedanta is strictly non-dualist (one consciousness only, Atman = Brahman; liberation is moksha, realising non-difference). A question may attribute "Atman = Brahman / non-dualism" to Samkhya, or attribute "plural selves / dualism" to Advaita; both are reversed. Second trap: do not confuse Atman with Ahamkara, the ego-function of the antahkarana. Ahamkara is the false "I" that obscures Atman; Atman is the real Self. Third: "Tat Tvam Asi" is the famous mahavakya, but the paper may offer "Aham Brahmasmi" or "Prajnanam Brahma" and ask for the source Upanishad, so learn at least that Tat Tvam Asi comes from the Chandogya.

7.8 Yoga and Patanjali: the applied psychology of mind-control

The Yoga Sutras of Patanjali (a text of roughly 196 terse aphorisms, the sutras, compiled around the 2nd century BCE to 4th century CE) are the foundational text of the Yoga darshana. Yoga is the practical twin of Samkhya: it accepts Samkhya's whole metaphysics (Purusha as pure consciousness, Prakriti as nature, the three gunas, the goal of separating consciousness from its entanglement in nature) but adds two things Samkhya lacks: the acceptance of Ishvara (a special Purusha, a focus for devotion) and, above all, a systematic method. This is why Yoga functions as an applied Indian psychology: it is not merely a theory of mind but a step-by-step technology for transforming and stilling it.

Patanjali's definition of yoga, given in the second sutra, is the single most quoted line in the whole of Indian psychology: "yogaḥ citta-vṛtti-nirodhaḥ". Parsed term by term: yogah = yoga (union, or the disciplined stilling that leads to it); chitta = the mind-stuff, the whole mental field (the same chitta you met as the memory-reservoir of the antahkarana, here standing for the entire mind); vritti = a modification, fluctuation, or wave of that mind-field (a thought, perception, memory, or imagining); nirodha = restraint, cessation, stilling. So yoga is the stilling (nirodha) of the modifications (vrittis) of the mind-stuff (chitta). The mind is normally a restless stream of these vrittis; yoga is the systematic quieting of them. The very next sutra states the payoff: tada drashtuh svarupe avasthanam, "then the seer (Purusha) abides in its own true nature". When the waves of the mind subside, the witnessing consciousness, no longer identified with the mental turbulence, rests as itself. (Patanjali classifies the vrittis into five kinds: pramana right knowledge, viparyaya error, vikalpa imagination/verbal construction, nidra sleep, and smriti memory.)

Definition

Patanjali defines yoga as citta-vritti-nirodha: the stilling (nirodha) of the modifications/fluctuations (vrittis) of the mind-stuff (chitta). When the mind's waves are stilled, the seer (Purusha) rests in its own nature. This is the most-tested single line in Indian psychology.

Before the limbs, Patanjali names the obstacles the method must overcome: the five kleshas (afflictions), the root causes of suffering and bondage that colour and disturb the mind. They are: Avidya (ignorance, specifically mistaking the impermanent for the permanent and the non-Self for the Self; this is the root klesha from which the other four grow), Asmita (egoism, the false identification of consciousness with the instrument of mind, essentially the ahamkara error), Raga (attachment, craving for pleasure), Dvesha (aversion, the recoil from pain), and Abhinivesha (clinging to life, the deep-seated fear of death that grips even the wise). Yoga's practices are designed to thin and finally uproot these kleshas, with avidya as the master root: remove ignorance and the rest lose their soil.

KleshaTranslationWhat it is
AvidyaIgnoranceMistaking the impermanent for permanent, non-Self for Self; the root affliction
AsmitaEgoismIdentifying pure consciousness with the mind/instrument (the ahamkara error)
RagaAttachmentCraving and clinging to pleasure
DveshaAversionRecoiling from and resisting pain
AbhiniveshaClinging to lifeThe instinctive fear of death; will-to-live

The method itself is the eight limbs (ashtanga, from ashta = eight and anga = limb), also called raja yoga ("royal yoga") or ashtanga yoga. The eight progress from outer ethical and physical discipline toward inner mental absorption, each limb preparing the ground for the next:

#LimbMeaningWhat it involves
1YamaEthical restraints (toward others)Non-violence (ahimsa), truthfulness (satya), non-stealing (asteya), continence (brahmacharya), non-possessiveness (aparigraha)
2NiyamaPersonal observances (toward oneself)Purity (saucha), contentment (santosha), austerity (tapas), self-study (svadhyaya), surrender to Ishvara (ishvara-pranidhana)
3AsanaPostureA steady, comfortable seated posture for prolonged stillness
4PranayamaBreath regulationControl and extension of the breath (prana = vital force)
5PratyaharaSense-withdrawalWithdrawing the senses inward, detaching attention from external objects
6DharanaConcentrationFixing attention on a single point or object
7DhyanaMeditationSustained, unbroken flow of attention on that object
8SamadhiAbsorptionComplete absorption; meditator and object merge, the mind is fully stilled

A structural point the paper rewards: the first five limbs (Yama to Pratyahara) are the "outer" or external limbs (bahiranga), preparing the body, conduct, and senses; the last three (Dharana, Dhyana, Samadhi) are the "inner" limbs (antaranga), and together this concentrative trio is given a special name, samyama. The three differ only in degree of continuity: Dharana is attention repeatedly returned to one point (it keeps slipping and being brought back); Dhyana is attention flowing unbroken to that point (a steady stream); Samadhi is attention so total that the sense of a separate meditator dissolves and only the object shines. Worked example: a student told to concentrate on a candle flame who keeps catching the mind wandering and dragging it back is in Dharana; when the attention finally settles into a continuous, effortless rest on the flame, that is Dhyana; if self-awareness drops away and "only flame" remains, with no felt watcher, that is Samadhi.

High-yield

Yoga = citta-vritti-nirodha (stilling the mind's modifications). The eight limbs in order: Yama, Niyama, Asana, Pranayama, Pratyahara, Dharana, Dhyana, Samadhi. First five = outer/external limbs; last three (Dharana-Dhyana-Samadhi) = inner limbs, collectively samyama. The five kleshas: Avidya (root), Asmita, Raga, Dvesha, Abhinivesha.

Modern-psychology mapping. Patanjali's system is, in contemporary terms, an attention-training and emotion-regulation technology, and it is the explicit ancestor of clinical mindfulness and meditation research. Pratyahara (disengaging the senses from external pulls) and Dharana-Dhyana (focused, then sustained, attention) correspond closely to the focused-attention and open-monitoring practices studied in mindfulness, and the progression Dharana to Dhyana to Samadhi maps onto deepening concentrative absorption. The kleshas read as an indigenous cognitive model of suffering: avidya (a core cognitive distortion about self and permanence) generating attachment and aversion (the approach-avoidance drivers of much clinical distress), with abhinivesha as existential/death anxiety. The yamas and niyamas function as a values-and-conduct framework comparable to the values work in acceptance and commitment therapy. You can legitimately present ashtanga yoga in the exam as a structured, staged programme of behavioural discipline, attentional training, and cognitive insight aimed at regulating the mind.

How it is tested. The most frequent items are: complete or order the eight limbs (often with one or two blanked or scrambled); identify a named limb from its description ("withdrawal of the senses" = Pratyahara; "steady posture" = Asana; "breath control" = Pranayama); recall the definition of yoga (citta-vritti-nirodha) and who gave it (Patanjali); and list the five kleshas or name the root one (Avidya). A subtler item asks for the difference between Dharana, Dhyana, and Samadhi, or for the collective name of the inner three (samyama).

Exam trap

Order and identity traps dominate. The classic snare is swapping Dharana (concentration, attention fixed on a point) with Dhyana (meditation, the unbroken flow), or placing Pratyahara (sense-withdrawal, limb 5) after the concentrative limbs instead of before them. Lock the sequence: senses are withdrawn (Pratyahara) before concentration begins (Dharana). A second trap attributes the definition citta-vritti-nirodha to the wrong school or sage; it is Patanjali's Yoga Sutra, not Vedanta or Samkhya proper. A third confuses the kleshas (five afflictions: avidya, asmita, raga, dvesha, abhinivesha) with the yamas/niyamas (the ethical restraints and observances of the first two limbs); the kleshas are the afflictions to be removed, the yama-niyama are practices to be cultivated.

Memory aid

Eight limbs in order: "Young Ninjas Always Practise Precise Deep Dedicated Stunts", Yama, Niyama, Asana, Pranayama, Pratyahara, Dharana, Dhyana, Samadhi. The last three (Dharana to Dhyana to Samadhi) are the inner concentrative trio (samyama). Five kleshas: think "An Angry Raging Dog Attacks", Avidya, Asmita, Raga, Dvesha, Abhinivesha, with Avidya (ignorance) as the root of the rest. And the one-line definition you must never lose: yoga = chitta vritti nirodha, stilling the modifications of the mind.

7.9 The Purusharthas: the indigenous model of motivation and well-being

Indian thought frames human motivation and a well-lived life through the four purusharthas (from purusha = a person/human, and artha = aim/object/purpose), the four legitimate aims or goals of human life. They are set out across the Dharmashastra literature (the treatises on righteous living, such as the Manusmriti) and woven through the epics and the Bhagavad Gita; the Gita in particular develops the idea that one may pursue worldly aims while remaining inwardly oriented toward liberation. The four are not a ranking in which only the last matters; they are a balanced scheme in which each aim is legitimate in its place, governed and harmonised by the first.

The four aims are Dharma, Artha, Kama, and Moksha. Dharma (from dhr = to hold, support, uphold) is duty, righteousness, moral and social order, ethical living in accordance with what is right for one's role and stage of life. It is the foundational aim because it is the framework within which the other worldly aims are to be pursued: wealth and pleasure are legitimate only when sought dharmically, without harm or transgression. Artha (here, wealth/means) is material prosperity, economic security, success, and the resources, possessions, status, and means necessary to live and to discharge one's duties; it is the recognition that a human being needs a material base. Kama (desire/pleasure) is the enjoyment of the senses, emotions, aesthetic and sexual pleasure, love, and the legitimate satisfaction of desire; far from being denied, it is honoured as a proper aim of life when pursued within dharma. Moksha (from much = to release, to free) is liberation, the release from samsara (the cycle of birth, death, and rebirth) and from all bondage; it is the ultimate, transcendent spiritual goal that orients and crowns the whole scheme.

PurusharthaTranslationDomainRole in the scheme
DharmaDuty / righteousnessMoral and social order; ethical livingThe foundation and frame; governs the pursuit of the other two worldly aims
ArthaWealth / meansProsperity, security, possessions, statusThe material base of life, sought within dharma
KamaPleasure / desireSensory, emotional, aesthetic enjoyment, loveLegitimate enjoyment, sought within dharma
MokshaLiberationRelease from the cycle of birth and deathThe ultimate, transcendent aim; crowns and orients the rest

Together the four form an indigenous model of motivation and well-being. A balanced life pursues prosperity (artha) and pleasure (kama) within the bounds of righteousness (dharma), while orienting ultimately toward liberation (moksha). The traditional ashrama scheme of life-stages mirrors this trajectory: the student stage emphasises dharma (learning duty), the householder stage legitimately pursues artha and kama (raising a family, earning, enjoying), and the later forest-dweller and renunciant stages turn progressively toward moksha. A concrete example: a householder running a business is fully within the scheme when he earns honestly (artha within dharma), enjoys family and aesthetic life (kama within dharma), discharges his social and ethical duties (dharma), and keeps the ultimate horizon of liberation (moksha) in view, rather than treating wealth or pleasure as ends in themselves.

Definition

The four purusharthas are the legitimate aims of human life: Dharma (duty/righteousness, the governing frame), Artha (wealth/material means), Kama (pleasure/desire), and Moksha (liberation, the ultimate aim). Worldly aims (artha, kama) are pursued within dharma and oriented toward moksha.

Modern-psychology mapping. The purusharthas are routinely compared with Western models of motivation and well-being, and the comparison is itself a likely exam discussion point. Like Maslow's hierarchy of needs, the scheme runs from material and security needs (artha) and the satisfaction of desire (kama) toward a higher, self-transcending aim, but the contrast is sharp and worth stating: the apex of the purusharthas is explicitly spiritual liberation (moksha), not the secular self-actualisation that crowns Maslow's pyramid; and the whole structure is held together by an ethical-duty dimension (dharma) that has no clean Maslovian equivalent. The model also maps onto the modern distinction between hedonic well-being (kama, pleasure and enjoyment) and eudaimonic well-being (dharma and moksha, meaning, virtue, and self-transcendence), with artha supplying the material preconditions. Presented this way, the purusharthas are an integrative, indigenous account of human motivation that combines material, hedonic, ethical, and spiritual aims in one balanced framework.

How it is tested. Common items: name the four purusharthas, or supply the missing one; match each term to its meaning (dharma = duty/righteousness; artha = wealth; kama = pleasure; moksha = liberation); identify the ultimate/highest aim (Moksha); identify the aim that governs or frames the others (Dharma); and compare the scheme with Maslow (the apex is liberation, not self-actualisation).

Exam trap

Kama means pleasure/desire in general, not merely sexual desire, and Artha means wealth/material means, not "art". The paper exploits the false friend "Artha"/"art" and the narrowed reading of "Kama". A second trap reverses the hierarchy: the ultimate aim is Moksha (liberation), and the foundational governing aim is Dharma (righteousness); a question asking for "the highest goal of life" wants Moksha, while one asking which aim "regulates the pursuit of the others" wants Dharma, and the paper swaps these. A third trap matches the wrong term to "duty": Dharma is duty/righteousness; do not pair duty with Artha or Kama.

Memory aid

Four aims, in their standard order: Dharma (duty), Artha (wealth), Kama (pleasure), Moksha (liberation), "DAKM" or "Duty And Kindling, then Moksha". Dharma frames the lot (it comes first and governs); Moksha crowns it (it comes last and transcends). The two in the middle (Artha, Kama) are the worldly pair held inside dharma.

7.10 RCI history: the tested date pair and the professional frame

The Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) is the statutory body that regulates and monitors the training of rehabilitation professionals and personnel (clinical psychologists among them), prescribes minimum standards of education, and maintains the Central Rehabilitation Register of qualified professionals. For Indian clinical psychology this is not a peripheral administrative fact but the central professional frame of the discipline you are entering: RCI is the body that licenses and registers clinical psychologists in India, and lawfully practising or designating oneself as a clinical psychologist requires RCI registration. The M.Phil in Clinical Psychology (and, increasingly, RCI-approved M.A./M.Sc. programmes) is RCI-recognised training precisely because RCI prescribes and accredits the curriculum. The Council also has the authority to act against unqualified or fraudulent practitioners. This is why a "history of the profession" question on the entrance paper is fair game, and why the examiners fix on a specific, easily-confused date pair.

The chronology runs as follows. The RCI began as a registered society in 1986. At that stage it was a voluntary, non-statutory body: it could promote and coordinate training, but it had no legal teeth, no power to compel standards or to register practitioners with legal effect. Recognising that a voluntary society could not effectively regulate a profession, Parliament gave the body statutory backing through the Rehabilitation Council of India Act, 1992. That Act was enforced (brought into force) in 1993. The Act transformed RCI from a voluntary society into a statutory regulator with the legal authority to prescribe and recognise training programmes, lay down minimum standards of education, maintain the Central Rehabilitation Register, register qualified professionals, and prosecute unqualified practitioners. The Act was subsequently amended in 2000 to widen its scope (broadening the categories of disability and professionals covered).

YearEventStatus of RCI
1986RCI established as a registered societyVoluntary, non-statutory (no legal regulatory power)
1992RCI Act passed by ParliamentGranted statutory status
1993RCI Act enforced / brought into forceStatutory regulator operational
2000RCI Act amendedScope widened

Why it matters in the paper, and the worked distinction. The single fact the examiners want you to hold cleanly is the difference between RCI as a registered society and RCI as a statutory body. A registered society is formed by people voluntarily associating and registering under the Societies Registration Act; it is private and has no power to bind anyone outside its membership. A statutory body, by contrast, is created or empowered by an Act of Parliament and exercises legal authority over an entire profession. RCI was the former in 1986 and became the latter through the 1992 Act (enforced 1993). Worked example of the trap in action: the question "In which year did RCI become a statutory body?" has the answer 1992 (Act passed, status granted), or 1993 if the question specifies "brought into force", and emphatically not 1986; whereas "In which year was RCI first established/founded?" has the answer 1986 (the society), not 1992. Read which event the stem is asking about.

Definition

The Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) is the statutory regulator of rehabilitation professionals in India. It prescribes and recognises training, maintains the Central Rehabilitation Register, and registers clinical psychologists; RCI registration is required to practise lawfully as a clinical psychologist.

Exam trap

The trap is the 1986 vs 1992/1993 distinction. In 1986 RCI was only a registered society (non-statutory); it became a statutory body with the RCI Act of 1992 (enforced 1993). The paper offers "1986" as the answer to "when did RCI become a statutory body?" (wrong: that is the year it was merely registered as a society) and offers "1992" for "when was RCI first established?" (wrong: that is the year of the statutory Act). Read whether the stem asks about the society (1986) or the statutory Act (1992, enforced 1993). Do not invent "1986 Act"; the society was not an Act, and there was no RCI Act until 1992.

High-yield

To recap the trap map for this whole part, the reliable Amity snares: (1) Tamas is inertia/dullness, not activity. (2) The gunas belong to Prakriti, never to Purusha. (3) The koshas run Anna to Prana to Mano to Vijnana to Ananda, and Manomaya (mind) is below Vijnanamaya (intellect). (4) Turiya is the transcendent fourth state, not a sleep stage. (5) In the antahkarana, Ahamkara (ego) is not Atman (Self); Manas (processing) is not Buddhi (deciding). (6) Atman = Brahman is Advaita non-dualism (Shankara, "Tat Tvam Asi"); Samkhya by contrast is dualist. (7) Yoga = citta-vritti-nirodha (Patanjali); the eight limbs run Yama to Samadhi, with Pratyahara (sense-withdrawal) before Dharana (concentration). (8) The purusharthas are Dharma-Artha-Kama-Moksha, with Moksha the highest aim and Dharma the governing frame. (9) RCI = society 1986, statutory Act 1992 (enforced 1993). If you can reproduce every table in this part from memory and recite those lines, you have neutralised your weakest, most heavily weighted cluster.
